BRIDGELAND STABILITY CONDITIONS ON THE CATEGORY OF HOLOMORPHIC TRIPLES OVER CURVES
EVA MARTÍNEZ-ROMERO, ALEJANDRA RINCÓN-HIDALGO, ARNE RÜFFER

Abstract
. We give a complete description of the Bridgeland stability manifold for the bounded derived category of holomorphic triples over a smooth projective curve of genus 1 as a connected, four dimensional complex manifold.



1.
Introduction

The concept of a holomorphic triple was introduced by Bradlow and García-Prada in
[
8
]
and
[
9
]
. The abelian category of holomorphic triples
\TCoh(C)
consists of objects of the form
\varphi\colon E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}
, where
E_{1},E_{2}
are coherent sheaves on a nonsingular projective curve
C
together with a morphism
\varphi
between them. It was shown in
[
8
]
that moduli spaces of semistable holomorphic triples of vector bundles exist and are projective. This category has also played an important role in the study of Higgs bundles
[
10
]
. Assuming
C
to have genus 1, we will provide a complete description of the Bridgeland stability manifold of the triangulated category
\mathcal{T}_{C}\coloneqq D^{b}(\TCoh(C))
.

Stability conditions on triangulated categories have been introduced by Bridgeland in
[
11
]
as a formalisation of Douglas’ work in
[
19
]
and
[
20
]
. The main result in
[
11
]
asserts that the set of stability conditions has the structure of a complex manifold (usually referred to as stability space). Stability manifolds have several applications in algebraic geometry as for example they serve as an important aid for the understanding of derived categories or as a tool in birational geometry (
[
25
]
,
[
4
]
). The description of stability spaces is not an easy endeavour in geometric situations. For nonsingular projective curves it is well understood (
[
11
]
,
[
15
]
,
[
33
]
,
[
36
]
) and our strategy is to use this as a building stone in the case of holomorphic triples.

Our first finding is that
D^{b}(\Coh(C))
can be embedded as a strictly full subcategory into
\mathcal{T}_{C}
in three different ways. These are

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\colon

\displaystyle X\in D^{b}(\Coh(C))\}

\displaystyle\textnormal{ }\subset\mathcal{T}_{C},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\colon

\displaystyle X\in D^{b}(\Coh(C))\}

\displaystyle\textnormal{ }\subset\mathcal{T}_{C},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\id}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\colon

\displaystyle X\in D^{b}(\Coh(C))\}

\displaystyle\textnormal{ }\subset\mathcal{T}_{C}

and subsequent pairing of two of the resulting subcategories, respectively, leads to three semiorthogonal decompositions

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle\textnormal{, }\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}\rangle\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{3},\mathcal{C}_{1}\rangle.

Following
[
7
]
, we can prove the existence of the Serre functor on
\mathcal{T}_{C}.
Additionally, the reiterated application of the Serre functor to any of the semiorthogonal decompositions (
1.2
) leads to permuting through all three of them.

We construct discrete pre-stability conditions on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
in two different ways. The first approach is to construct pre-stability conditions on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
by using the semiorthogonal decompositions (
1.2
) to glue stability conditions from
\Stab(D^{b}(\Coh(C))
following
[
17
]
. Moreover, in the Appendix we compare this gluing procedure with the well-known recollement from
[
6
]
, which provides the same hearts when the conditions for gluing as in
[
17
]
(we refer to them as CP-glued hearts) are satisfied and we prove that in the case of
\mathcal{T}_{C}
we cannot obtain stability conditions from hearts constructed by recollement using hearts that are not CP-glued hearts. The second approach is to tilt the standard heart in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
with respect to a certain torsion pair following
[
24
]
.

The next step is then to study the structure of the stability manifold
\Stab(\mathcal{T}_{C})
. We first prove that for each pre-stability condition at least two of the three embeddings of
D^{b}(C)
into
\mathcal{T}_{C}
have the property that they map all line bundles and all skyscrapers to stable objects in Theorem
4.13
. This finding is a crucial step in the process of describing the entire stability space as a connected complex manifold.

It turns out, however, that there are stability conditions that are not in a
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-orbit with a stability condition obtained by CP-gluing. In Proposition
4.32
we obtain then, that up to the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action, the stability space of
\mathcal{T}_{C}
is given by the stability conditions obtained via either CP-gluing via the semiorthogonal decompositions (
1.2
) or by tilting with regard to the torsion pair from Lemma
3.43
.

In order to prove our main result, we need to verify that tilting and CP-gluing actually produces stability conditions in our situation. This includes the verification of the support property. To prove that the support property is fulfilled for CP-gluing pre-stability conditions we use a generalisation of some of the equations of
[
8
]
to arbitrary stability conditions. For the non-gluing case, under the condition
g(C)=1,
we use the Euler form as a Bogomolov-type inequality. We conjecture that the support property will hold for genus
g(C)>1
as well. We will now extend the Harder–Narashiman-property using Bridgeland’s deformation result to the non-discrete case as in
[
12
]
and
[
3
]
. Hence, we obtain the main theorem of this paper.

Theorem 1.1

(Theorem
6.13
)
.

If

(1)

g(C)=1
or

(2)

g(C)>1
and all non-gluing pre-stability conditions with negative discriminant satisfy the support property,

then
\Stab(\mathcal{T}_{C})
is a connected 4-dimensional complex manifold.

Finally, we conjecture that if
g(C)=1,
then
\mathcal{T}_{C}
is a fractional Calabi–Yau of fractional dimension
\frac{4}{3}
.

As far as the organisation of this paper is concerned, we proceed as follows: after providing the necessary framework in Section 2, we go on to introduce and subsequently make use of the necessary techniques we require to construct pre-stability conditions on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
in Section 3. In Section 4 we study the stability of skyscraper sheaves and line bundles under the embeddings (
1.1
) and we prove that every stability condition on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
is obtained by the techniques introduced in Section 3. Next, the support property is proved in Section 5, while a topological description of the stability space is provided in Section 6. Finally, in the Appendix we compare the two procedures to glue hearts from semiorthogonal decompositions and justify why we restricted the first part of our construction to CP-glued hearts.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Review of t-structures and semiorthogonal decompositions.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a
\mathbb{C}
-linear triangulated category of finite type. For the general theory of t-structures we suggest
[
6
, Chapter 1]
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
t-structure
on a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
consists of a pair of full additive subcategories
(\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0},\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0})
, with
\mathcal{D}^{\leq i}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}[-i]
and
\mathcal{D}^{\geq i}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}[-i]
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, such that:

(1)

\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0},\mathcal{D}^{\geq 1})=0
.

(2)

For all
E\in\mathcal{D}
, there is a distinguished triangle
T\rightarrow E\rightarrow F\rightarrow T[1]

with
T\in\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}
and
F\in\mathcal{D}^{\geq 1}
.

(3)

\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\leq 1}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}\supset\mathcal{D}^{\geq 1}
.

A t-structure is
bounded
if every
E\in\mathcal{D}
is contained in
D^{\leq n}\cap D^{\geq-n}
for some
n>0.

Definition 2.2
.

The
heart
of a bounded t-structure
(\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0},\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0})
is defined as
\mathcal{A}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}.

Remark 2.3
.

[
34
, Lemma 5.2]
The heart of a bounded t-structure
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
is an abelian category whose short exact sequences are precisely the exact triangles in
\mathcal{D}
with objects in
\mathcal{A}.
A morphism
A\rightarrow B
between two objects in
\mathcal{A}
is defined to be an inclusion if its cone is also in
\mathcal{A},
and it is defined to be a surjection if the cone is in
\mathcal{A}[1].

Example 2.4
.

[
6
, Chapter 1]

(1)

If
(\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0},\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0})
is a t-structure, then the pair
(\mathcal{D}^{\leq n},\mathcal{D}^{\geq n})
is also a t-structure.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{D}=D^{b}(\mathcal{A}),
where
\mathcal{A}
is an abelian category. The standard bounded t-structure of
\mathcal{D}
is given by
\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}\coloneqq\{E\mid H^{i}(E)=0\textnormal{ , }i>0\}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}\coloneqq\{E\mid H^{i}(E)=0\textnormal{ , }i<0\}.
Note that
\mathcal{A}
is the heart of the standard bounded t-structure.

Lemma 2.5
.

[
11
, Lemma 3.2]
,
[
28
, Rem. 1.16]

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{D}
be a full additive subcategory of a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
. Then
\mathcal{A}
is the heart of a bounded t-structure if and only if

(1)

\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{A}[k_{1}],\mathcal{A}[k_{2}])=0
for
k_{1}>k_{2}
.

(2)

For every nonzero
E\in\mathcal{D}
there exists a finite sequence of integers

k_{1}>k_{2}>\cdots>k_{m}

and a collection of distinguished triangles

\textstyle{0=E_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{m-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{m}=E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

with
A_{j}\in\mathcal{A}[k_{j}]
for all
j
.

Remark 2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
be the heart of a bounded t-structure. For every object
E\in\mathcal{D},
the objects
A_{j}\in\mathcal{A}[k_{j}]
are its
cohomological objects
with respect to
\mathcal{A}.
They are denoted by
A_{i}=H_{\mathcal{A}}^{-k_{i}}(E).
Moreover, they induce a cohomological functor i.e. they are functorial and induce a long exact sequence of cohomology for any exact triangle. Note that
a t-structure is uniquely determined by its heart (see
[
28
, Def. 1.13]
).

Hearts of bounded t-structures play an important role in the definition of Bridgeland stability conditions. Therefore, we will discuss different ways of giving hearts of bounded t-structures.

Torsion pairs and tilting

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category. A
torsion pair
for
\mathcal{A}
consists of a pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
of full subcategories such that

(1)

\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})=0
.

(2)

For all
E\in\mathcal{A}
, there is a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow T\rightarrow E\rightarrow F\rightarrow 0

with
T\in\mathcal{T}
and
F\in\mathcal{F}
.

Proposition 2.8
.

[
24
, Prop. 2.1]
and
[
34
, Lemma 6.3]
Let
\mathcal{A}
be the heart of a bounded t-structure on
\mathcal{D}.
Given a torsion pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
in
\mathcal{A},
then the full subcategory

\mathcal{A}^{\sharp}=\{E\in\mathcal{D}\mid H_{\mathcal{A}}^{i}(E)=0\textnormal{ for }i\notin\{-1,0\}\textnormal{, }H_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}(E)\in\mathcal{F}\textnormal{ and }H_{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(E)\in\mathcal{T}\}

is the heart of a bounded t-structure on
\mathcal{D}.
We call
\mathcal{A}^{\sharp}
the
tilt
of
\mathcal{A}
with respect to
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}).

Moreover, the torsion pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
gives rise to the torsion pair
(\mathcal{F}[1],\mathcal{T})
for the tilt
\mathcal{A}^{\sharp}.

Remark 2.9
.

Note that
\mathcal{A}^{\sharp}[-1]=(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{T}[-1])
is also the heart of a bounded t-structure. The heart
\mathcal{A}^{\sharp}[-1]=(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{T}[-1])
is called the
right tilt
of
\mathcal{A}
with respect to
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}).

Admissible subcategories and semiorthogonal decompositions

In this section we introduce concepts to study a triangulated category
\mathcal{D},
namely semiorthogonal decompositions and Serre functors. We refer to
[
27
]
and
[
7
]
. If
X
is a smooth projective variety we refer to
D^{b}(\Coh(X))
just as
D^{b}(X).

Definition 2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a triangulated category. Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
be a strictly full triangulated subcategory. The category
\mathcal{A}
is
(left) right-admissible
if the inclusion functor
i\colon\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
has a (left) right adjoint (
i^{*}\colon\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
)
i^{!}\colon\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}.
It is called
admissible
if it is right and left admissible.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a triangulated category. A
semiorthogonal decomposition
of
\mathcal{D}
consists of a collection
\mathcal{A}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{A}_{n}
of full triangulated subcategories such that

(1)

\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{A}_{j})=0
for every
1\leq j<i\leq n
.

(2)

\mathcal{D}
is generated by the
\mathcal{A}_{i}
.

We write
\mathcal{D}=\langle\mathcal{A}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{A}_{n}\rangle
.

Lemma 2.12

(
[
7
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a triangulated category. Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be strictly full triangulated subcategories of
\mathcal{D}
. Assume that
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})=0
. Then, the following are equivalent:

(1)

The category
\mathcal{D}
is generated by
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
i.e. for each
X\in\mathcal{D}
, there exists a distinguished triangle
B\rightarrow X\rightarrow A\rightarrow B[1]

with
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
i.e.
\mathcal{D}=\langle\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\rangle.

(2)

\mathcal{B}=\prescript{\perp}{}{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq\{D\in\mathcal{D}\mid\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(D,A)=0\textnormal{ for all }A\in\mathcal{A}\}
and there exists a functor
i^{*}\colon\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
which is left adjoint to the inclusion
i\colon\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}
i.e.
\mathcal{A}
is left admissible.

(3)

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}^{\perp}\coloneqq\{D\in\mathcal{D}\mid\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(B,D)=0\textnormal{ for all }B\in\mathcal{B}\}
and there exists a functor
j^{!}\colon\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
which is right adjoint to the inclusion
j\colon\mathcal{B}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}
i.e.
\mathcal{B}
is right admissible.

Remark 2.13
.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
be an autoequivalence. If there is a semiorthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{D}=\langle\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\rangle,
then
\mathcal{D}=\langle F(\mathcal{A}),F(\mathcal{B})\rangle.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a
k
-linear triangulated category of finite type, where
k
is a field. We say that
\mathcal{D}
is
right (resp. left) saturated
if every contravariant (resp. covariant) cohomology functor
\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\Vect_{k}
of finite type is representable.

Proposition 2.15

(
[
7
, Proposition 2.6]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be right (resp. left) saturated. Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is embedded in a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
as a full triangulated subcategory. Then
\mathcal{A}
is right (resp. left) admissible.

Theorem 2.16

(
[
7
, Theorem 2.14]
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety. Then,
D^{b}(X)
is right and left saturated.

Serre functor

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a
\mathbb{C}
-linear triangulated category.

Definition 2.17
.

A
Serre functor
on
\mathcal{D}
is an exact autoequivalence
S\colon\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
such that for any
E,F\in\mathcal{D}
, there is an isomorphism

\eta_{E,F}\colon\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(E,F)\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(F,S(E))^{*}

(of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces) which is functorial in
E
and
F
.

Remark 2.18
.

For
\mathcal{D}
of finite type, a Serre functor, if it exists, is unique up to isomorphism. Moreover, it commutes with equivalences, i.e. for
F:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
an equivalence,
S_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\circ F\cong F\circ S_{\mathcal{D}}
. Furthermore, given an admissible subcategory
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{D},
by Serre duality
S_{\mathcal{D}}
sends
\prescript{\perp}{}{\mathcal{X}}
to
\mathcal{X}^{\perp}.

Example 2.19
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety defined over
\mathbb{C}.
The autoequivalence of
D^{b}(X)
given by
\mathcal{S}_{X}(E)=E\otimes\omega_{X}[\dim X],
where
\omega_{X}
is the dualizing line bundle, is the Serre functor on
D^{b}(X)
. In particular, if
X
is a Calabi–Yau variety, its Serre functor is simply the shift functor
S_{X}=[\dim X]
.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. A triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
is a
n
-
Calabi-Yau category
if it has a Serre functor
S_{\mathcal{D}}
and
S_{\mathcal{D}}\cong[n]
. The integer
n
is called the
CY-dimension
of
\mathcal{D}
.

Definition 2.21
.

A triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
is a
fractional Calabi–Yau category
if it has a Serre functor
S_{\mathcal{D}}
and there are integers
p
and
q\neq 0
such that
S^{q}_{\mathcal{D}}\cong[p]
. In this case we say that
\mathcal{D}
has (
CY
-)
fractional dimension

p/q
.

The following result plays an important role in proving the existence of the Serre functor on
\mathcal{T}_{C}.

Proposition 2.22

(
[
7
, Proposition 3.8]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a triangulated category and
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{D}
an admissible full triangulated subcategory with
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\mathcal{B}^{\perp}
admissible. If
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
have Serre functors, then there exists a Serre functor on
\mathcal{D}
.

2.2.
Review of Bridgeland stability conditions

In this section we define Bridgeland stability conditions on a
\mathbb{C}
-linear triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
of finite type. We follow Bridgeland’s papers
[
11
]
and
[
12
]
. We also recommend the following lecture notes on Bridgeland stability theory
[
34
]
,
[
1
]
,
[
28
]
and

[
3
, Appendix B.]
.

Definition 2.23
.

The
Grothendieck group

K(\mathcal{D})
of a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
is the abelian group generated by the isomorphism classes of objects in
\mathcal{D}
subject to the relation
[A]=[C]+[B],
where
C\rightarrow A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C[1]
is an exact triangle.
We consider the Euler bilinear form given by
\chi(E,F)=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(E,F[i]).
We define the
numerical Grothendieck group

\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{D})
as the quotient
K(\mathcal{D})/K(\mathcal{D})^{\perp},
where
K(\mathcal{D})^{\perp}
denotes the right orthogonal with respect to the Euler form. Moreover, if
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{D})
has finite rank then
\mathcal{D}
is called
numerically finite
.

Example 2.24
.

(1)

If
X
is a smooth projective variety over
\mathbb{C}
, then
D^{b}(X)
is numerically finite.

(2)

If
\mathcal{D}=\langle D_{1},\dots,D_{n}\rangle,
then
K(\mathcal{D})=\oplus_{i}K(D_{i}).

Throughout this entire section, we assume that
\mathcal{D}
is numerically finite.

Definition 2.25
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is an abelian category, we define the Grothendieck group
K(\mathcal{A})
as the abelian group generated by isomorphism classes of objects of
\mathcal{A}
subject to the relation

[A]=[C]+[B],
where
0\rightarrow C\rightarrow A\rightarrow B\rightarrow 0
is a short exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}.
If
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
is a heart of a bounded t-structure then
K(\mathcal{D})\cong K(\mathcal{A}).

We now fix a finite rank
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice
\Lambda
and a surjective homomorphism
v\colon K(\mathcal{D})\twoheadrightarrow\Lambda.

Since
\mathcal{D}
is numerically finite, then
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{D})
is a finite rank
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice. We often choose
\Lambda=\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{D})
and
v
as the natural projection.

The definition of a Bridgeland stability condition has two main components: the heart of a bounded t-structure and a stability function.

Definition 2.26
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category. We say that a group homomorphism
Z\colon K(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a
weak stability function
on
\mathcal{A}
if, for all
E\in\mathcal{A}
, we have
\Im(Z([E]))\geq 0
, with
\Im(Z([E]))=0
implying
\Re(Z([E]))\leq 0.

If additionally, for
E\neq 0,
we have that if
\Im(Z([E]))=0
then
\Re(Z([E]))<0
, we say that
Z
is a
stability function
on
\mathcal{A}
. Note that in this case, the image of
Z
is contained in the semi-closed upper half plane

\overline{\mathbb{H}}=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\mid\Im(\alpha)\geq 0\textnormal{ and if }\Im(\alpha)=0\textnormal{, then }\Re(\alpha)<0\}.

We consider a group homomorphism
Z\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{C},
such that
Z\circ v\colon K(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a stability function on
\mathcal{A}
. We define the slope
\mu_{Z}\colon K(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\infty
by

\mu_{Z}(E)=\begin{cases}-\frac{\Re(Z(E))}{\Im(Z(E))}&\textnormal{ if }\Im(Z(E))\neq 0\\
+\infty&\textnormal{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
Z(E)\coloneqq Z(v([E])).

We say that
0\neq E\in\mathcal{A}
is
Z-semistable (stable)
if for all proper subobjects
F\subseteq E,
we have that
\mu_{Z}(F)\leq\mu_{Z}(E)(\mu_{Z}(F)<\mu_{Z}(E)).
We also define the phase of
0\neq E
as
\phi(E)=\arg(Z(E))\frac{1}{\pi}\in(0,1].
Note that
E
is
Z
-semistable if and only if for all proper subobjects
F\subseteq E,
we have that
\phi(F)\leq\phi(E).
We will constantly use the correspondence between slope and phase given for the complex numbers in the semi-closed upper half plane.

Definition 2.27
.

A stability function
Z\colon K(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
satisfies the
Harder–Narasimhan property
(HN-property, for short) on
\mathcal{A}
if for every
0\neq E\in\mathcal{A},
there is a filtration
0=E_{0}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq E_{m-1}\subseteq E_{m}=E
on
\mathcal{A},
such that
E_{i}/E_{i-1}
is
Z
-semistable for
i=1,\ldots,m
and
\phi(E_{1}/E_{0})>\cdots>\phi(E_{m}/E_{m-1}).

Moreover, as the HN-filtration is unique, we define the
HN-factors
of
E
as the quotients
E_{i}/E_{i-1}.

Definition 2.28
.

A
(weak) pre-stability condition
on
\mathcal{D}
is a pair
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A}),
where
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
is the heart of a bounded t-structure and
Z\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a group homomorphism such that
Z\circ v\colon K(\mathcal{A})(=K(\mathcal{D}))\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a (weak) stability function on
\mathcal{A}
satisfying the HN-property. The homomorphism
Z
is also called a central charge.

Remark 2.29
.

[
2
, Proposition 2.9]
Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
be a weak pre-stability condition, and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}.
We form the following subcategories of
\mathcal{A}

\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}^{\alpha}\coloneqq\{E\in\mathcal{A}\mid\textnormal{The HN-factors $F$ of $E$ satisfy }\mu_{Z}(F)>\alpha\},

\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{\alpha}\coloneqq\{E\in\mathcal{A}\mid\textnormal{The HN-factors $F$ of $E$ satisfy }\mu_{Z}(F)\leq\alpha\}.

By the HN-property, we obtain that
(\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}^{\alpha},\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{\alpha})
is a torsion pair on
\mathcal{A}.

Therefore, by
[
24
, Proposition 2.1]
, after tilting we obtain a heart of a bounded t-structure denoted by
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{\alpha}
with a torsion pair given by
(\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}^{\alpha}[1],\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}_{\sigma}).

We will now define a slicing. Intuitively, a heart of a bounded t-structure
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
breaks up every object in
\mathcal{D}
in terms of its cohomology indexed by
\mathbb{Z},
a slicing further refines the heart of a bounded t-structure, which allows us to break up each object into pieces indexed by the real numbers.

Definition 2.30
.

[
11
]
A
slicing

\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{D}
is a collection of full subcategories
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
for all
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying:

(1)

\mathcal{P}(\phi)[1]=\mathcal{P}(\phi+1)
, for all
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

If
\phi_{1}>\phi_{2}
and
E_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\phi_{i})
,
i=1,2
, then
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(E_{1},E_{2})=0
.

(3)

For every nonzero object
E\in\mathcal{D}
there exists a finite sequence of maps

0=E_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}E_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\cdots\rightarrow E_{m-1}\xrightarrow{f_{m-1}}E_{m}=E

and of real numbers
\phi_{0}>\cdots>\phi_{m}
such that the cone of
f_{j}
is in
\mathcal{P}(\phi_{j})
for
j=0,\cdots,{m-1}
.

For every interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
we define
\mathcal{P}(I)
as the extension-closed subcategory generated by the subcategories
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
with
\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

Proposition 2.31

(
[
11
, Proposition 5.3]
)
.

To give a pre-stability condition
\sigma
on
\mathcal{D}
is equivalent to giving a slicing
\mathcal{P}
and a group homomorphism
Z\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that for every
0\neq E\in\mathcal{P}(\phi),
we have that
Z(E)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\cdot e^{i\pi\phi}.

Remark 2.32
.

The key point of the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 5.3]
is to show that if
\mathcal{P}
is a slicing, then
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{P}(0,1]
is the heart of a bounded t-structure satisfying the HN-property. If we have a pre-stability condition
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A}),
we define
\mathcal{P}(\phi),
for
\phi\in(0,1]
as the set of all
Z
-semistable objects in
\mathcal{A}.

Remark 2.33
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{P})
be a pre-stability condition. The objects of
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
are called
\sigma
-semistable
and the simple ones
\sigma
-stable
objects.
By Definition
2.30
, for every
E\in\mathcal{D},
there is a filtration associated to
E
, that we also refer to as the Harder–Narasimhan filtration. The semistable objects in the filtration are called
Harder–Narasimhan factors
(HN-factors, for short). Moreover, we write
\phi^{+}(E),\phi^{-}(E)
for the largest and the smallest phase appearing in this filtration respectively. If
E
is
\sigma
-semistable,
\phi^{+}(E)=\phi^{-}(E)=\phi(E).

Remark 2.34
.

(1)

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
be a pre-stability condition. By definition if
E\in\mathcal{D}
is
\sigma
-semistable, then there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
E[n]\in\mathcal{A}.

(2)

If
E,A\in\mathcal{D}
and
\phi^{-}(E)>\phi^{+}(A),
then
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(E,A)=0.

(3)

Consider the last triangle
E_{m-1}\rightarrow E\rightarrow A_{m}\rightarrow E_{m-1}[1]
of the HN-filtration of
E\in\mathcal{D},
where
A_{m}
is the cone of
f_{m-1}.
We have that
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}^{\leq 0}(E_{m-1},A_{m})=0.

Let
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
be the universal covering of
\rm{GL}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
whose elements are given by pairs
(T,f)
where
T\in\rm{GL}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
and
f\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a continuous increasing function that satisfies
f(x+1)=f(x)+1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
such that the induced maps of
T
and
f
on
S^{1}=\mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z}=(\mathbb{R}^{2}-\{(0,0)\})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
coincide. In the next section we study in detail
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
and its action on the set of pre-stability conditions.

We define a right action of
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
on the set of pre-stability conditions. If
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
is a pre-stability condition and
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
then we define
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma g=(Z^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
as
Z=T^{-1}\circ Z
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\phi)=\mathcal{P}(f(\phi)),
where
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
are the slicings of
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
respectively.
Note that the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action preserves the semistable objects, but relabels their phases.

Let us consider the group
\Aut_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{D})
of autoequivalences
\Phi
on
\mathcal{D}
whose induced automorphism
\phi_{*}
of
K(\mathcal{D})
is compatible with the map
v\colon K(\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\Lambda.

We define a left action of the group
\Aut_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{D})
on the set of pre-stability conditions. For
\Phi\in\Aut_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{D})
of
\mathcal{D}.
We define
\Phi(\sigma)=(Z^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
as
Z^{\prime}=Z\circ\phi^{-1}_{*}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\phi)=\Phi(\mathcal{P}(\phi)).
Note that if
E
is a
\sigma
-semistable object, then
\Phi(E)
is
\Phi(\sigma)
-semistable.

Definition 2.35
.

A pre-stability condition
\sigma
is
locally finite
if there is some
\epsilon>0
such that each category
\mathcal{P}((\phi-\epsilon,\phi+\epsilon)),
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R},
is of finite length.

Definition 2.36
.

A pre-stability condition is
discrete
if the image of
Z
is a discrete subgroup of
\mathbb{C}.

Lemma 2.37

(
[
12
, Lemma 4.5]
)
.

Suppose that
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{P})
is a discrete pre-stability condition and fix
0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{2}.
Then for each
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
the category
\mathcal{P}((\phi-\epsilon,\phi+\epsilon))
is of finite length. In particular
\sigma
is locally finite.

Remark 2.38

(
[
11
, Lemma 5.2]
)
.

The categories
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
with
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
are abelian. If
\sigma
is locally finite, then
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
has finite length. Therefore, a
\sigma
-semistable object
E\in\mathcal{P}(\phi)
admits a finite Jordan-Hölder filtration, i.e. a finite filtration
E_{0}\subset\ldots\subset E_{n}=E
with stable quotient
E_{i+1}/E_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\phi),
as the stable objects are the simple objects in
\mathcal{P}(\phi).

We will now recall the support property. It plays an important role in proving good deformation properties and a well-behaved wall and chamber decomposition.
We suggest
[
5
, App. A]
to understand better the relation between the support property and effective deformations of Bridgeland stability conditions.

Definition 2.39
.

A pre-stability condition
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
satisfies the
support property
if there is a symmetric bilinear form
Q
on
\Lambda\otimes\mathbb{R}\coloneqq\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}}

which satisfies

(1)

All
\sigma
-semistable objects
E\in\mathcal{A},
satisfy
Q(v(E),v(E))\geq 0.

(2)

All nonzero vectors
v\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}}
with
Z(v)=0
satisfy
Q(v,v)<0.

Remark 2.40
.

If
\rk(\Lambda)=2
and
Z\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is injective, then every pre-stability condition
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
trivially satisfies the support property with respect to any positive semidefinite quadratic form.

Definition 2.41
.

A pre-stability condition
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
that satisfies the support property is called a
Bridgeland stability condition
. The set of Bridgeland stability conditions with respect to
(\Lambda,v)
is denoted by
\Stab_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{D}).
If
\Lambda=\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{D})
and
v
the natural projection, then the set of stability conditions is denoted by
\Stab(\mathcal{D}).

Remark 2.42
.

There is a generalized metric on the set of slicings
\Slice(\mathcal{D}),
i.e. a metric that does not need to be finite: given two slicings
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q},
we define

d(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})=\sup_{0\neq E\in\mathcal{D}}\{\lvert\phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{+}(E)-\phi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}(E)\rvert,\lvert\phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{-}(E)-\phi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{-}(E)\rvert\}\in[0,\infty].

In order to study
\Stab_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{D}),
we study the projection

\mathcal{Z}\colon\Stab_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Lambda,\mathbb{C}).

Theorem 2.43

(
[
11
, Theorem. 7.1]
)
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{P})
be a stability condition. If
1/8>\epsilon>0,
then for any group homomorphism
W\colon K(\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
with
||W-Z||_{\sigma}<\sin(\pi\epsilon),
there exists a stability condition
\tau=(W,\mathcal{Q})
on
\mathcal{D}
with
d(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})<\epsilon.

Theorem 2.44

(
[
11
]
)
.

The map
\mathcal{Z}\colon\Stab_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\Hom(\Lambda,\mathbb{C})
is a local homeomorphism. Particularly, this implies that
\Stab_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{D})
is a complex manifold of dimension
\rk(\Lambda).

For a complete proof see
[
1
, Sec 5.5]
.

Lemma 2.45

(
[
12
, Lemma 4.5]
,
[
3
, Proposition B.4]
)
.

Let
\sigma
be a Bridgeland stability condition and fix
0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{2}.
Then, the quasi-abelian subcategory
\mathcal{P}((\phi-\epsilon,\phi+\epsilon))
is of finite length, for each
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
. As a consequence, Bridgeland stability conditions are locally-finite.

The main point of the proof of the last lemma is that for a Bridgeland stability condition
\sigma
there exist discrete stability conditions arbitrarily close to
\sigma.

Remark 2.46
.

Let
\sigma\in\Stab_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{D})
and
g\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
then
\sigma g
is also a Bridgeland stability condition. Indeed, if
\sigma
satisfies the support property with respect to
Q,
then
\sigma g
also satisfies the support property with respect to
Q.
If
\Phi\in\Aut_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{D}),
then
\Phi(\sigma)
satisfies the support property with respect to
Q\circ\phi^{-1}_{*}.
By
[
11
, Lemma 8.2]
, the right action of
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
and the left action of
\Aut_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{D})
on
\Stab_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{D})
commute.

2.3.
Bridgeland stability conditions on curves with
g>0

Let
C
be a nonsingular projective curve of genus
g(C)>0.

To describe
\Stab(C)\coloneqq\Stab(D^{b}(C)),
we follow closely
[
11
, Section 9]
and
[
33
]
.
Note that there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{N}(D^{b}(C))\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
given by
(\deg(E),\rk(E))
for
E\in D^{b}(C).

To describe
\Stab(C),
one studies the last triangle of their HN-filtrations
E\rightarrow X\rightarrow G\rightarrow E[1],
where
X
is either
\mathbb{C}(x)
or
\mathcal{L}.
Note that by Remark
2.34
, we have that
\Hom^{\leq 0}(E,G)=0.
The next lemma is a strong consequence of
D^{b}(C)
being hereditary, i.e. it has homological dimension
1
and the fact that
g(C)>0.

Lemma 2.47

(
[
23
, Lemma 7.2]
)
.

Given a distinguished triangle
E\rightarrow X\rightarrow G\rightarrow E[1]
in
D^{b}(C)
,
with
X\in\Coh(C)
and
\Hom^{\leq 0}_{D^{b}(C)}(E,G)=0
, then
E,G\in\Coh(C)
.

Proposition 2.48

(
[
33
]
)
.

For any
\sigma\in\Stab(D^{b}(C))
, every line bundle
\mathcal{L}
and skyscraper sheaf
\mathbb{C}(x)
of a point
x\in C
are
\sigma
-stable.

Moreover, there is a distinguished stability condition given by the standard slope stability:
\sigma_{\mu}\coloneqq(Z_{\mu},\coh(C))

where
Z_{\mu}(r,d)=-d+ir
with
(r,d)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
r\coloneqq\rk(E)
and
d\coloneqq\deg(E)
for
[E]\in\mathcal{N}(D^{b}(C)).
The next theorem states that this is in fact the only stability condition in
\Stab(D^{b}(C))
up to the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action.

Theorem 2.49

(
[
11
, Theorem. 9.1]
,
[
33
, Theorem 2.7]
)
.

The action of
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
on
\Stab(D^{b}(C))
is free and transitive. In particular,

(2.1)

\Stab(D^{b}(C))\cong\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}).

Remark 2.50
.

Let us consider
\sigma_{\mu}=(Z_{\mu},\Coh(C))
and its corresponding slicing
\mathcal{P}_{\mu}.
Note that
\Coh^{r}(C)\coloneqq\mathcal{P}_{\mu}(r,r+1]
for
r\in\mathbb{R}
is a heart of a bounded t-structure. All the hearts appearing in the stability conditions
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})\in\Stab(C)
are of this form. Indeed, if
\sigma=\sigma_{\mu}g
with
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
we obtain that
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{P}_{\mu}(f(0),f(1)=f(0)+1].
If
f(0)=n+\theta,
with
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\theta\in[0,1)
then we also have
\mathcal{A}=\Coh^{\theta}(C)[n].

For our purpose it is important to understand the isomorphism (
2.1
). We define the following matrices in
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})

K_{\phi}=\begin{bmatrix}\cos(\phi)&-\sin(\phi)\\
\sin(\phi)&\cos(\phi),\end{bmatrix}\textnormal{, }A_{a}=\begin{bmatrix}a&0\\
0&\frac{1}{a}\end{bmatrix}\textnormal{ and }N_{x}=\begin{bmatrix}1&x\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},

for
\phi\in[0,2\pi),

x,a\in\mathbb{R}
and
a>0.

Lemma 2.51

(Iwasawa decomposition
[
26
, Section 16.3]
)
.

For every
T\in\rm{GL}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
there are real numbers
\phi\in[0,2\pi),

k,a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
x\in\mathbb{R},
such that
T=kK_{\phi}A_{a}N_{x}.
Moreover, this representation is unique.

Let us consider
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
where
T=kK_{\phi}A_{a}N_{x}
and
f(0)=n+\theta,
as above. We will now relate
\phi
and
\theta.

Remark 2.52
.

(1)

Let us consider
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
where
T=kK_{\phi}A_{a}N_{x}
as above, then there is
m\in\mathbb{Z},
such that
2m+\frac{\phi}{\pi}=f(0).
Moreover, we have two cases: either

n=2m\textnormal{ with }\theta=\frac{\phi}{\pi}\textnormal{ or }n=2m+1\textnormal{ with }\theta=\frac{\phi}{\pi}-1.

(2)

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})\in\Stab(C).
If
\sigma=\sigma_{\mu}g
with
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
where
T=kK_{\phi}A_{a}N_{x}
then
\mathcal{A}=\Coh^{\frac{\phi}{\pi}}(C)[2m].

(3)

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})\in\Stab(C).
If
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma g
with
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
where
T=\pm kA_{a}N_{x},
then there is
l\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}[l]
where
\sigma^{\prime}=(Z^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}).

(4)

Let
\sigma=\sigma_{\mu}g=(Z,\mathcal{A})
with
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
and
T^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-A&B\\
-D&C\end{bmatrix}
. We obtain that
Z=T^{-1}Z_{\mu},
i.e.
Z(d,r)=Ad+Br+i(Cr+Dd).
Moreover, if
T=kK_{\phi}A_{a}N_{x},
then
\arg(C+Di)=\phi.
Indeed, as
C=\cos(\phi)\frac{a}{k}\textnormal{ and }D=\sin(\phi)\frac{a}{k},
then

\arg(C+Di)=\arg(\cos(\phi)+\sin(\phi)i)=\phi.

(5)

Let
\theta\in(0,1).
First note, that by the HN-property, if
\mathcal{T}_{\theta}=\mathcal{P}_{\mu}(\theta,1]
and
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}=\mathcal{P}_{\mu}(0,\theta],
then
(\mathcal{T}_{\theta},\mathcal{F}_{\theta})
is a torsion pair of
\Coh(C).
By
[
24
, Proposition 2.1]
it induces a heart of a bounded t-structure which is precisely
\Coh^{\theta}(C).
Moreover, if we define
\alpha=-\cot(\pi\theta),
then by Remark
2.29
we obtain that
\Coh^{\theta}(C)=\Coh(C)^{\alpha}_{{\sigma}_{\mu}}.
Indeed, it is enough to notice that
\mathcal{T}_{\theta}=\mathcal{T}_{\sigma_{\mu}}^{\alpha}
and that
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}=\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{\mu}}^{\alpha},
because

\mu(E)>\alpha\textnormal{ if and only if }\phi(E)>\theta.

Let
\sigma=\sigma_{\mu}g=(Z,\mathcal{A})
with
g\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
and
\mathcal{P}
its slicing. The objects
\mathbb{C}(x)
and
\mathcal{L}
are
\sigma
-stable. By the definition of the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action, this implies that for every
\sigma\in\Stab(C)
we obtain
\phi_{0}\coloneqq\phi_{\sigma}(\mathbb{C}(x))
for
x\in C.

By definition of the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action, we obtain that
\mathcal{P}(\phi_{0})=\mathcal{P}_{\mu}(f(\phi_{0}))=\mathcal{P}_{\mu}(1),
i.e.
f(\phi_{0})=1.
Analogously, if
\phi_{1}=\phi_{\sigma}(\mathcal{O}_{C}),
then
f(\phi_{1})=\frac{1}{2}.

Remark 2.53
.

(1)

We have that
-n<\phi_{0}=f^{-1}(1)\leq-n+1\textnormal{ if and only if }f(0)=n+\theta,
with
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\theta\in[0,1).

(2)

There is a homeomorphism

\displaystyle\rho\colon\Stab(C)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{(m_{0},m_{1},\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\mid\phi_{1}<\phi_{0}<\phi_{1}+1,\textnormal{ and }m_{0},m_{1}>0\}

\displaystyle\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(m_{0},m_{1},\phi_{0},\phi_{1}),

where
m_{0}=|Z([\mathbb{C}(x)])|,m_{1}=|Z([\mathcal{O}_{C}])|.

3.
Bridgeland stability conditions on
\mathcal{T}_{C}

Let
C
denote a smooth projective curve of genus
g>0
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
holomorphic triple

E=(E_{1},E_{2},\varphi)
(also denoted by
E=E_{1}\overset{\varphi}{\rightarrow}E_{2}
) on
C
consists of two coherent sheaves
E_{1},E_{2}\in\coh(C)
and a sheaf morphism
\varphi\colon E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}
between them. We denote by
\TCoh(C)
the category of holomorphic triples on
C.

Remark 3.2
.

The category
\TCoh(C)
is abelian (see
[
29
, Theorem 1]
).

3.1.
The triangulated category
\mathcal{T}_{C}

To be studied in this subsection is the category
\mathcal{T}_{C}\coloneqq D^{b}(\TCoh(C)).
We construct different semiorthogonal decompositions for
\mathcal{T}_{C}.
We prove the existence of the Serre functor.

We note that we have three different ways to embed
\Coh(C)
into
\TCoh(C)
:

\displaystyle i_{*}\colon\Coh(C)

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow

\displaystyle\TCoh(C)

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle X\rightarrow 0,

\displaystyle j_{*}\colon\Coh(C)

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow

\displaystyle\TCoh(C)

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow X,

\displaystyle l_{*}\colon\Coh(C)

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow

\displaystyle\TCoh(C)

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle X\xrightarrow{\id}X.

As these functors are exact, we take their corresponding derived functors and by
[
14
, Theorem 2.4]
,
we obtain three different exact embeddings from
D^{b}(C)
into
\TCoh(C)
. We adapt the same notation
i_{*}
,
j_{*}
,
l_{*}
. We denote the strictly full subcategories of
\mathcal{T}_{C}
obtained as images of
D^{b}(C)
in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
under each embedding (
i_{*}
,
j_{*}
,
l_{*}
respectively) by
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
.

Since the subcategories
\mathcal{C}_{i}
of
\mathcal{T}_{C}
are equivalent to
D^{b}(C),
they are also saturated.

Remark 3.3

(
[
7
]
)
.

As a consequence of Proposition
2.15
, we obtain that the strictly full subcategories
\mathcal{C}_{i}
are admissible in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
for all
i=1,2,3.

Proposition 3.4
.

The triangulated category
\mathcal{T}_{C}
admits 3 semiorthogonal decompositions

\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle\textnormal{, }\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}\rangle\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{3},\mathcal{C}_{1}\rangle.

Proof.

By Remark
3.3
we already know that the
\mathcal{C}_{i}
are admissible in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
for all
i=1,2,3.

We define the following functors

\displaystyle l_{*}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{C}_{3}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{C}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto X\overset{\id}{\rightarrow}X

\displaystyle l^{*}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{T}_{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{3}

\displaystyle E_{1}\overset{\varphi}{\rightarrow}E_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto E_{2}

\displaystyle l^{!}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{T}_{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{3}

\displaystyle E_{1}\overset{\varphi}{\rightarrow}E_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto E_{1}.

Left adjointness,
(l^{*},l_{*})
and right adjointness,
(l_{*},l^{!})
follow directly from the definitions.

Additionally, we define the functors

\displaystyle i_{*}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{C}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto X\rightarrow 0

\displaystyle i^{*}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{T}_{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{1}

\displaystyle E_{1}\overset{\varphi}{\rightarrow}E_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto E_{1}.

Left adjointness,
(i^{*},i_{*})
follows directly from the definitions.

Finally, we have to prove that

\mathcal{C}_{3}^{\perp}=\{E\in\mathcal{T}_{C}\mid\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(l_{*}\mathcal{C},E)=0\}=\mathcal{C}_{2}.

Indeed, by adjointness we have
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(l_{*}(X),E)=\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(X,l^{!}(E))
for any
E=E_{1}\overset{\varphi}{\rightarrow}E_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
and any
X\in\mathcal{C}
. This means that
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(X,E_{1})=0
for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
. This happens if and only if
E_{1}=0
.

With a similar argument, one can see that

\prescript{\perp}{}{\mathcal{C}}_{3}=\mathcal{C}_{1}.
and
\prescript{\perp}{}{\mathcal{C}}_{1}=\mathcal{C}_{2}.

∎

Remark 3.5
.

Let
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{C}.

(1)

The semiorthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle
induces the distinguished triangle
j_{*}(E_{2})\rightarrow E\rightarrow i_{*}(E_{1})\rightarrow j_{*}(E_{2})[1].

(2)

The semiorthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{3},\mathcal{C}_{1}\rangle
induces the distinguished triangle

i_{*}i^{!}(E)\rightarrow E\rightarrow l_{*}(E_{2})\rightarrow i_{*}i^{!}(E)[1].

(3)

The semiorthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}\rangle
induces the distinguished triangle
l_{*}(E_{1})\rightarrow E\rightarrow j_{*}(j^{*}(E))\rightarrow l_{*}(E_{1})[1].

By admissibility of the full subcategories
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
, and the unicity of the triangles associated to the semiorthogonal decompositions, we have that
l^{*}=j^{!}
, where
j^{!}
satisfies right adjunction
(j_{*},j^{!})
, similarly
l^{!}=i^{*}
.

In order to describe the precise distinguished triangles associated to the semiorthogonal decompositions of
\mathcal{T}_{C}
we need to understand the functors
i^{!}
and
j^{*}
satisfying the adjunction relations
(i_{*},i^{!})
and
(j^{*},j_{*})
respectively. We will now describe the functors
i^{!}
and
j^{*}
at the level of objects and some features at the level of morphisms.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
E=(E_{1},E_{2},\varphi_{E})
and
F=(F_{1},F_{2},\varphi_{F})\in\mathcal{T}_{C}.
The functor
i^{!}
is given by

(3.1)

\displaystyle i^{!}\colon\mathcal{T}_{C}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle D^{b}(C)

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle C(\varphi_{E})[-1],

where
C(-)
is the cone,
at the level of objects. If
\psi\colon E\rightarrow F
is a morphism of triples, then the following diagram commutes

(3.2)

\textstyle{C(\varphi_{E})[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{!}(\psi)}
\scriptstyle{{\pi}_{E}}
\textstyle{C(\varphi_{F})[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\pi}_{F}}
\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{*}(\psi)}
\textstyle{F_{1}.}

Proof.

From the triangle in part (3) from Remark
3.5
and the fact that
i^{*}
is an exact functor, we obtain the triangle

i^{!}(E)\xrightarrow{\pi_{E}}E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\rightarrow i^{!}(E),

therefore
i^{!}(E)=C(\varphi_{E})[-1].

We use the naturality of the adjunction and we obtain
\psi\circ\pi_{E}=i_{*}i^{!}(\psi)\circ\pi_{F}
and by taking
i^{*}
in both sides we obtain the square (
3.2
).
∎

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
E=(E_{1},E_{2},\varphi_{E})
and
F=(F_{1},F_{2},\varphi_{F})\in\mathcal{T}_{C}.
The functor
j^{*}
is given by

(3.3)

\displaystyle j^{*}\colon\mathcal{T}_{C}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle D^{b}(C)

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle C(\varphi_{E})

at the level of objects. If
\psi\colon E\rightarrow F
is a morphism of triples, then the following diagram commutes

(3.4)

\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{!}(\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{E}}
\textstyle{F_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{F}}
\textstyle{C(\varphi_{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}(\psi)}
\textstyle{C(\varphi_{F}).}

Proof.

The proof goes along the lines of Lemma
3.6
.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

In many cases, we express a morphism
\Phi\in\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}}(E,F)
as two horizontal arrows, but note that they just represent
i^{*}(\Phi)
and
j^{!}(\Phi)
and they do not characterize the morphism
\Phi.

We denote by
\mathcal{N}(C)=\mathcal{N}(D^{b}(C))
the numerical Grothendieck group of
\coh(C).

Remark 3.9
.

Note that
K(\mathcal{T}_{C})=K(D^{b}(C))\oplus K(D^{b}(C)).

Moreover, the Euler form
\chi(E,E^{\prime})\coloneqq\sum\limits_{i}(-1)^{i}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,E^{\prime}[i])
on
\TCoh(C)
vanishes if and only if
r_{1}=r_{2}=0
and
d_{1}=d_{2}=0
, where
d_{i}\coloneqq\deg(E_{i})
,
r_{i}\coloneqq\rk(E_{i})
for
i=1,2
. Hence,
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{T}_{C})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{4}
by identifying the class
[E]\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{T}_{C})
of a holomorphic triple
E=E_{1}\overset{\varphi}{\rightarrow}E_{2}
with the point
(r_{1},d_{1},r_{2},d_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}.

Serre functor

By Proposition
2.22
and Proposition
3.4
, we have the existence of the Serre functor
S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}
on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
. The adjunction properties of the Serre functor provide us with the following lemma.

Lemma 3.10

(
[
37
, Lemma 2.42]
)
.

Let
X\in D^{b}(C)
. The following equalities hold:

(1)

S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(i_{*}(X))=j_{*}(S_{C}(X))[1],

(2)

S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(j_{*}(X))=l_{*}(S_{C}(X)),

(3)

S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(l_{*}(X))=i_{*}(S_{C}(X)),
where
S_{C}
is as in Example
2.19
.

Remark 3.11
.

Let
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}
be an object in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}.
As we have
F^{!}S_{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}}=S_{C}F^{*}
for
F=i,j
or
l,
we directly
i^{!}(S_{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}}(E))=S_{C}(E_{1})\textnormal{ and }j^{!}(S_{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}}(E))=S_{C}(C(\varphi)).

Remark 3.12

(
[
6
, Proposition 1.3.3]
and
[
6
, Corollary 1.1.10]
)
.

Let us consider the triangle
j_{*}j^{!}(E)\rightarrow E\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}(E)\xrightarrow{t_{E}}j_{*}j^{!}(E)[1].
Note that the morphism
t_{E}\colon i_{*}i^{*}(E)\rightarrow j_{*}j^{!}(E)[1]
characterizes the triangle, i.e. it is the unique morphism representing the isomorphism class of the triangle
induced by the semiorthogonal decomposition
\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle
for
E.

Moreover, note that
C(t_{E})[-1]
is isomorphic to
E
up to a nonunique isomorphism in
\mathcal{T}_{C}.

Remark 3.13
.

Let us consider the triangle
j_{*}j^{!}(E)\rightarrow E\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}(E)\xrightarrow{t_{E}}j_{*}j^{!}(E)[1].
After applying the Serre functor we obtain a triangle

S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(j_{*}j^{!}(E))\rightarrow S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E)\rightarrow S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(i_{*}i^{*}(E))\xrightarrow{S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(t_{E})}S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(j_{*}j^{!}(E)[1].

As
S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}({{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{D})=\mathcal{D}^{\perp}
for an admissible triangulated subcategory
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{C},
then
S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(j_{*}j^{!}(E))\in\mathcal{C}_{3}\textnormal{ and }S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(i_{*}i^{*}(E))\in\mathcal{C}_{2}.
By the uniqueness of the triangle induced by the semiorthogonal decomposition, we obtain precisely the corresponding triangle of
S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E)
induced by the semiorthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}\rangle
up to isomorphism. Moreover
S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(t_{E})
is the unique morphism that characterizes the triangle as mentioned in Remark
3.12
. See
[
32
, Lemma 2.3]
and
[
6
, Proposition 1.3.3]
.

The following remark connects the triple
E
with the roof
[\varphi_{E}]\coloneqq(\id,\varphi_{E})
in
\Mor(D^{b}(C),D^{b}(C))
and it plays a role in the explicit construction of the Serre functor.

Remark 3.14

(
[
37
, Lemma 2.49]
)
.

t_{E}=t_{l_{*}(E_{2})}\circ i_{*}([\varphi]).

We will now describe the Serre functor at the level of objects.

Proposition 3.15

(
[
37
, Lemma 2.51]
)
.

Let
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}
be an object of
\mathcal{T}_{C}.
If
S_{C}(i_{E})
is a morphism of complexes, where
E_{2}\xrightarrow{i_{E}}C(\varphi)
is the morphism of complexes given by the injection, then
S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E)
is isomorphic to the triple
S_{C}(E_{2})\xrightarrow{S_{C}(i_{E})}S_{C}(C(\varphi))
in
\mathcal{T}_{C}.

On the other hand, if
S_{C}^{-1}(p_{E})
is a morphism of complexes, where
C(\varphi)[-1]\xrightarrow{p_{E}}E_{1}
is the projection, then
S^{-1}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E)
is isomorphic to
S^{-1}_{C}(C(\varphi)(-1))\xrightarrow{S^{-1}_{C}(p_{E})}S^{-1}_{C}(E_{1}).

Remark 3.16
.

First of all, note that by Serre duality and the semiorthogonal decomposition described above, it is clear that the homological dimension of
\TCoh(C)
is
\leq 2
(see
[
35
, Proposition 2.7.24]
). Moreover, given
E_{1}\in\Coh(C)
, if we take
i_{*}(E_{1})
and
j_{*}(E_{1})
, by Serre duality and Lemma
3.10
we get

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(i_{*}(E_{1}),j_{*}(E_{1})[2])

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(j_{*}(E_{1})[2],S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(i_{*}(E_{1})))^{*}

\displaystyle=\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}(E_{1},E_{1}\otimes\omega_{C})^{*}[2]\neq 0.

This implies that the category
\TCoh(C)
has
homological dimension 2.

If
g(C)=1
, we have the following conjecture.

Conjecture 3.17
.

Let
C
be an elliptic curve. Then
S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{3}=[4]
, which implies that
\mathcal{T}_{C}
is a fractional Calabi–Yau category of fractional dimension
4/3
.

3.2.
Constructing pre-stability conditions

3.2.1.
CP-gluing

In
[
17
]
(and Collins in
[
16
]
) Collins and Polishchuk introduced a way to construct hearts from semiorthogonal decompositions of a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
which later allow us to define stability conditions on
\mathcal{D}
in a natural way.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a triangulated category equipped with a semiorthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{D}=\langle\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2}\rangle
. Let
\rho_{2}\colon\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{2}
be the right adjoint functor to the full embedding
i_{2}\colon\mathcal{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
and let
\lambda_{1}\colon\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{1}
be the left adjoint functor to the full embedding
i_{1}\colon\mathcal{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
.

Proposition 3.18

(
[
17
, Lemma 2.1]
)
.

With the above notations, assume that we have t-structures
(\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\leq 0},\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\geq 0})
with hearts
\mathcal{A}_{i}
on
\mathcal{D}_{i}
, for
i=1,2
, such that

(3.5)

\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}^{\leq 0}(i_{1}\mathcal{A}_{1},i_{2}\mathcal{A}_{2})=0.

Then there is a t-structure on
\mathcal{D}
with the heart

(3.6)

\glu(i_{1}\mathcal{A}_{1},i_{2}\mathcal{A}_{2})=\{E\in\mathcal{D}\mid\rho_{2}E\in\mathcal{A}_{2},\lambda_{1}E\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\}.

Moreover,
i_{k}\mathcal{A}_{k}\subset\mathcal{A}\coloneqq\glu(i_{1}\mathcal{A}_{1},i_{2}\mathcal{A}_{2})
for
k=1,2
.

Definition 3.19
.

We will refer to hearts of the form (
3.6
) as hearts obtained by
CP-gluing
.

Notation 3.20
.

In the case of the semiorthogonal decompositions
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{j}\rangle
, with
ij\in\{12,23,31\}
, we will denote the heart obtained by CP-gluing of hearts
\mathcal{A}_{i}
on
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and
\mathcal{A}_{j}
on
\mathcal{C}_{j}
by
\glu_{ij}(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{A}_{j})
. Recall that for
r\in\mathbb{R},
we define
\Coh^{r}(C)
as in Remark
2.50
.

Proposition 3.21
.

We distinguish 3 cases according to the semiorthogonal decomposition of
\mathcal{T}_{C}
.

(1)

If
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle
, we have

\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{\leq 0}(\Coh_{1}^{r_{1}}(C),\Coh_{2}^{r_{2}}(C))=0
if and only if
r_{1}\geq r_{2}
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{3},\mathcal{C}_{1}\rangle
, we have

\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{\leq 0}(\Coh_{3}^{r_{3}}(C),\Coh_{1}^{r_{1}}(C))=0
if and only if
r_{3}\geq r_{1}+1
.

(3)

If
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}\rangle
, we have

\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{\leq 0}(\Coh_{2}^{r_{2}}(C),\Coh_{3}^{r_{3}}(C))=0
if and only if
r_{2}\geq r_{3}+1
.

Proof.

As in Remark
2.52
, we write each
r_{i}=n_{i}+\theta_{i}
for unique
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\theta_{i}\in[0,1)
.
Firstly, we want to determine under which conditions

\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(\Coh_{1}^{\theta_{1}}(C)[n_{1}],\Coh_{2}^{\theta_{2}}(C)[n_{2}+i])=0

for every
i\leq 0
. Assume
n_{2}=0
, up to shifting by
-n_{2}
. For a fixed
i
, take
0\rightarrow E_{2}\in\Coh_{2}^{\theta_{2}}(C)
and
E_{1}\rightarrow 0\in\Coh_{1}^{\theta_{1}}(C)
. By Serre duality we have

(3.7)

\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E_{1}[n_{1}]\rightarrow 0,0\rightarrow E_{2}[i])=\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(0\rightarrow E_{2},S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E_{1}\rightarrow 0)[n_{1}-i])^{*}.

By Lemma
3.10
,
S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E_{1}\rightarrow 0)[n_{1}]\in j_{*}(S_{C}(\Coh^{\theta_{1}}(C)[n_{1}]))[1]
, so (
3.7
) vanishes for all
i\leq 0
if and only if
n_{1}\geq 0
and if
n_{1}=0
, we see that we need that
\theta_{1}\geq\theta_{2}
. Indeed, if
n_{1}=0
, recall that each heart
\Coh^{\theta_{i}}(C)
was defined by tilting
\Coh^{\theta_{i}}(C)=\langle F_{\theta_{i}}[1],T_{\theta_{i}}\rangle
, for
i=1,2
. By the previous argument with the Serre functor, the only restriction appears when
E_{1}\rightarrow 0\in T_{\theta_{1}}
and
0\rightarrow E_{2}[1]\in F_{\theta_{2}}[1]
. Here, if
\theta_{1}\geq\theta_{2}
, we have
T_{\theta_{1}}\subset T_{\theta_{2}}
. Use Serre duality for
D^{b}(C)
, such that

\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}(E_{2}[1],E_{1}\otimes\omega_{C}[2-i])=\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}(E_{1}[1-i],E_{2}[1])^{*}.

This vanishes for all
i\leq 0
, since
(T_{\theta_{2}},F_{\theta_{2}})
is a torsion pair.

Case 2 (resp. 3) follows applying the Serre functor
S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}
(resp.
S^{-1}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}
) to case 1.
∎

Remark 3.22
.

By Theorem
2.49
, the CP-gluing conditions in Proposition
3.21
can be re-stated in terms of the corresponding elements
(T_{i},f_{i})\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
for
i=1,2,3
by considering
r_{i}=f_{i}(0)
.

Trivial examples of hearts in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
defined by CP-gluing can be given by considering
\coh(C)
embedded in each triangulated subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
as follows.

Definition 3.23

(
[
17
]
)
.

Let
\sigma_{i}=(Z_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i})
be stability conditions on
\mathcal{D}_{i}
for
i=1,2
, such that the hearts
\mathcal{A}_{i}
satisfy (
3.5
). Then we say that a pair
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
on
\mathcal{D}=\langle\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2}\rangle
is a
CP-glued pair
(from
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
) if the heart
\mathcal{A}
is given by (
3.6
) and
Z\colon K(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is given by

(3.8)

Z=Z_{1}\circ\lambda_{1}+Z_{2}\circ\rho_{2}.

Remark 3.24
.

Note that this CP-glued pair is uniquely determined by
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
. It is a pre-stability condition if and only if the Harder–Narasimhan property holds for the stability function
Z
on the glued heart
\mathcal{A}
. We will check this property separately later.

Notation 3.25
.

In the case of the semiorthogonal decompositions
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{j}\rangle
for
ij\in\{12,23,31\}
we will denote by
\glu_{ij}(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})
the CP-glued pair obtained by CP-gluing of stability conditions
\sigma_{i}
on
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and
\sigma_{j}
on
\mathcal{C}_{j}
.

Definition 3.26

(Standard hearts)
.

Consider
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle.
By definition we have that

\TCoh(C)=\gl_{12}(\Coh_{1}(C),\Coh_{2}(C)).

Consider
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{3}\rangle,
then we define

\mathcal{H}_{23}\coloneqq\gl_{23}(\Coh_{2}(C)[1],\Coh_{3}(C)).

Consider
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{3},\mathcal{C}_{1}\rangle,
then we define

\mathcal{H}_{31}\coloneqq\gl_{31}(\Coh_{3}(C)[1],\Coh_{1}(C)).

Lemma 3.27

(
[
17
, Proposition 2.2]
)
.

(1)

A pre-stability condition
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
on
\mathcal{D}
is CP-glued from
\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\mathcal{A}_{1})
on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}=(Z_{2},\mathcal{A}_{2})
on
\mathcal{D}_{2}
if and only if
Z_{i}=Z|_{\mathcal{D}_{i}}
for
i=1,2
,
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}^{\leq 0}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})=0
and
\mathcal{A}_{i}\subset\mathcal{A}
for
i=1,2
.

(2)

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
be a pre-stability condition on
\mathcal{D}
. Assume that the heart
\mathcal{A}
is glued from hearts
\mathcal{A}_{1}\subset\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}\subset\mathcal{D}_{2}
, with
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}^{\leq 0}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})=0
, such that (
3.6
) holds. Then, there exists a pre-stability condition
\sigma_{i}=(Z_{i}=Z|_{\mathcal{D}_{i}},\mathcal{A}_{i})
on
\mathcal{D}_{i}
, for
i=1,2
, such that
\sigma
is glued from
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
.

(3)

If
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{P})
is CP-glued from the pre-stability conditions
\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\mathcal{P}_{1})
and
\sigma_{2}=(Z_{2},\mathcal{P}_{2})
, then
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\phi)\subset\mathcal{P}(\phi)
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\phi)\subset\mathcal{P}(\phi)
for every
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
.

We will now explain the behaviour of the CP-glued pre-stability conditions under the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action.

Lemma 3.28
.

Let
\sigma_{i}=(Z_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i})
be pre-stability conditions on
\mathcal{D}_{i}
for
i=1,2
and let
\sigma
be a CP-glued pre-stability condition from
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
on a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
with respect to the semiorthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{D}=\langle\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2}\rangle.
Let
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
then
\sigma g=(W,\mathcal{B})
satisfies the following conditions:
If
\sigma_{i}g=(W_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i}),
for
i=1,2
then

(1)

i_{1}\mathcal{B}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
and
i_{2}\mathcal{B}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{B}

(2)

{\left.\kern-1.2ptW\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{D}_{i}}}=W_{i},
for i=1,2.

Moreover, if
\Hom^{\leq 0}_{\mathcal{D}}(i_{1}\mathcal{B}_{1},i_{2}\mathcal{B}_{2})
then
\sigma g=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1}g,\sigma_{2}g)
.

Proof.

Let us consider the slicing
\mathcal{P}_{i}
of
\sigma_{i},
for
i=1,2,
and
\mathcal{P}
the slicing of
\sigma.
By definition of the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action we have
\mathcal{B}_{i}=\mathcal{P}_{i}(f(0),f(1)]
and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{P}(f(0),f(1)].
By the third part of Proposition
3.27
we obtain directly that
i_{1}\mathcal{B}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
and
i_{2}\mathcal{B}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{B}.

Next, recall that by definition we have that
W=T^{-1}\circ Z.
Let
E_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
for
i=1,2
. We note that
\lambda_{1}(E_{1})=E_{1}
and
\rho_{2}(E_{1})=0
(resp.
\lambda_{1}(E_{2})=0
and
\rho_{2}(E_{2})=E_{2}
) which implies
W([E_{i}])=T^{-1}\circ Z_{i}([E_{i}])=W_{i}
. Finally, if we also assume that
\Hom^{\leq 0}_{\mathcal{D}}(i_{1}\mathcal{B}_{1},i_{2}\mathcal{B}_{2})=0,
by the first part of Proposition
3.27
we obtain that
\sigma g=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1}g,\sigma_{2}g).

∎

It is important to remark that the gluing condition may not be preserved after applying the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action, as we can see in the following example.

Example 3.29
.

Consider the CP-glued pair
\sigma=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{\mu})
with
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{\mu}g
and
g=(N_{1},f)
in
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}).

Note that
f(0)=0
and that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)=\mathcal{P}_{\mu}(f(t)).

We have
1>t>f(t)
for all
t\in(0,1)
. Indeed, let
E\in\mathcal{P}_{\mu}(t)
be an object of phase
t\in(0,1)
. In particular, the rank
r
of
E
is strictly positive. Now, since
E\in\mathcal{P}_{\mu}(t)=\mathcal{P}_{1}(f^{-1}(t))
, together with the fact that
\Im Z_{1}(E)=\Im Z_{\mu}(E)=r
and
\Re Z_{1}(E)=-d+r>\Re Z_{\mu}(E)
, we have
0<f(t)<t<1
. The inequalities follow because
f
is strictly increasing.
Let us consider
g^{\prime}=(K_{\phi\pi},f_{\phi\pi})
for
\phi\in(0,1).
We will now study
\sigma_{1}^{\prime}=\sigma_{1}g^{\prime}

and
\sigma_{2}^{\prime}=\sigma_{\mu}g^{\prime},
which are also given by
(T^{\prime}_{i},f^{\prime}_{i})\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
for
i=1,2
respectively. Note that
f^{\prime}_{1}(0)=f\circ g^{\prime}(0)=f(\phi)
and
f^{\prime}_{2}(0)=g^{\prime}(0)=\phi.
Therefore, we obtain
f^{\prime}_{1}(0)<f^{\prime}_{2}(0)
and from Proposition
3.21
it follows that
\sigma^{\prime}_{1}
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{2}
do not satisfy gluing conditions with respect to the semiorthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle.
See Subsection
4.4
for the general picture.

Now that we have hearts in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
with the corresponding stability functions, we have to check that they satisfy the Harder–Narasimhan property and the support property. We begin with the Harder–Narasimhan property, arguing along the lines of
[
17
]
.

Theorem 3.30

(
[
17
, Theorem 3.6]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2})
be a semiorthogonal decomposition of a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
. Suppose
\sigma_{i}=(Z_{i},\mathcal{P}_{i})
is a stability condition on
\mathcal{D}_{i}
for
i=1,2
and let
a\in(0,1)
be a real number. Assume the following conditions hold:

(1)

\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}^{\leq 0}(i_{1}\mathcal{P}_{1}(0,1],i_{2}\mathcal{P}_{2}(0,1])=0

(2)

\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}^{\leq 0}(i_{1}\mathcal{P}_{1}(a,a+1],i_{2}\mathcal{P}_{2}(a,a+1])=0
.

Then, there exists a locally finite pre-stability condition
\sigma
glued from
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
.

Definition 3.31
.

For a real number
a\in(0,1)
, we define the subset
S(a)
as the subset of pairs of stability conditions
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in\Stab(\mathcal{D}_{1})\times\Stab(\mathcal{D}_{2})
satisfying the conditions in Theorem
3.30
.

Theorem 3.32

(
[
17
, Theorem 4.3]
)
.

Let
\textnormal{gl}\colon S(a)\rightarrow\Stab(\mathcal{D})
be the map associating to
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in\Stab(\mathcal{D}_{1})\times\Stab(\mathcal{D}_{2})
the corresponding glued pre-stability condition
\sigma
on
\mathcal{D}
(defined by Theorem
3.30
). Then, the map
\rm{gl}
is continuous on
S(a)
.

For
a\in(0,1)
, we have a precise description of the sets
S(a)
for the semiorthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle
.

Proposition 3.33
.

Consider the semiorthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle
. For
a\in(0,1)
, we have
S(a)\cong\left\{((T_{1},f_{1}),(T_{2},f_{2}))\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})\colon f_{1}(0)\geq f_{2}(0)\textnormal{ and }f_{1}(a)\geq f_{2}(a)\right\}.

Proof.

Suppose
\sigma_{i}=(Z_{i},\coh_{i}^{\theta_{i}}(C)[n_{i}])
is a stability condition on
\mathcal{C}_{i}
with
\theta_{i}\in[0,1)
and
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
, for
i=1,2
. Assume that these stability conditions satisfy the gluing condition, i.e.
n_{1}+\theta_{1}\geq n_{2}+\theta_{2}
. Let
(T_{i},f_{i})
be the elements in
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
corresponding to
\sigma_{i}
under the equivalence in Theorem
2.49
(for
i=1,2
). Note that
f_{i}(0)=n_{i}+\theta_{i}
(for
i=1,2
) so the condition 1 in Theorem
3.30
is equivalent to
f_{1}(0)\geq f_{2}(0)
. We end the proof by showing that condition 2 is equivalent to
f_{1}(a)\geq f_{2}(a)
. Indeed, we will have

\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}^{\leq 0}(i_{*}\mathcal{P}_{1}(a,a+1],j_{*}\mathcal{P}_{2}(a,a+1])=0

if and only if the stability condition
\sigma^{\prime}
obtained from
\sigma
, acting by rotation of angle
a
satisfies the gluing property. Hence, if we denote
\mathcal{P}(0,1]=\Coh(C)
the standard heart associated to slope-stability
Z_{\mu}
, then
\mathcal{P}_{i}(a,a+1]=\mathcal{P}(f_{i}(a),f_{i}(a)+1]=\coh^{f_{i}(a)}(C),
for
i=1,2
and they will satisfy the gluing condition if and only if
f_{1}(a)\geq f_{2}(a)
.
∎

Example 3.34
.

Consider the semiorthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle
. Let
\sigma_{i}=(Z_{i},\coh_{i}^{r_{i}}(C))
be a stability conditions on
\mathcal{C}_{i}
with
r_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, for
i=1,2
.

(1)

If
r_{1}>r_{2}
, then
\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
is a locally finite pre-stability condition on
\mathcal{T}_{C}

(2)

The CP-glued pair
\sigma=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{\mu})
with
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{\mu}g
and
g=(N_{1},f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
, that we considered in Example
3.29
, does not satisfy the conditions in Theorem
3.30
.

See
[
35
, Example 2.9.11]
for more details on these and more examples.

For small hearts that fulfill the condition 1 in Theorem
3.30
but not 2, we may need a different strategy.

Proposition 3.35

(HN-property for
\mathbb{Q}
)
.

Let

\glu_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=(Z_{12}=Z_{1}\circ i^{*}+Z_{2}\circ j^{!},\mathcal{A}_{12}=\glu_{12}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}))

be a CP-glued pair in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
obtained by CP-gluing the stability conditions
\sigma_{i}=(Z_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i})
on
\mathcal{C}_{i}
with
\mathcal{A}_{i}=\Coh_{i}^{\theta_{i}}(C)
for
\theta_{i}\in[0,1)
for
i=1,2
.
If
\tan(\pi\theta_{i})\in\mathbb{Q}
, for all
i
, then
Z_{12}
has the HN-property.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
0
is an isolated point of
\Im Z_{i}(\mathcal{A}_{i})\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for
i=1,2
. Then, it follows from
[
17
, Proposition 3.5]
that
Z_{12}
has the HN-property on
\mathcal{A}_{12}
.
∎

Corollary 3.36
.

Let

\glu_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=(Z_{12}=Z_{1}\circ i^{*}+Z_{2}\circ j^{!},\mathcal{A}_{12}=\glu_{12}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}))

be a CP-glued pair in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
obtained by CP-gluing the stability conditions
\sigma_{i}=(Z_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i})
on
\mathcal{C}_{i}
with
\mathcal{A}_{i}=\Coh_{i}^{\theta_{i}}(C)
for
\theta_{i}\in[0,1)
for
i=1,2
, such that
\theta_{1}\geq\theta_{2}
but it does not belong to
S(a)
for any
a\in(0,1)
. If
\tan(\pi\theta_{i})\in\mathbb{Q}
, for all
i
, then
\glu_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
is a pre-stability condition.

Example 3.37

(classic
\alpha
-stability as CP-gluing)
.

Note that by
[
18
, Theorem 5.3.11]
, pre-stability conditions on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
with heart
\TCoh(C)
are obtained as CP-gluing of two stability conditions on
D^{b}(C)
with heart
\Coh(C)
and the stability function is given as

Z(r_{1},d_{1},r_{2},d_{2})\coloneqq-A_{1}d_{1}-A_{2}d_{2}+B_{1}r_{1}+B_{2}r_{2}+i(C_{1}r_{1}+C_{2}r_{2})

where
A_{i},B_{i},C_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
A_{i},C_{i}>0
, for
i=1,2
. In particular, we can recover the classic notion of
\alpha
-stability for holomorphic triples of vector bundles of García-Prada et al. in
[
21
]
and
[
8
]
by taking
\sigma_{\alpha}=(Z_{\alpha},\TCoh(C))
where
Z_{\alpha}(r_{1},d_{1},r_{2},d_{2})\coloneqq-d_{1}-d_{2}-\alpha r_{1}+i(r_{1}+r_{2})
with
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}.

Remark 3.38
.

There is a way to construct hearts from semiorthogonal decompositions which agrees with CP-gluing when the gluing condition (
3.5
) is satisfied. This is the well-known recollement introduced in
[
6
]
. See Appendix A. and Lemma
A.5
for examples of hearts that do not accept a stability function.

3.2.2.
Constructing pre-stability conditions via tilting

In this section we construct pre-stability conditions in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
whose hearts are not given by Proposition
3.18
. We follow the steps of
[
12
, Lemma 6.1]
, i.e. we use weak stability functions on
\TCoh(C)
to obtain torsion pairs on
\TCoh(C)
via truncation of the HN-filtrations. After tilting in the sense of
[
24
, Proposition 2.1]
, we obtain hearts that admit Bridgeland stability functions.

Remark 3.39
.

The intuition of this construction comes from Proposition
4.18
. This proposition gives us a description of torsion pairs of
\TCoh(C),
which after tilting will give us a heart of a pre-stability condition.

We define the following homomorphism:

(3.9)

\displaystyle Z\colon\mathbb{Z}^{4}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle(r_{1},d_{1},r_{2},d_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle D_{1}d_{1}+(C_{1}-1)r_{1}+i(r_{1}+r_{2}),

where
D_{1},C_{1}\in\mathbb{Q},
and
D_{1}<0.
For every
E\in\TCoh(C)
with
E\neq j_{*}(T),
where
T
is a torsion sheaf, we define the phase of
E
as
\lambda(E)=(1/\pi)\arg(Z([E]))\in(0,1].

Remark 3.40
.

Let
0\rightarrow A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C\rightarrow 0
be a short exact sequence where
A,B,C\in\TCoh(C)
and
A,B,C\neq 0\rightarrow T,
where
T
is a torsion sheaf, then

\lambda(A)<\lambda(B)\iff\lambda(B)<\lambda(C)\textnormal{ and }\lambda(A)>\lambda(B)\iff\lambda(B)>\lambda(C).

For a triple
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\TCoh(C),
let
T(E_{i})
be the torsion part and
F(E_{i})
be the torsion-free part of of
E_{i}
for
i=1,2.
By the functoriality of the torsion part, we obtain
T(E)=T(E_{1})\rightarrow T(E_{2})
and the following short exact sequence

(3.10)

0\rightarrow T(E)\rightarrow E\rightarrow F(E)\rightarrow 0,

where
F(E)=F(E_{1})\rightarrow F(E_{2})=E/T(E).

A triple
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\TCoh(C)
is called
torsion-free
if
T(E_{i})=0,
for
i=1,2.
We define the
torsion-free triple
of
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\textnormal{ as }F(E)=F(E_{1})\xrightarrow{f}F(E_{2}).

Definition 3.41
.

A torsion-free triple
E\in\TCoh(C)
is called
\lambda
-semistable if for all non-zero subobjects
F\subseteq E
we have
\lambda(F)\leq\lambda(E).

We will now show that the
\lambda
-semistable objects admit HN-filtrations. The proof goes along the lines of the classical proof for
\mu
-stability on
curves
.

Lemma 3.42

(HN-filtration for
\lambda
-stability.)
.

Let
F=F_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}F_{2}\in\TCoh(C)
be a torsion-free object, then there is a unique Harder–Narasimhan filtration i.e. there is an increasing filtration
0\subseteq E_{1}\cdots\subseteq E_{n-1}\subseteq E_{n}=F
where
G_{i}=E_{i}/E_{i-1}
is
\lambda
-semistable for each
i=0,\ldots,n
and
\lambda(G_{1})>\cdots>\lambda(G_{n-1})>\lambda(G_{n}).

Moreover, this filtration is unique.

Proof 1
.

If
F
is
\lambda
-semistable, there is nothing to prove. Let us consider the subobjects
E
of
F.
Note that
E
is also torsion-free. Take the object
E_{1}
with maximal
\lambda(E_{1})
among all the subobjects of
F
and with maximal imaginary part among all the subobjects of
F
with maximal
\lambda
-phase. This object exists because the phase is bounded. Indeed, by the correspondence between slope and phase, it follows from the fact that
-D>0
and by Riemann–Roch.
As a consequence, the subobject
E_{1}
is necessarily
\lambda
-semistable and
F/E_{1}
is torsion free. We also have that for all
E
with
0\neq E/E_{1}\subseteq F/E_{1},
we get
\lambda(E/E_{1})<\lambda(E_{1}).
We now apply the same construction to
F/E_{1}.
We get the desired filtration.

Lemma 3.43
.

Let
\phi=3/4.
There is a torsion pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
on the category
\TCoh(C)
defined as follows:
E\in\mathcal{T}
if the Harder–Narasimhan
\lambda
-semistable factors
A_{i}
of
F(E)
satisfy
\lambda(A_{i})>\phi
and
i^{!}(E)\in\Coh(C).
We say that
E\in\mathcal{F}
if either
i^{*}(E)
is torsion-free and the Harder–Narasimhan factors
A_{i}
of
F(E)
satisfy
\lambda(A_{i})\leq\phi
or
i^{*}(E)=0.

Proof 2
.

Note that if
E\in\mathcal{T},
by our definition of
\mathcal{T}
and the correspondence between slope and phase, we have that
F(E)
satisfies

(3.11)

\frac{-D_{1}d_{1}-(C_{1}-1)r_{1}}{r_{1}+r_{2}}>-\cot(3\pi/4)\textnormal{ i.e.\ }-D_{1}d_{1}-C_{1}r_{1}-r_{2}>0.

where here
d_{i}=\deg(F(E_{i}))
and
r_{i}=\rank(F(E_{i})),
for
i=1
,
2.

We show that
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
is a torsion pair of
\TCoh(C).
First, we prove that
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})=0.
By our definition of stability we have that
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,F)=0,
for all objects
E\in\mathcal{T}
and
F\in\mathcal{F}
that are torsion-free.

Let
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
and
G=G_{1}\rightarrow G_{2}\in\mathcal{F}.
Let us consider the following short exact sequences as in the triangle (
3.10
)

0\rightarrow T(E)\rightarrow E\rightarrow F(E)\rightarrow 0\textnormal{ and }0\rightarrow T(G)\rightarrow G\rightarrow F(G)\rightarrow 0.

By definition of
\mathcal{F}
, we have that
F(G)\in\mathcal{F}
and as
i^{*}(G)
is torsion-free, we get
i^{*}(T(G))=0
and
T(G)\in\mathcal{F}.
Then it is enough to show
\Hom_{\TCoh(C)}(E,G)=0
for
G=0\rightarrow H,
for any
H\in\Coh(C)
and
G=G_{1}\rightarrow G_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
where
G_{1},G_{2}
are torsion-free.

Case 1:

G\in\mathcal{F}
a torsion-free triple.

By definition
F(E)\in\mathcal{T}
and by stability we have
\Hom_{\TCoh(C)}(F(E),G)=0.
Also
\Hom_{\TCoh(C)}(T(E),G)=0
as
G
is torsion-free.
Therefore, it follows that
\Hom_{\TCoh(C)}(E,G)=0.

Case 2:

G=0\rightarrow H.
We have
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,G)=\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,j_{*}(j^{!}(G))),
by adjointness

\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,j_{*}(j^{!}(G)))=\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}(j^{*}(E),j^{!}(G))=\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}(\Ker(\varphi)[1],H)=0,

because
j^{*}(E)[1]=i^{!}(E)=\Ker(\varphi)\oplus\Coker(\varphi)[1]
is in
\Coh(C),
which implies that
\Coker(\varphi)=0.

Note that for a torsion-free object
E,
by Lemma
3.42
, there is a short exact sequence
0\rightarrow T\rightarrow E\rightarrow F\rightarrow 0,
with
T
torsion-free such that the HN-factors
A_{i}
of
T
satisfy that
\lambda(A_{i})>\frac{3}{4}
and
F=F_{1}\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}F_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
also torsion-free. Note that
T=T_{1}\xrightarrow{t^{\prime}}T_{2}
is not necessarily surjective, however we have the following claim.

Claim 3.44
.

Either
\Coker(t^{\prime})=0
or
\Coker(t^{\prime})
is a torsion sheaf.

Proof 3
.

Because of Lemma
3.42
, it is enough to show the statement for a
\lambda
-semistable object
T
satisfying
\lambda(T)>\frac{3}{4}.

Let us assume that
\Coker(t^{\prime})\neq 0.
Note that
t^{\prime}\neq 0.

Then
\Img(t^{\prime}),\Coker(t^{\prime})\neq 0,
and as a consequence, we have

(3.12)

\textstyle{T_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{T_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{T_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Coker(t^{\prime})}
and
\textstyle{T_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{T_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\Img(t^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{2}.}

If
\rk(\Coker(t^{\prime}))>0,
then by
\lambda
-semistability of
T,
we obtain
\lambda(T)=\frac{1}{2}<\frac{3}{4},
which gives us a contradiction.

We obtain the following short exact sequence

(3.13)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{F_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Img(t^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{2}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Note that
T^{\prime}\coloneqq T_{1}\xrightarrow{t^{\prime}}\Img(t^{\prime})
is in
\mathcal{T}.
Indeed, let us consider the last short exact sequence in its HN-decomposition
0\rightarrow E\rightarrow T^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A\rightarrow 0
with
A=A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}
a
\lambda
-semistable torsion free sheaf. We want to show that
\lambda(A)>\frac{3}{4}.

Note that
E
is also a subobject of
T,
as a consequence we consider the short exact sequence
0\rightarrow E\rightarrow T\rightarrow T/E\rightarrow 0.
We have that
T/E
is a quotient of
T
and therefore
\lambda(F(T/E))>\frac{3}{4}.
We also have that
\frac{3}{4}<\lambda(F(T/E))=\lambda(A).
Moreover, we get that
F
is the torsion-free part of
F_{1}\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}F_{2}^{\prime}
and this implies that
F_{1}\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}F_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}.
Therefore, if
E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}
is torsion-free, the triangle (
3.13
) gives us the decomposition of
E
in
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}).

Let
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\TCoh(C).
Let us, again, consider the short exact sequence

(3.14)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T(E_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{F(E_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
