\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T(E_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(E_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since
F(E)
is torsion-free, as mentioned before there is a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow T^{{}^{\prime}}\rightarrow F(E)\rightarrow F^{\prime}\rightarrow 0,

with
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
and
F^{{}^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{F}.
Explicitly

(3.15)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{\prime}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l_{1}}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{F(E_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{\prime}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l_{2}}
\textstyle{F(E_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{2}}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

After choosing a splitting
E_{i}=F(E_{i})\oplus T(E_{i}),
for
i=1,2,
we have a morphism

l\colon F(E_{1})\hookrightarrow E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\rightarrow T(E_{2}).

We now define the following morphism:

\beta_{1}\colon T^{{}^{\prime}}_{1}\oplus T(E_{1})\xrightarrow{\begin{bmatrix}l_{1}&0\\
0&\id\\
\end{bmatrix}}F(E_{1})\oplus T(E_{1}).

We obtain the following short exact sequence:

(3.16)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{1}^{\prime}\oplus T(E_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi\circ\beta_{1}}
\textstyle{F(E_{1})\oplus T(E_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(g_{1},0)}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{F^{{}^{\prime}}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l^{\prime}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Img(\varphi\circ\beta_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{F(E_{2})\oplus T(E_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Note that
\varphi(x,y)=(f(x),l(x)+t(y)),
for
(x,y)\in F(E_{1})\oplus T(E_{1}).

We claim that
T^{\prime}_{1}\oplus T(E_{1})\xrightarrow{\varphi\circ\beta_{1}}\Img(\varphi\circ\beta_{1})\in\mathcal{T}\textnormal{ and }F_{1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}G\in\mathcal{F}.

Indeed, note that we have the following decomposition:

(3.17)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T(E_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{T^{\prime}_{1}\oplus T(E_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi\circ\beta_{1}}
\textstyle{T^{\prime}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Ker(\pi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Img(\varphi\circ\beta_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{T^{\prime}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Note that, as seen from triangle (
3.15
),
we have that
\pi\colon\Img(\varphi\circ\beta_{1})\subseteq T^{\prime}_{2}\oplus T(E_{2})\rightarrow T^{\prime}_{2}
is just the projection. Note also that we abused the notation by ignoring the inclusion
l_{2}.

As
\Ker(\pi)=\Img(\varphi\circ\beta_{1}\mid_{\Ker{t^{\prime}}\oplus T(E_{1})})
is given by the points
(0,x)\in\Img(\varphi\circ\beta_{1})\subseteq T^{\prime}_{2}\oplus T(E_{2})
and we can see it as a subset of
\subseteq T(E)_{2}.
Thus,
\Ker(\pi)
is a torsion sheaf and by definition
T^{\prime}_{2}
is torsion-free. As a consequence, the torsion-free part of
T^{\prime}_{1}\oplus T(E_{1})\xrightarrow{\varphi\circ\beta_{1}}\Img(\varphi\circ\beta_{1})\in\mathcal{T}
is
T^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{t^{\prime}}T^{\prime}_{2}.

Analogously, we have the following decomposition:

(3.18)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l^{\prime}}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Ker([g_{2},0])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[g\circ g_{2},0]}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

We first check that
[g_{2},0]
is well-defined. As
G=F(E_{2})\oplus T(E_{2})/\Img(\varphi\circ\beta_{1}),
then if
(x,y)
in
\Img(\varphi\circ\beta_{1}),
there are
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in T^{\prime}_{1}\oplus T(E_{1}),
which satisfy
(f(x^{\prime}),l(x^{\prime})+t(y^{\prime}))=(x,y).
Note that
f(x^{\prime})\in T^{\prime}_{2},
therefore
g_{2}(f(x^{\prime}))=0.
Since
g_{2}
is surjective, we have that
[g_{2},0]
is clearly surjective.

Note that
\Ker([g\circ g_{2},0])=T_{2}\oplus T(E_{2})/\Img(\varphi\circ\beta_{1}).
As a consequence,
\Ker([g_{2}])
is a torsion sheaf.
We now obtain that the torsion-free part of
F^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}G
is the same as the one of
F^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow F^{\prime}_{2}
and this implies that

F^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}G\in\mathcal{F}.

After tilting
\TCoh(C)
with respect to the torsion pair of Lemma
3.43
as in
[
24
, Proposition 2.1]
, we obtain the heart

\mathcal{A}_{r}=\{E\in\mathcal{T}_{C}\mid H^{i}(E)=0\textnormal{ for }i\neq 1,0,H^{1}(E)\in\mathcal{T}\textnormal{ and }H^{0}(E)\in\mathcal{F}\},

where
r=\frac{\arg(C_{1}+D_{1}i)}{\pi}\in(-1,0].
It has a corresponding torsion pair
\mathcal{A}_{r}=(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{T}[-1]).

We will now define a stability function
Z_{r}
on
\mathcal{A}_{r}
such that the pair
(Z_{r},\mathcal{A}_{r})
is a Bridgeland stability condition.

Remark 3.45
.

If we have
\sigma=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
a pre-stability condition and we act by
\sigma_{2}^{-1}=g_{2}
in
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
and obtain a non CP-glued pre-stability condition, then the heart of
\sigma g_{2}
is going to be given by
\mathcal{A}_{r}
(see Subsection
4.1
).

We now define

(3.19)

\displaystyle\noindent Z_{r}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{4}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle(r_{1},d_{1},r_{2},d_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle A_{1}d_{1}+B_{1}r_{1}-d_{2}+i(D_{1}d_{1}+r_{1}C_{1}+r_{2}),

where

M=\begin{bmatrix}-A_{1}&B_{1}\\
-D_{1}&C_{1}\\
\end{bmatrix}

with
\det(M)>0,

\det(M+I)>0,

A_{1},B_{1}\in\mathbb{R},

C_{1},D_{1}\in\mathbb{Q}
and
D_{1}<0.

Remark 3.46
.

Note that if
E\in\TCoh(C)
is a
\lambda
-semistable torsion-free triple, then
\lambda(E)>\frac{3}{4}
if and only if

\Im(Z_{r}(E))<0.
Moreover,
\lambda(E)\leq\frac{3}{4}
if and only if
\Im(Z_{r}(E))\geq 0.

If we consider the same
C_{1},D_{1}
as above for the construction of
\mathcal{A}_{r},
we obtain the following lemma.

Lemma 3.47
.

The group homomorphism
Z_{r}
is a stability function on
\mathcal{A}_{r}

Proof 4
.

First of all, we show that the image of
E\in\mathcal{A}_{r}
under
Z_{r}
lies in
\mathbb{H}\cup\mathbb{R}_{<0}.
Let
E\in\mathcal{T},
then we consider the short exact sequence
0\rightarrow T(E)\rightarrow E\rightarrow F(E)\rightarrow 0.
Note that by definition
F(E)\in\mathcal{T}
and the right exactness of
\Coker(-)
we obtain that
i^{!}(F(E))\in\Coh(C).

We prove now that
Z_{r}(E[-1])\in\mathbb{H}\cup\mathbb{R}_{<0}.

We first show that
Z_{r}(F(E)[-1])\in\mathbb{H}\cup\mathbb{R}_{<0}.
It is enough to assume that
F(E)
is
\lambda
-semistable and this follows directly from Remark
3.46
.

As
\rk(T(E_{1}))=\rk(T(E_{2}))=0,
if
T(E_{1})\neq 0,
then
\deg(T(E_{1}))\geq 0
and
\Im(Z_{r}((T(E)[-1]))
is precisely
-\deg(T(E_{1}))D_{1}>0.

If
\deg(T(E_{1}))=0,
then
T(E_{1})=0,
and
F(E_{1})\cong E_{1}.
This implies
\Im(Z_{r}(F(E)[-1]))=\Im Z_{r}(E[-1])>0.

Since
Z
is additive with respect to short exact sequences, we obtain that
Z(E[-1])\in\mathbb{H}\cup\mathbb{R}_{<0}.

We will now show that if
E\in\mathcal{F},
then
Z_{r}(E)\in\mathbb{H}\cup\mathbb{R}_{<0}.
Once again we consider the short exact sequence
0\rightarrow T(E)\rightarrow E\rightarrow F(E)\rightarrow 0.
As
i^{*}(E)
is a torsion-free sheaf
T(E)=0\rightarrow T(E_{2}),
where
T(E_{2})
is a torsion sheaf. Note that by definition
T(E)\in\mathcal{F}
and
F(E)\in\mathcal{F}.

Clearly
Z_{r}(T(E))=-\deg(T(E_{2}))<0,
as
T(E_{2})
is a torsion-sheaf.

Let
F(E)\in\mathcal{F}
be a torsion-free
\lambda-
semistable object. If
F(E)
satisfies
\lambda(F(E))<3/4,
then
-D_{1}d_{1}-C_{1}r_{1}-r_{2}<0
and
Z(E)
lies in the upper-half plane.

We now assume that
F(E)=F(E_{1})\xrightarrow{f}F(E_{2})
in
\mathcal{F}
is a torsion-free object with
D_{1}d_{1}+r_{1}C_{1}+r_{2}=0.
It suffices to show our statement for
F(E)
a
\lambda
-semistable object. We will now prove that
A_{1}d_{1}+B_{1}r_{1}-d_{2}<0.

First note that if
F(E_{1})=0,
then
0=D_{1}d_{1}+r_{1}C_{1}+r_{2}=r_{2}
and this implies
F(E)=0.
If
F(E_{2})=0,
then
D_{1}d_{1}+r_{1}C_{1}=0,
and
A_{1}d_{1}+B_{1}r_{1}-d_{2}=A_{1}d_{1}+B_{1}r_{1}<0,
because
\det(M)>0.
Therefore, we assume
F(E_{1})
and
F(E_{2})\neq 0.
Moreover, note that
\rk(\Coker(f))=0.
Indeed, otherwise
j_{*}(\Coker(f))
would be a quotient of
F(E)
with slope
\frac{1}{2},
which gives us a contradiction.

Note that
\lambda(F(E_{1})\rightarrow\Img(f))=\lambda(F(E)),
and moreover

[F(E)]=[F(E_{1})\rightarrow\Img(f)]+(0,0,0,d_{2}^{\prime\prime}),
where
d_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\deg(\Coker(f))>0,

d^{\prime\prime}_{1}=\deg(\Img(f))
and
d_{2}=\deg(F(E_{2})).

As

A_{1}d_{1}+B_{1}r_{1}-d_{2}=A_{1}d_{1}+B_{1}r_{1}-d^{\prime\prime}_{1}-d_{2}^{\prime\prime}<A_{1}d_{1}+B_{1}r_{1}-d^{\prime\prime}_{1}

and
F(E_{1})\rightarrow\Img(f)\in\mathcal{F},
since it is a subobject of
F(E),
it is enough to show our statement for torsion-free objects
F(E_{1})\xrightarrow{f}F(E_{2})\in\mathcal{F}
with
\Coker(f)=0.
As a consequence, we assume
r_{1}=\rk(F(E_{1}))\geq\rk(F(E_{2}))=r_{2}.

Since
K=i_{*}(i^{!}(F(E)))=\Ker(f)\rightarrow 0
is a subobject of
F(E)
in
\TCoh(C)
and
\mathcal{F}
is closed under subobjects, we obtain that
K\in\mathcal{F}.
Since
[i_{*}(i^{!}(E))]=[K]=(r_{1}-r_{2},d_{1}-d_{2},0,0),
where
d_{i}=\deg(F(E_{i}))
for
i=1,2,
it follows that
-D_{1}(d_{1}-d_{2})-C_{1}(r_{1}-r_{2})\leq 0.
By hypothesis
D_{1}d_{1}+r_{1}C_{1}+r_{2}=0.
Therefore we obtain
D_{1}d_{2}\leq-(C_{1}+1)r_{2}.

Now we want to show that
A_{1}d_{1}+B_{1}r_{1}-d_{2}<0.
First note that
d_{1}=\frac{r_{2}+C_{1}r_{1}}{-D_{1}},
we obtain

\displaystyle A_{1}d_{1}+B_{1}r_{1}-d_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{-D_{1}})(A_{1}r_{2}+r_{1}A_{1}C_{1}-r_{1}B_{1}D_{1}+D_{1}d_{2}).

Since
-D_{1}>0,
it is enough to show that
A_{1}r_{2}+r_{1}A_{1}C_{1}-r_{1}B_{1}D_{1}+D_{1}d_{2}<0.
We have

(3.20)

\displaystyle A_{1}r_{2}+r_{1}A_{1}C_{1}-r_{1}B_{1}D_{1}+D_{1}d_{2}

\displaystyle{\leq}

\displaystyle r_{2}(A_{1}-C_{1}-1)-r_{1}(\det(M))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle r_{2}(A_{1}-C_{1}-1)-r_{2}(\det(M))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{2}(-\det(M+I))<0,

as
-r_{1}\leq-r_{2}
and also
\det(M)>0
and
\det(M+I)>0.
Since
Z_{r}
is additive with respect to short exact sequences we obtain that
Z_{r}(E)\in\mathbb{H}\cup\mathbb{R}_{<0}
for
E\in\mathcal{F}.

Lemma 3.48
.

If
A_{1},B_{1},C_{1},D_{1}\in\mathbb{Q},
the pair
(Z_{r},\mathcal{A}_{r})
is a pre-stability condition.

Proof 5
.

We follow the steps of
[
12
, Proposition 7.1]
.
First note that by
[
3
, Proposition B.2]
, it is enough to show that if
E\in\mathcal{A}_{r}
and
0\subset L_{1}\subset L_{2}\ldots\subset L_{i}\subset\ldots\subset E,
where
L_{i}
belongs to the full subcategory
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(1)
of objects with phase one, the sequence stabilizes.
As
L_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{r}=(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{T}[-1]),
we consider the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow F_{i}\rightarrow L_{i}\rightarrow T_{i}[-1]\rightarrow 0,
where
F_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
and
T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}.
As
\Im Z_{r}(L_{i})=0,
we obtain that
\Im Z_{r}(T_{i}[-1])=0.
Note that this implies that
T_{i}=0.
Therefore, we obtain that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(1)\subseteq\mathcal{F}\subseteq\TCoh(C),
as
\TCoh(C)
is noetherian, our result follows.

Remark 3.49
.

(1)

By Lemma
2.37
the pre-stability condition
\sigma_{r}=(Z_{r},\mathcal{A}_{r})
is locally finite.

(2)

Let
\sigma=(Z_{r},\mathcal{A}_{r})
be a pre-stability condition constructed in Lemma
3.48
. Note that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1],

l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1]
and
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are in
\mathcal{A}_{r}
and
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is stable of phase one.

(3)

We consider the torsion pair
(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})=\Coh(C),
which is given by truncating the Harder–Narasimhan filtration with respect to
\sigma_{1}=(Z(r,d)=D_{1}d+(C_{1}-1)r+ir,\Coh(C))
at
\frac{3}{4}
in
\Stab(C).

Lemma 3.50
.

We have that
\Coh_{2}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{r}\textnormal{ and }\Coh_{1}^{r}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{r}\textnormal{ and }\Coh_{3}^{r_{3}}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{r},

where
r_{3}=\frac{\textnormal{acot}(\frac{C_{1}+1}{D_{1}})}{\pi}.

Proof 6
.

Note that
\Coh_{2}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{r}.

We consider the torsion pair
\Coh(C)=(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})
of Remark
3.49
, which also induces the heart
\Coh^{r}_{1}(C)
after a right tilt, i.e.
\Coh^{r}_{1}(C)=(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}[-1]).

Let
E\in\Coh_{1}(C)
be a
\mu
-semistable object. If
E=i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),
then by Remark
3.49
, we have
E[-1]\in\mathcal{A}_{r}.
We assume that
E
is torsion free. As the only possible subobjects or quotients of
E
are in
\Coh_{1}(C),
we have that
E
is
\mu
-semistable if and only if it is
\lambda
-semistable. It follows directly from the definition of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{F}
that

i_{*}(\mathcal{T}_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{T}\textnormal{ and }i_{*}(\mathcal{F}_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{F}.

We also consider a torsion pair
\Coh(C)=(\mathcal{T}_{3},\mathcal{F}_{3}),
as in Remark
2.52
, such that after taking the right tilt,
\Coh^{r_{3}}(C)=(\mathcal{F}_{3},\mathcal{T}_{3}[-1]).

Let
E\in\Coh_{3}(C)
be a
\mu
-semistable object. We have that
E=l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))\in\mathcal{A}_{r}
by Remark
3.49
. It is easy to see by computing the slope of the Harder–Narasimhan factors that
l_{*}(\mathcal{T}_{3})\subseteq\mathcal{T}
and
l_{*}(\mathcal{F}_{3})\subseteq\mathcal{F}.

4.
The stability manifold
\Stab(\mathcal{T}_{C})

Lemma
2.47
is the main tool to prove that
\mathbb{C}(x)
is a
\sigma
-stable object for every
\sigma\in\Stab(C)
and every
x\in C.
In this section, we prove the analogous statement for
\mathcal{T}_{C},
to give a characterization of every
\sigma\in\Stab(\mathcal{T}_{C})
in terms of the skyscraper sheaves. In Subsection
4.1
, we follow closely the steps of
[
12
, Lemma 10.1]
to describe the possible hearts appearing in pre-stability conditions on
\mathcal{T}_{C}.
We finally prove that every pre-stability condition on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
has to be given by one of the already constructed pairs in Lemma
3.48
or Subsection
3.2.2
i.e. either by CP-gluing or by tilting.

Remark 4.1
.

Due to the assumption that
g(C)\geq 1,
if
\Hom_{\Coh(C)}(E,A)\neq 0,
then

\Hom_{\Coh(C)}(E,A\otimes\omega_{C})\neq 0.
If
f
is a non-zero morphism in
\Hom_{\Coh(C)}(E,A),
we denote by
f_{C}
the non-zero morphism in
\Hom_{\Coh(C)}(E,A\otimes\omega_{C})
associated to
f.

Lemma 4.2
.

If there is a distinguished triangle in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
of the form

(4.1)

\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{E}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{A}}
\textstyle{E_{1}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{E}[1]}
\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l}
\textstyle{E_{2}[1]}

with
X\in\Coh(C)
and
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{\leq 0}(E,A)=0,
then
E_{1},A_{1}\in\Coh(C)
.

Proof 7
.

First of all note that
l_{2}\coloneqq j^{!}(l)
is an isomorphism in
D^{b}(C).

We now consider the functor

F\colon\mathcal{T}_{C}\rightarrow\Mor(D^{b}(C))
defined as
F(E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{E}}E_{2})=E_{1}\xrightarrow{[\varphi_{E}]}E_{2}
at the level of objects. For
\Phi
in
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,F),
by
3.6
, we get the commutative square

(4.2)

\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{*}(\Phi)}
\scriptstyle{[\varphi_{E}]}
\textstyle{F_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\varphi_{F}]}
\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{!}(\Phi)}
\textstyle{F_{2}}

in
D^{b}(C),
which is in fact a morphism in
\Mor(D^{b}(C)).

Claim 4.3
.

The functor
F\colon\mathcal{T}_{C}\rightarrow\Mor(D^{b}(C))
is full.

Proof.

Let
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Hom_{\Mor(D^{b}(C))}(F(E),F(G)),
with
E,G\in\mathcal{T}_{C}.
By definition we have the commutative diagram

(4.3)

\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{[\varphi_{E}]}
\textstyle{G_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\varphi_{G}]}
\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{2}}
\textstyle{G_{2}.}

Note that we obtain a commutative square in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
given by

(4.4)

\textstyle{i_{*}(E_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{1}}
\textstyle{j_{*}(E_{2})[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{2}}
\textstyle{i_{*}(G_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{F}}
\textstyle{j_{*}(G_{2})[1].}

By taking cones of the horizontal arrows and using Remark
3.12
, we obtain that there is a morphism
\Phi\in\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,F)
with
F(\Phi)=(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}).

∎

By
[
27
, Corollary 3.15]
, for every
G\in D^{b}(C),
we have that
G=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}G_{i}[-i]
with
G_{i}=H^{i}(G)\in\Coh(C).
We also have the canonical morphisms (which come from the genuine chain maps)

G^{\alpha}[-\alpha]\xrightarrow{i_{\alpha}}G\xrightarrow{\pi_{\beta}}G^{\beta}[-\beta],

for
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}.
Moreover, note that if we have a morphism
\psi\colon G_{1}\rightarrow G_{2}
in
D^{b}(C)
, then
H^{i}(\psi)[-i]=\pi^{2}_{i}\circ\psi\circ i^{1}_{i}\colon G^{i}_{1}[-i]\rightarrow G_{2}^{i}[-i].

For
G=G_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{G}}G_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{C}

we get that
F(G)\in\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}(\oplus_{i}G_{1}^{i}[-i],\oplus_{i}G_{2}^{i}[-i]),
with
G_{j}^{i}\in\Coh(C),
for
j=1,2
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}.
Due to the fact that
\Coh(C)
has homological dimension one, there is a non-zero morphism
{t_{G}}^{i}
from
G^{i}_{1}[-i]\rightarrow G_{2}^{i}[-i]\oplus G_{2}^{i-1}[-i+1]
to
G_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{G}}G_{2}
in
\Mor(D^{b}(C)).
We construct the morphism

\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}\circ[\varphi_{A}]}
\scriptstyle{[\varphi_{A}]}
\textstyle{A_{2}^{i}[-i]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id_{C}[-i]}
\scriptstyle{\id}
\textstyle{A^{i}_{2}[-i]\otimes{\omega_{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l[-i]\otimes\omega_{C}}
\scriptstyle{l[-i]\otimes\omega_{C}}
\textstyle{E^{i+1}_{2}[-i]\otimes\omega_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{i}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E)}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces E_{2}\otimes\omega_{C}[1]}
\scriptstyle{[i_{E}]}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}
\textstyle{A_{2}^{i}[-i]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{i+1}_{2}[-i]\otimes\omega_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}(\varphi_{E})^{i+1}[-i]\oplus\mathcal{C}(\varphi_{E})^{i}[-i+1]\otimes\omega_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C(\varphi_{E})[1]}

in
\Mor(D^{b}(C)).

Note that the morphism
\id_{C}
is given by Remark
4.1
.

We obtain a morphishm
A\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E)
in
\Mor(D^{b}(C)).
Since
F
is full, we have a morphism in
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(A,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E)).
By Serre duality, we obtain

\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(A,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E))\cong\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,A)^{*}.

By hypothesis, this morphism has to be zero. This directly implies that
\id_{C}[-i]\circ(\pi_{i}\circ[\varphi_{A}])=0,
because
l\otimes\omega_{C}
is an isomorphism. Since the construction does not depend on the
i\in\mathbb{Z},
we obtain that the induced morphism
A^{i}_{1}[-i]\xrightarrow{\id_{C}[-i]\circ\pi_{i}\circ[\varphi_{A}]\circ i_{i}}A^{i}_{2}[-i]\otimes\omega_{C}
is zero. Due to the fact that it is precisely the morphism
(\pi_{i}\circ[\varphi_{A}]\circ i_{i})_{C}[-i]
in
\Coh(C)[-i],
as in Remark
4.1
, we obtain that
A^{i}_{1}\xrightarrow{\pi_{i}\circ[\varphi_{A}]\circ i_{1}}A^{i}_{2}
is also zero. The triangle (
4.1
) in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
induces a long exact sequence in cohomology and we get

\textstyle{A_{1}^{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}\circ[\varphi_{A}]\circ i_{i}}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i+1}\circ[\varphi_{E}]\circ i_{i+1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}^{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{E_{2}^{i+1},}

as the morphism in the component
i
in
\Mor(D^{b}(C))
is precisely the one given by the cohomology. Therefore
\pi_{i}\circ[\varphi_{E}]\circ i_{1}=0,
for all
i\neq-1,0.

This implies that there is a morphism from
E^{i}_{1}[-i]\rightarrow E^{i-1}_{2}[-i+1]
to
E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{E}}E_{2},
which, moreover, is a split monomorphism for
i\neq 0,1.
In addition, it provides a split monomorphism from
A^{i}_{1}[-i]\rightarrow A^{i-1}_{2}[-i+1]
to
A_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{A}}A_{2}
for
i\neq-1,0.

We get the isomorphism

\textstyle{A^{i}_{1}[-i]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{A}}
\textstyle{E_{1}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{E}[1]}
\textstyle{E^{i+1}_{1}[-i]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{i-1}_{2}[-i+1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l}
\textstyle{E_{2}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{i}_{2}[-i+1]}

in
\Mor(D^{b}(C))
, were the morphism of the first row is the isomorphism induced by the long exact sequence of cohomology of
the triangle (
4.1
).

From the isomorphism above, we construct the morphism

\textstyle{E_{1}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\varphi_{E}]}
\textstyle{E^{i+1}_{1}[-i]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{A^{i}_{1}[-i]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\varphi_{A}]}
\textstyle{E_{2}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{i}_{2}[-i+1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{A^{i-1}_{2}[-i+1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{2}}

in
\Mor(D^{b}(C)).
Once again, since the functor
F
is full, this needs to be the zero morphism. As a consequence, we obtain that
A^{i}_{1}[-i],A^{i}_{2}[-i]
are zero for all
i\neq 0,-1.

We now study the remaining object in the long exact sequence of cohomology

(4.5)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{-1}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{1}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{E^{0}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{0}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{t_{2}}
\textstyle{E^{1}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{-1}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{E^{0}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{0}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{E^{1}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

We will now show that
t_{i}=0,
for
i=1,2.

If
t_{1}\neq 0,
by Remark
4.1
, we obtain a non-zero morphism
{t_{1}}_{C}\colon A^{-1}_{1}\rightarrow E^{0}_{1}\otimes\omega_{C}.
By Serre duality

\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}(A_{1}^{-1},E^{0}_{1}\otimes\omega_{C})\cong\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}(E^{0}_{1}[-1],A_{1}^{-1})

and there is a non-zero morphism
t^{\prime}_{1}\colon E^{0}_{1}[-1]\rightarrow A^{-1}_{1}.
As there is a split monomorphism from
E_{1}^{0}\rightarrow E^{0}_{2}
to
E,
we get the non-zero morphism

\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{E}}
\textstyle{E^{0}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}_{1}[1]}
\textstyle{A_{1}^{-1}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{A}}
\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{0}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{A_{2}^{-1}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{2}}

in
\Mor(D^{b}(C)).

As above, this implies that
t^{\prime}_{1}=0
and therefore
t_{1}=0.

We will now prove that
t_{2}=0
.

The triangle
A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\rightarrow C(\varphi_{A})
induces a long exact sequence of cohomology. It follows that
H^{i}(C(\varphi_{A}))=0
unless
i=-1,
i.e.
C(\varphi_{A})\in\Coh(C)[1].

Note that
A_{2}[-1]\rightarrow C(\varphi_{A})[-1]\rightarrow A_{1}
is a short exact sequence in
\Coh(C),
then the morphism
C(\varphi_{A})[-1]\xrightarrow{\pi_{A}}A_{1}
is surjective in
\Coh(C).

Let us assume that
t_{2}\neq 0.

By Remark
4.1
, there is a non-zero morphism
t^{\prime}_{2}\colon A_{1}\rightarrow E^{1}_{1}\otimes\omega_{C}.
We now consider
t_{3}\coloneqq t^{\prime}_{2}\circ\pi_{A}
in
\Coh(C).
Since
\pi_{A}
is surjective and
t^{\prime}_{2}\neq 0,
then
t_{3}\neq 0.

This induces the morphism
\Phi\colon A\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E)
given by

\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{A}}
\textstyle{E^{1}_{1}\otimes\omega_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{E_{1}[1]\otimes\omega_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{E}\otimes\id}
\textstyle{E_{2}[1]\otimes\omega_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}(\varphi_{E})[1]\otimes\omega_{C}}

in
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(A,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E)).
Note that
i
is a split monomorphism. By Serre duality,

\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(A,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E))\cong\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,A)^{*}=0.

Therefore, we have that
\Phi=0.
After applying
i^{!},
we obtain

i^{!}(\Phi)=\mathcal{C}(\varphi_{A})[-1]\xrightarrow{t_{3}}E^{1}_{1}\otimes\omega_{C}\xrightarrow{i}E_{1}[1]\otimes\omega_{C},

which has to be zero.
As consequence
t_{3}=0
and we have a contradiction.
Hence, we get
A^{-1}_{1}=E^{1}_{1}=0,
which implies that
A_{1},E_{1}\in\Coh(C).

Remark 4.4
.

From now on we assume that all pre-stability conditions on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
that we are considering are locally finite.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
X
be either a skyscraper sheaf
\mathbb{C}(x)
or a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
C
. For any pre-stability condition
\sigma
on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
, if
i_{*}(X)
is not
\sigma
-semistable, then
j_{*}(X)
and
l_{*}(X)
are
\sigma
-stable.

Proof 8
.

We assume that
i_{*}(X)
is not
\sigma
-semistable. Therefore, we consider the last triangle of its Harder–Narasimhan filtration
E\rightarrow i_{*}(X)\rightarrow A\rightarrow E[1]
with
\Hom^{\leq 0}_{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}}(E,A)=0
and
A\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
semistable. By Lemma
4.2
, we have
E_{1},A_{1}\in\Coh(C),
thus we obtain a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow E_{1}\rightarrow X\rightarrow A_{1}\rightarrow 0,
in
\Coh(C)
which is not possible. Hence, either
E_{1}=0
or
A_{1}=0.

If
E_{1}=0,
we obtain a contradiction. As a consequence,
A_{1}=0
and
E_{1}\cong X.
By adjointness we have

\Hom^{\leq 0}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,A)=\Hom^{\leq 0}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,j_{*}(A_{2}))=\Hom^{\leq 0}_{D^{b}(C)}(C(\varphi_{E}),A_{2})=0.

Exactness of the functor
j^{*}
yields the triangle

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 17.46877pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.46877pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C(\varphi_{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.46877pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.46877pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 91.09377pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 91.09377pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 133.07991pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 133.07991pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C(\varphi_{E})[1]}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Since
\Hom^{\leq 0}(C(\varphi_{E}),A_{2})=0,
due to Lemma
2.47
, the classical GKR lemma for curves, we get that
C(\varphi_{E})[-1],A_{2}[-1]\in\Coh(C)
which once again is not possible. As
A_{2}
cannot be zero, we get
C(\varphi_{E})=0
and
A_{2}\cong X[1].
This implies that
A\cong j_{*}(X)[1]
and that
E\cong l_{*}(X)\in\mathcal{C}_{3}.
As a consequence,
j_{*}(X)
is
\sigma
-semistable.

We will now show that
l_{*}(X)
is
\sigma
-semistable. We proceed by contradiction. If
l_{*}(X)
is not
\sigma
-semistable, we examine the last triangle of its HN-filtration and we apply the Serre functor and the autoequivalence
G_{\omega^{*}_{C}}.

Arguing as above, we obtain
B\cong i_{*}(X)
and
i_{*}(X)
is
\sigma
-semistable, which contradicts our assumption. Therefore
l_{*}(X)
is
\sigma
-semistable.

We prove now that
l_{*}(X)
and
j_{*}(X)
are
\sigma
-stable. We start by proving by contradiction that
l_{*}(X)
is stable. If
l_{*}(X)
is not
\sigma
-stable, we consider its Jordan-Hölder filtration. Note that all its
\sigma
-stable factors
A_{i}
have the same phase
\phi.

We assume that
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(A_{i_{0}},l_{*}(X))\neq 0
for a
\sigma
-stable factor
A_{i_{0}}.
Therefore by
[
28
, Example 1.6]
, we have that all the stable factors of
l_{*}(X)
are isomorphic to
A_{i_{0}}.
Hence,
[l_{*}(X)]=n[A_{i_{0}}]
, where
n
is the number of stable factors. Since
[l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))]=(0,1,0,1)
and
[\mathcal{L}]=(1,\deg(\mathcal{L}),1,\deg(\mathcal{L}))
, we must have
n=1
, i.e.
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
l_{*}(\mathcal{L})
are stable.

An analogous proof works for the stability of
j_{*}(X).
Instead of using the Serre functor, we use
\mathcal{S}^{-1}.

Consequently, we obtain that
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
j_{*}(\mathcal{L})
are stable.

Remark 4.6
.

If
X
is either
\mathbb{C}(x)
or
\mathcal{L}
as above, we use Proposition
4.5
to prove that if
j_{*}(X)
(
l_{*}(X)
) is not
\sigma
-semistable, then
i_{*}(X)
and
l_{*}(X)
(
j_{*}(X)
and
i_{*}(X)
) are
\sigma
-stable. Meaning that if one of the objects
i_{*}(X),j_{*}(X),l_{*}(X)
is not
\sigma
-semistable then the other two have to be
\sigma
-stable.

Remark 4.7
.

If
i_{*}(X)
is not
\sigma
-semistable, where

X
is either
\mathbb{C}(x)
or
\mathcal{L},
then by Proposition
4.5
, we obtain the HN-filtration for
i_{*}(X).
It is given precisely by

l_{*}(X)\rightarrow i_{*}(X)\rightarrow j_{*}(X)[1]\rightarrow l_{*}(X)[1].

After applying the Serre functor, we obtain the corresponding HN-filtration for
j_{*}(X)
and
l_{*}(X).

Moreover, we define

\phi^{0}_{x}\coloneqq\phi_{\sigma}(i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)))\textnormal{ and }\phi^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}\coloneqq\phi_{\sigma}(i_{*}(\mathcal{L})),

\phi^{2}_{x}\coloneqq\phi_{\sigma}(j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)))\textnormal{ and }\phi^{3}_{\mathcal{L}}\coloneqq\phi_{\sigma}(j_{*}(\mathcal{L})),

\phi^{4}_{x}\coloneqq\phi_{\sigma}(l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)))\textnormal{ and }\phi^{5}_{\mathcal{L}}\coloneqq\phi_{\sigma}(l_{*}(\mathcal{L})).

If
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}_{C},
then
\phi_{1}=\phi_{\sigma}(i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})),

\phi_{3}=\phi_{\sigma}(j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})),
and
\phi_{5}=\phi_{\sigma}(l_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})).

Remark 4.8
.

Note that if
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma
-semistable, then
\phi^{4}_{x}>\phi^{2}_{x}+1.
Similarly, if
i_{*}(\mathcal{L})
is not
\sigma
-semistable, then
\phi^{5}_{\mathcal{L}}>\phi^{3}_{\mathcal{L}}+1.

Remark 4.9
.

After applying the Serre functor to the HN-filtration of
i_{*}(X)
, we obtain the analogous results for
j_{*}(X)
and
l_{*}(X).
For example, if
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma
-semistable, then
\phi^{2}_{x}>\phi^{0}_{x}.

Remark 4.10
.

If
i_{*}(X)
is strictly
\sigma
-semistable, where

X
is either
\mathbb{C}(x)
or
\mathcal{L},
then
j_{*}(X)
and
l_{*}(X)
are
\sigma
-stable. Moreover, a Jordan-Hölder filtration is given by

l_{*}(X)\rightarrow i_{*}(X)\rightarrow j_{*}(X)[1]\rightarrow l_{*}(X)[1].

It now makes sense to define the following sets:

Definition 4.11
.

We define the set
\Theta_{ij}
of pre-stability conditions on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
for
ij=12,23
or
31
as follows:

\displaystyle\Theta_{12}

\displaystyle=\{

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\mid

\displaystyle i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),i_{*}(\mathcal{L}),j_{*}(\mathcal{L})\>\>\rm{stable}\textnormal{ for all line bundles $\mathcal{L}\in\Coh(C)$ and all $x\in C$ }

\displaystyle\}

,

\displaystyle\Theta_{23}

\displaystyle=\{

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\mid

\displaystyle j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),j_{*}(\mathcal{L}),l_{*}(\mathcal{L})\>\>\rm{stable}\textnormal{ for all line bundles $\mathcal{L}\in\Coh(C)$ and all $x\in C$ }

\displaystyle\}

,

\displaystyle\Theta_{31}

\displaystyle=\{

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\mid

\displaystyle i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),i_{*}(\mathcal{L}),l_{*}(\mathcal{L})\>\>\rm{stable}\textnormal{ for all line bundles $\mathcal{L}\in\Coh(C)$ and all $x\in C$ }

\displaystyle\}

.

Recall that we assumed that all the pre-stability conditions are locally finite.

Remark 4.12
.

Note that

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(\Theta_{12})=\Theta_{23}\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(\Theta_{23})=\Theta_{31}\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(\Theta_{31})=\Theta_{12}.

Theorem 4.13
.

If
\sigma
is a pre-stability condition on
\mathcal{T}_{C},
then
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}\cup\Theta_{23}\cup\Theta_{31}.

Proof 9
.

We first assume that

\sigma\notin\Theta_{23}
and we prove that
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
or
\sigma\in\Theta_{31}.
Thus, there is a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
such that
j_{*}(\mathcal{L})
or
l_{*}(\mathcal{L})
is not
\sigma
-stable, or either
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
or
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma
-stable for some
x\in C.
Assume that there is
x\in C
such that
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma
-stable. We need to show that
\sigma\in\Theta_{12},
as it cannot be in
\Theta_{31}.
By Remark
4.6
, it follows that
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are
\sigma
-stable.
We will now show that for every line bundle
\mathcal{L}
and every
x\in C,
we have that
j_{*}(X)
and
i_{*}(X)
are
\sigma
-stable, where
X=\mathcal{L}
or
X=\mathbb{C}(x).

We prove this by contradiction, assume that there is a line bundle
\mathcal{L},
such that
i_{*}(\mathcal{L})
is not
\sigma
-stable, which implies that
j_{*}(\mathcal{L})
and
l_{*}(\mathcal{L})
are
\sigma
-stable. Since
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma
-stable, by Remark
4.7
we obtain that
\phi_{x}^{2}\geq\phi^{0}_{x}.
Analogously, since
i_{*}(\mathcal{L})
is not
\sigma
-semistable, then we get
\phi^{3}_{\mathcal{L}}+1\leq\phi^{5}_{\mathcal{L}}.
Let us consider the non-zero morphism
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))\rightarrow j_{*}(\mathcal{L})[1].
Since by hypothesis both are stable and not isomorphic, we obtain that
\phi^{2}_{x}<\phi^{3}_{\mathcal{L}}+1.

We have a non-zero morphism
l_{*}(\mathcal{L})[1]\rightarrow i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[1],
as both objects are
\sigma
-stable and not isomorphic, we get
\phi^{5}_{\mathcal{L}}<\phi^{0}_{x}.
We now put all the inequalities together, yielding

\phi^{0}_{x}\leq\phi^{2}_{x}<\phi_{\mathcal{L}}^{3}+1\leq\phi^{5}_{\mathcal{L}}<\phi^{0}_{x},

which is a contradiction. Therefore
i_{*}(\mathcal{L})
has to be
\sigma
-stable for all line bundles. Analogously we prove that
j_{*}(\mathcal{L})
has to be also stable for all line bundles
\mathcal{L}.

We now assume that there is a point
y\in C,
such that
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(y))
is not
\sigma
-stable. Then, by Remark
4.6
we obtain
\phi^{0}_{y}-1\geq\phi_{y}^{4}.
Note that
[i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))]=[i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(y))]
in the Grothendieck group. As a consequence, we obtain
\phi^{0}_{x}=\phi^{0}_{y}+m,
with
m\in\mathbb{Z}.
But as
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
is
\sigma
-semistable and we have non-zero morphisms from
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
to
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and to
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(y),
we also get non-zero morphisms from
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(y))
to
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})[1],

we obtain

\phi_{1}<\phi^{0}_{y}<\phi_{1}+1\textnormal{ and }\phi_{1}<\phi^{0}_{x}<\phi_{1}+1,

which implies that
\phi^{0}_{y}=\phi^{0}_{x}.

Since we have a non-zero morphism
j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})\rightarrow l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(y)),
we obtain
\phi_{3}<\phi^{4}_{y}
and from the morphism
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))\rightarrow j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})[1]
we obtain
\phi^{2}_{x}<\phi_{3}+1.

As a consequence, we get
\phi_{3}+1<\phi^{4}_{y}+1\leq\phi^{0}_{y}=\phi^{0}_{x}\leq\phi^{2}_{x}<\phi_{3}+1,
which is a contradiction. Therefore, we obtain that
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(y))
is
\sigma
-stable. Then
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}.

The other cases follow analogously.

Corollary 4.14
.

Let
\sigma
be a pre-stability condition on
\mathcal{T}_{C},
with
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))

\sigma
-stable for
x\in C
. Then, for all
y\in C,
we have that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(y))
is
\sigma
-stable and
\phi^{0}_{x}=\phi^{0}_{y}.

Proof 10
.

By Theorem
4.13
, we have that if
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
or
\sigma\in\Theta_{31},
it follows directly that for all
y\in C
we have that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(y))
is
\sigma
-stable.
Note that
[i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))]=[i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(y))]
in the Grothendieck group. This implies that
\phi^{0}_{x}=\phi^{0}_{y}+m,
with
m\in\mathbb{Z}.
By stability, we obtain
m=0.

Remark 4.15
.

Analogously, by using Serre duality, we prove Corollary
4.14
for
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)).

By Corollary
4.14
, if
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable for some
x\in C,
then
\phi^{0}_{x}
does not depend on
x.
Therefore, we define
\phi_{0}\coloneqq\phi^{0}_{x}
,
\phi_{2}\coloneqq\phi^{2}_{x}
,
\phi_{4}\coloneqq\phi^{4}_{x}.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{r},\mathcal{A}_{r})
be a pre-stability condition constructed in Lemma
3.48
. Then

(1)

i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))

l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are stable.

(2)

i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
and
j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
are
\sigma
-stable if and only if
\phi_{1}<\frac{3}{2}.

(3)

i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}),

j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
and
l_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
are
\sigma
-stable if and only if
\frac{1}{2}=\phi_{3}<\phi_{5}<\phi_{1}<\frac{3}{2}.

(4)

j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
and
l_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
are
\sigma
-stable and
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
is not
\sigma
-stable if and only if
\phi_{1}\geq\frac{3}{2}
and
\phi_{5}>\frac{1}{2}.

Moreover, we have that
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
and
l_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
are
\sigma
-stable and
j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
is not
\sigma
-stable if and only if
\phi_{1}\geq\frac{3}{2}
and
\phi_{5}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Proof 11
.

We start by proving the first part. By Remark
3.49
, we have that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1],

l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1]
and
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are in
\mathcal{A}_{r}
and that
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is stable of phase one. We will now show that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1]
is
\sigma
-stable. By contradiction we first assume that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma
-stable. As a consequence of Remark
4.9
, we have that
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable and
\phi_{4}\geq\phi_{2}+1,
but
\phi_{2}=1
and
1<\phi_{4}<2,
which gives a contradiction. The same reasoning works to prove that
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1]
is stable.
For the second part, note that
\phi_{1}=\phi(i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}))
and
\phi_{3}=\phi(j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}))=\frac{1}{2}
makes sense, as Lemma
3.50
implies that
j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})\in\mathcal{A}_{r}
and that
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
is in
\mathcal{A}_{r}
or in
\mathcal{A}_{r}[1].
Moreover, if
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
and
j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
are
\sigma
-stable, then
\phi_{1}<\phi_{3}+1=\frac{3}{2},
because there is a non-zero morphism
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})\rightarrow j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})[1].
We will now prove the other direction. We assume that
\phi_{1}<\frac{3}{2}
and that
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
is not stable. By Remark
4.9
, the statement follows. The other two statements follow in a similar way.

4.1.
Pre-stability conditions in
\Theta_{12}

We are going to show that pre-stability conditions
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
are given as the ones constructed in Corollary
3.36
or in Lemma
3.48
.

We first characterise the hearts of the pre-stability conditions in terms of the stability of the skyscraper sheaves. We study pre-stability conditions satisfying that
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is stable of phase one. We separate them into two cases when
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma
-stable and when it is. If
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma
-stable, then the pre-stability conditions are CP-glued. If
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable, we obtain stability conditions of the form of Lemma
3.47
. We follow closely
[
12
, Proposition 10.1]
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
be a pre-stability condition such that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable,
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable of phase one and
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma
-stable.

We assume that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[n]\in\mathcal{A}.
If
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\mathcal{A},
then
H^{i}(E_{1})=0,
unless
i=-n-1,-n.
Also
H^{i}(E_{2})=0
unless
i=0
and
H^{i}(C(\varphi))=0,
unless
i=-n-1,-n-2,0.

Proof 12
.

First note that
n\geq 0.
Indeed, as
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma
-stable, it implies that
\phi_{0}-\phi_{2}\leq 0.
As
\phi_{2}=1
we have
\phi_{0}\leq 1,
also
0<\phi(i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[n])=\phi_{0}+n\leq 1.
Combining the inequalities above, we get
n\geq 0.

Let
E\in\mathcal{A}
be stable with phase
0<\phi(E)<1.
As
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is stable we have that
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{i}(E,j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)))=0,
for all
i<0.

By adjointness, we have

0=\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{i}(E,j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)))=\Hom^{i}_{D^{b}(C)}(C(\varphi),\mathbb{C}(x)).

Moreover, by stability we have
\Hom^{i}(i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[n],E)=0,
for all
i<0.
By adjointness and Serre duality in
D^{b}(C),
we get

0=\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{i}(i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[n],E)=\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}^{i}(\mathbb{C}(x)[n],C(\varphi)[-1])=\Hom^{n-i+2}_{D^{b}(C)}(C(\varphi),\mathbb{C}(x))^{*},

i.e.
\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}^{j}(C(\varphi),\mathbb{C}(x))=0
for
j>n+2.
Therefore, we obtain
\Hom^{i}(C(\varphi),\mathbb{C}(x))=0,
unless
0\leq i\leq n+2.
By
[
13
, Proposition 5.4]
, it follows that
C(\varphi)
is isomorphic to a complex of locally-free sheaves and
H^{i}(C(\varphi))=0\textnormal{ unless }-n-2\leq i\leq 0.

Similarly, by stability
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{i}(E,i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[n])=0,
for all
i<0.
By adjointness, we get
0=\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{i}(E,i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[n])=\Hom^{i+n}_{D^{b}(C)}(E_{1},\mathbb{C}(x)),
i.e.
\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}^{j}(E_{1},\mathbb{C}(x))=0
for
j<n.
Since
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma
-semistable, it follows from its HN-filtration that
\phi_{2}\geq\phi_{4}\geq\phi_{0},
which implies that
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[n]\in\mathcal{P}(0,n+1],
because
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))\in\mathcal{P}(0,1]
and
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[n]\in\mathcal{P}(0,1].
As a consequence, we have
\Hom^{i}(l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[n],E)=0,
for all
i<0.
By adjointness and Serre duality in
D^{b}(C),

we get
0=\Hom_{T_{C}}^{i}(l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[n],E)=\Hom^{n+1-i}_{D^{b}(C)}(E_{1},\mathbb{C}(x))^{*},
i.e.
\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}^{j}(E_{1},\mathbb{C}(x))=0
for
j>n+1.
Consequently, we obtain
\Hom^{i}(E_{1},\mathbb{C}(x))=0
unless
n\leq i\leq n+1.
By
[
13
, Proposition 5.4]
, we have that
E_{1}
is isomorphic to a length two complex of locally-free sheaves and
H^{i}(E_{1})=0\textnormal{ unless }i=-n,-n-1.

We will now prove a similar result for
E_{2}.
In this case we use that
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is stable of phase one. First of all, by the same reasoning as above,
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))\in\mathcal{P}(-n,1].
Hence, we get

\Hom^{i}(E,l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)))=0,
for all
i<0.
By adjointness, we obtain

0=\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{i}(E,l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)))=\Hom^{i}_{D^{b}(C)}(E_{2},\mathbb{C}(x)).
In addition, since
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is stable of phase
1,
we get
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{i}(j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),E)=0
for
i\leq 0.
By adjointness and Serre duality in
D^{b}(C),
we have

0=\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{i}(j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),E)=\Hom^{1-i}_{D^{b}(C)}(E_{2},\mathbb{C}(x))^{*},

i.e.
\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}^{j}(E_{2},\mathbb{C}(x))=0
for
j>0.
Thus, we obtain
\Hom^{i}(E_{2},\mathbb{C}(x))=0,
unless
i=0.
By
[
13
, Proposition 5.4]
, we have that
E_{2}
is isomorphic to a length one complex of locally-free sheaves and
H^{i}(E_{2})=0\textnormal{ unless }i=0.

The triangle
E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}\rightarrow C(\varphi)
induces a long exact sequence in cohomology. As a consequence, we have that
H^{i}(C(\varphi))=0\textnormal{ unless }-n-2,-n-1,0.

If
E\ncong j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable with phase one, then
H^{i}(E_{1})=0
unless
i=-n-1\textnormal{ and }
H^{i}(E_{2})=0\textnormal{ for all }i.
This implies that
H^{i}(C(\varphi))=0\textnormal{ unless }i=-n-2
and that
E_{1}
is torsion free.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
be a pre-stability condition such that
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are
\sigma
-stable and
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable of phase one. Then, for
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
we have

(1)

If
E\in\mathcal{A},
then
H^{i}(E_{j})=0,
unless
i={0,1},
for
j=1,2.
Also
H^{i}(C(\varphi))=0,
unless
i={-1,0}.

Moreover,
H^{-1}(C(\varphi)),H^{0}(E_{1})
are torsion-free.

(2)

If
E
is stable of phase one, then either
E=j_{*}(T),
where
T\in\Coh(C)
a torsion sheaf, or
E\in\TCoh(C)
with
H^{0}(C(\varphi))=0.
We have that
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are torsion-free.

(3)

\TCoh(C)\subseteq\mathcal{P}(0,2]

(4)

The pair
\mathcal{T}=\TCoh(C)\cap\mathcal{P}(1,2]\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{F}=\TCoh(C)\cap\mathcal{P}(0,1]
defines a torsion pair on
\TCoh(C).
Moreover, the heart
\mathcal{A}
is the corresponding tilt.

Proof 13
.

First note that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1],l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1]\in\mathcal{A}.
Indeed, there are non-zero morphisms
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))\rightarrow j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)[1]),

j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))\rightarrow l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))\rightarrow i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)).
By stability, it follows that
1<\phi_{4}<\phi_{0}<2.

We start by proving part one. Let
E\in\mathcal{A}
be stable with phase
0<\phi(E)<1.
As
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is stable we have that
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{i}(E,j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)))=0,
for all
i<0.
By adjointness, we have
0=\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{i}(E,j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)))=\Hom^{i}_{D^{b}(C)}(C(\varphi),\mathbb{C}(x)).
Moreover, by stability we have
\Hom^{i}(i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1],E)=0,
for all
i<0.
By adjointness and Serre duality in
D^{b}(C),
we get

0=\Hom_{T_{C}}^{i}(i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1],E)=\Hom^{1-i}_{D^{b}(C)}(C(\varphi),\mathbb{C}(x))^{*},
i.e.
\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}^{j}(C(\varphi),\mathbb{C}(x))=0
for
j>1.
Therefore, we obtain
\Hom^{i}(C(\varphi),\mathbb{C}(x))=0,
unless
0\leq i\leq 1.
By
[
13
, Proposition 5.4]
, it follows that
C(\varphi)
is isomorphic to a length two complex of locally-free sheaves and
H^{i}(C(\varphi))=0\textnormal{ unless }i={-1,0}.

Similarly, we obtain
\Hom^{i}(E_{1},\mathbb{C}(x))=0
unless
-1\leq i\leq 0.
By
[
13
, Proposition 5.4]
, we have that
E_{1}
is isomorphic to a length two complex of locally-free sheaves and
H^{i}(E_{1})=0\textnormal{ unless }i={0,1}.

We prove a similar result for
E_{2}.
In this case we use that
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is stable of phase one. We obtain
\Hom^{i}(E_{2},\mathbb{C}(x))=0,
unless
-1\leq i\leq 0.
By
[
13
, Proposition 5.4]
, we have that
E_{2}
is isomorphic to a length two complex of locally-free sheaves and

H^{i}(E_{2})=0\textnormal{ unless }i={0,1}.
This concludes the proof of the first part.

We will now proceed to proving the second part. Let
E\in\mathcal{P}(1)
be a stable object, which is not isomorphic to
j_{*}(T),
where
T
is a torsion sheaf.
Since the phase of
E
is one, we have
H^{i}(E_{1})=0\textnormal{ unless }i={0}\textnormal{ and }H^{i}(E_{2})=0\textnormal{ unless }i={0}.

For the third part, we assume that
E\in\TCoh(C).
If
F\in\mathcal{P}((2,\infty)),
then by the first part
F\in D^{\leq-2}\subseteq D^{\leq-1},
where
(D^{\leq 0},D^{\geq 0})
is the standard t-structure. Consequently, we have
0=\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(D^{\leq 0},D^{\geq 1})=\Hom(D^{\leq-1},D^{\geq 0}),
therefore
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(F,E)=0.
Analogously, we have that if
B\in\mathcal{P}(\leq 0),
then
B\in D^{\geq 1}.
As
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(D^{\leq 0},D^{\geq 1})=0,
we obtain
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,B)=0.
It follows that
E\in\mathcal{P}(0,2].

We will now prove the fourth part. Let
E\in\TCoh(C).
By the third part of the statement, there is a triangle
A\rightarrow E\rightarrow B\rightarrow A[1],
where
A\in\mathcal{P}(1,2]
and
B\in\mathcal{P}(0,1].

After applying
i^{*},
we obtain a long exact sequence of cohomology and by part one we obtain

H^{-1}(i^{*}(A))=H^{1}(i^{*}(B))=0.

This implies that
i^{*}(A),i^{*}(B)\in\Coh(C).
Analogously, we have
j^{!}(A),j^{!}(B)\in\Coh(C)
and we obtain
A,B\in\TCoh(C).

Moreover, if
A\in\mathcal{T},
we have additional information. Indeed, as we have
H^{i}(j^{*}(B))=0
unless
i=-1,0
and
H^{i}(j^{*}(A))=0
unless
i=-2,-1.
After taking the long exact sequence we obtain
H^{i}(j^{*}(A))=0
unless
i=-1.
As a consequence, if
A=A_{1}\xrightarrow{g}A_{2},
it follows that
\Coker(g)=0.

By applying Serre duality of doing exactly the same proof under the new hypothesis, we obtain the following results.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
be a pre-stability condition such that
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are
\sigma
-stable and
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable of phase one. Then, for
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
we have

(1)

If
E\in\mathcal{A},
then
H^{i}(E_{j})=0,
unless
i={-1,0},
for
j=1,2.
Also
H^{i}(C(\varphi))=0,
unless
i={-1,0}.
We also have that
H^{-1}(E_{1}),H^{-1}(E_{2})
are torsion-free.

(2)

If
E
is stable of phase one, then either
E=i_{*}(T),
where
T\in\Coh(C)
is a torsion sheaf, or
E\in\mathcal{H}_{31}
with
H^{0}(E_{1})=0.
Moreover, we have that
E_{2}
and
C(\varphi)
are torsion-free.

(3)

\mathcal{H}_{31}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(0,2]

(4)

The pair
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{H}_{31}\cap\mathcal{P}(1,2]\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{H}_{31}\cap\mathcal{P}(0,1]
defines a torsion pair on
\mathcal{H}_{31}.
Moreover, the heart
\mathcal{A}
is the corresponding tilt.

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
be a pre-stability condition such that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are
\sigma
-stable and
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable of phase one. Then, for
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
we have

(1)

If
E\in\mathcal{A},
then
H^{i}(E_{1})=0,
unless
i={0,1}
and
H^{i}(E_{2})=0,
unless
i={0,-1}.
We also obtain that
H^{i}(C(\varphi))=0,
unless
i={-1,0}.
We also have that
H^{-1}(C(\varphi)),H^{-1}(E_{2})
are torsion-free.

(2)

If
E
is stable of phase one, then either
E=l_{*}(T),
where
T\in\Coh(C)
a torsion sheaf, or
E\in\mathcal{H}_{23}
with
H^{0}(E_{2})=0
and
H^{0}(E_{1})
torsion-free sheaves.

(3)

\mathcal{H}_{23}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(0,2]

(4)

The pair
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{H}_{23}\cap\mathcal{P}(1,2]\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{H}_{23}\cap\mathcal{P}(0,1]
defines a torsion pair on
\mathcal{H}_{23}.
Moreover, the heart
\mathcal{A}
is the corresponding tilt.

We will now study the orbit of
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
under the action of
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
in order to choose a simpler representative.

Proposition 4.21
.

For every
\sigma\in\Theta_{12},
there is
g\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that for
\sigma g=(Z,\mathcal{A})
we can find stability conditions
\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\Coh^{r}(C))\textnormal{ and }\sigma_{2}=(Z_{\mu},\Coh(C))\in\Stab(C)
with
\Coh^{r}_{1}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and
r>-1,

\Coh_{2}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},

{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}=Z_{1}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}_{2}}}=Z_{\mu}.

Proof 14
.

By the stability of
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}),
we have
\phi_{1}<\phi_{0}<\phi_{1}+1.
Therefore, there is an orientation preserving transformation
M\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying that

(A,D)\mapsto(-1,0)
and
(B,C)\mapsto(0,1),

where
Z(i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))=A+Di
and
Z(i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}))=B+Ci.
There is an increasing function
f\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that satisfies
f(x+1)=f(x)+1
with
f(1)=\phi_{0},

f(1/2)=\phi_{1}.
We obtain
(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
. The stability condition
\sigma^{{}^{\prime}}\coloneqq\sigma(T,f)
satisfies

Z^{{}^{\prime}}(r_{1},d_{1},0,0)=-d_{1}+r_{1}i\textnormal{ and }\Coh_{1}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime}},

where
\sigma^{{}^{\prime}}=(Z^{{}^{\prime}},\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime}}).
Indeed, we have

\displaystyle i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))\in\mathcal{P}(\phi_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}(1),

\displaystyle i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})\in\mathcal{P}(\phi_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}(1/2),

\displaystyle i_{*}(\mathcal{L})\in\mathcal{P}(\phi_{\mathcal{L}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}(t_{\mathcal{L}}),

with
t_{\mathcal{L}}\in(0,1).
Therefore, all skyscraper sheaves and line bundles in
\Coh_{1}(C)
are in
\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}(0,1].
Since any object in
\Coh_{1}(C)
admits a filtration with quotients either isomorphic to skyscraper sheaves or to line bundles, we obtain
\Coh_{1}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime}}.

As the action of
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
on the set of pre-stability conditions preserves the stability of the objects and
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),

j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
are
\sigma
-stable, we have
\phi_{3}<\phi_{2}<\phi_{3}+1
in
\sigma^{{}^{\prime}}.
Therefore, we can find
g_{1}=(T_{1},f_{1})\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
such that
\sigma^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\coloneqq\sigma^{\prime}(T_{1},f_{1})
satisfies
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{2}=Z_{\mu}
and
\Coh_{2}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}.

Moreover, if we consider
\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\Coh^{r}(C))\in\Stab(C)
with
f_{1}(0)=r\in\mathbb{R}
, which under our correspondence with
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
is
(T_{1},f_{1})\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
then
\sigma^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
also satisfies that
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{1}=Z_{1}
and
\Coh_{1}^{r}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}.
Indeed, by definition
Z^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=T_{1}^{-1}\circ Z^{{}^{\prime}}.
Consequently, we can assert that
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{1}=T_{1}^{-1}\circ Z_{\mu}.

We will now show that
\Coh_{1}^{r}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime\prime}},
where
f_{1}(0)=r=n+\theta,
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\theta\in[0,1).
We prove this in several steps. We first show that
\Coh_{1}(C)[n]\subseteq\mathcal{P}(-1,1].
Then, we construct a torsion pair of
\Coh_{1}(C)[n]
and we compare it with
\Coh(C)[n]=\langle\mathcal{T}_{\theta}[n],\mathcal{F}_{\theta}[n]\rangle,
which is the torsion pair given in Remark
2.52
.

We first show that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)[n])\in\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}.
Note that
f_{1}(\phi_{0})=1,
because

i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))\in\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}(1)=\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(f_{1}^{-1}(1))=\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\phi_{0})\textnormal{ and }f_{1}(-n)=\theta.

We apply
f_{1}^{-1}
to the following inequality
\theta<1\leq\theta+1
and we obtain

(4.6)

-n<\phi_{0}\leq-n+1,

which is equivalent to
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)[n])\in\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}.
By stability
-n<2.

Claim 4.22
.

f_{1}(0)=r>-1.

Proof 15
.

Since
r=n+\theta
and
n\geq-1,
we obtain
r\geq-1.
We just need to prove that
r\neq-1.
Assume that
r=-1.
Then if
Z_{1}(r_{1},d_{1})=A_{1}d_{1}+Br_{1}+(C_{1}r_{1}+D_{1}d_{1})i,
we get
D_{1}=0.
By the stability of
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
in
\sigma^{{}^{\prime\prime}},
we get
\phi_{0}<2.
But, by the definition of
Z_{1},
we obtain
\phi_{0}=\phi(i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1])+1=2,
which is a contradiction.

We will now study line bundles
\mathcal{L}.
Note that
f_{1}(\phi_{\mathcal{L}})=t_{\mathcal{L}},
with
t_{\mathcal{L}}\in(0,1)
because

i_{*}(\mathcal{L})\in\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}(t_{\mathcal{L}})=\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(f^{-1}(t_{\mathcal{L}}))=\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\phi_{\mathcal{L}}).

The proof naturally falls into two cases:

Case 1
:
\theta<t_{\mathcal{L}}\leq\theta+1.
We apply
f_{1}^{-1}
to the inequality above and we obtain
-n<\phi_{\mathcal{L}}\leq-n+1,
which is equivalent to
i_{*}(\mathcal{L}[n])\in\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}.

Case 2
:
\theta-1<t_{\mathcal{L}}\leq\theta.
We apply
f_{1}^{-1}
to the inequality above and we obtain
-n-1<\phi_{\mathcal{L}}\leq-n,
which is equivalent to
i_{*}(\mathcal{L}[n+1])\in\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}.

Claim 4.23
.

\Coh_{1}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{P}(-1,1].

Proof 16
.

We just proved that all line bundles and the skyscraper sheaves are in
\mathcal{P}(-n-1,-n+1],
therefore
\Coh_{1}(C)[n]\subseteq\mathcal{P}(-1,1].

We set
\mathcal{T}_{1}=\Coh_{1}(C)[n]\cap\mathcal{P}(0,1]\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{F}_{1}=\Coh_{1}(C)[n]\cap\mathcal{P}(-1,0].

Claim 4.24
.

(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})
is a torsion pair of
\Coh_{1}(C)[n].

Proof 17
.

The proof falls naturally into two cases:

Case 1:

l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
-stable.

Let
E\in\Coh(C).
We have
E[n]\in\Coh(C)[n].

Since
\Coh_{1}(C)[n]\subseteq\mathcal{P}(-1,1],
there are objects
T\in\mathcal{P}(0,1]
and
F\in\mathcal{P}(-1,1]
such that

0\rightarrow T\rightarrow i_{*}(E)[n]\rightarrow F\rightarrow 0.

We obtain long exact sequences in cohomology and by Lemma
4.17
, we have that
H^{i}(T_{1})=H^{i}(F_{1})=0
unless
i=-n
and that
T_{2}=F_{2}=0.
As a consequence, we get
\Coh_{1}(C)[n]=(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})
and
\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1}[1]\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}.

Case 2:

l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
-stable.

Note that the stability of
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
implies, as proved in Lemma
4.18
, that
n=-1
and that
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1]\in A^{{}^{\prime\prime}}.

Let
E\in\Coh(C).
We have
E[-1]\in\Coh(C)[-1].

Since
\Coh_{1}(C)[-1]\in\mathcal{P}(-1,1],
there are objects
T\in\mathcal{P}(0,1]
and
F\in\mathcal{P}(-1,1]
such that

0\rightarrow T\rightarrow i_{*}(E)[-1]\rightarrow F\rightarrow 0.

We obtain long exact sequences in cohomology. By Lemma
4.18
, we have
H^{i}(T_{1})=0
unless
i=0,1
and
H^{i}(F_{1})=0
unless
i=1,2.
Then, we obtain that
H^{i}(T_{1})=H^{i}(F_{1})=0
unless
i=1.

We also get that
H^{i}(T_{2})=0
unless
i=0,1
and
H^{i}(F_{2})=0
unless
i=1,2.

Therefore, we have
T_{2}=F_{2}=0.

We will now proceed to prove that
\Coh_{1}^{r}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}.
We show that
\mathcal{T}_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{\theta}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\mathcal{F}_{\theta}.
It is easy to show that
\mathcal{T}_{\theta}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{1},
where
\Coh(C)=(\mathcal{T}_{\theta},\mathcal{F}_{\theta})
is the torsion pair described in Remark
2.52
(up to shift) and
\theta\neq 0.

It is enough to show this for slope stable torsion free sheaves
E\in\Coh(C)
, such that
E[n]\in\Coh(C)[n].
Let
0\rightarrow T\rightarrow E[n]\rightarrow F\rightarrow 0
be the triangle induced by the torsion pair
(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1}).
If
T
and
F
are non-trivial, then as
i_{*}(F[1])\in\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
and
i_{*}(T)\in\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime\prime}},
we get that
\Im(Z)(i_{*}(T))>0,
which implies

\mu(T[-n])>-\cot(\theta\pi)
and
\Im(Z)(i_{*}(F))\leq 0,
which implies
\mu(F[-n])\leq-\cot(\theta\pi).
Since
E
is
\mu
-stable, we obtain a contradiction.

If
\theta=0,
then
\mathcal{T}^{\theta}_{1}=\Coh(C)[n]
and
\mathcal{F}^{\theta}_{1}=0.
Since
r\in\mathbb{Z},
we obtain

Z_{1}(r,d)=A_{1}d_{1}+Br_{1}+(Cr_{1})i,

i.e.
D_{1}=0.
This implies that

\mathcal{T}_{1}=\Coh_{1}(C)[n].
Therefore
\Coh^{r}_{1}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}.

Remark 4.25
.

Let us consider
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
and
g^{\prime}=(T^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}).
By Proposition
4.21
, there is
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that for
\sigma g=(Z,\mathcal{A})
we can find stability conditions

\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\Coh^{r}(C))\textnormal{ and }\sigma_{2}=(Z_{\mu},\Coh(C))\in\Stab(C)

with
\Coh^{r}_{1}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and
r>-1,

\Coh_{2}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}.
Note that the proof of the inclusion of the hearts in
\mathcal{A}
depends only on the stability of
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
for all
x\in C
and
i_{*}(\mathcal{L})
and
j_{*}(\mathcal{L}),
for every line bundle
\mathcal{L}.
As a consequence, we obtain that
\sigma gg^{\prime}=(Z^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
satisfies that

\Coh_{1}^{f\circ f^{\prime}(0)}(C)\subseteq A^{\prime}\textnormal{ and }\Coh^{f^{\prime}(0)}_{2}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}.

The following lemma gives us some CP-glued stability conditions in
\Theta_{12}.

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})\in\Theta_{12},
such that there are stability conditions

\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\Coh^{r}(C))=(T_{1},f_{1})\textnormal{ and }\sigma_{2}=(Z_{\mu},\Coh(C))\in\Stab(C)

with
\Coh^{r}_{1}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},

\Coh_{2}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},
and
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{1}=Z_{1}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{2}=Z_{\mu}.
If
f_{1}(0)=r\geq 0,
then
\sigma=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{\mu}).

Proof 18
.

Since
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
satisfy gluing conditions i.e
f_{1}(0)\geq f_{2}(0)=0,
then there is a pre-stability condition
\sigma_{12}=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
on
\mathcal{T}_{C}.
It follows directly from Proposition
3.36
that
\sigma=\sigma_{12}.

We will now study pre-stability conditions
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})\in\Theta_{12}.
For

\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\Coh^{r}(C))\textnormal{ and }\sigma_{2}=(Z_{\mu},\Coh(C))\in\Stab(C)

we have
\Coh^{r}_{1}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},

\Coh_{2}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},

{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{1}=Z_{1},

{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{2}=Z_{\mu},
and
-1<r<0,
where
r=n+\theta=f_{1}(0)
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\theta\in[0,1).
Here
\sigma_{1}
is given by
(T_{1},f_{1})
under the correspondence with
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}).

Remark 4.27
.

l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable. Assume that
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not stable. By Remark
4.9
this implies that
n+\theta=f_{1}(0)\geq 0,
which contradicts our assumption.

4.2.
Pre-stability conditions in
\Theta_{23}
and
\Theta_{31}

We have the analogous statement of Proposition
4.21
for
\Theta_{23}
and
\Theta_{31}.
We leave the proof to the reader, since it follows exactly the same steps as the result for
\Theta_{12}.

Proposition 4.28
.

For every
\sigma\in\Theta_{23},
there is a
g\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that for
\sigma g=(Z,\mathcal{A})
we can find stability conditions

\sigma_{3}=(Z_{3},\Coh^{r}(C))\textnormal{ and }\sigma_{2}=(Z_{\mu},\Coh(C))\in\Stab(C)

with
\Coh^{r}_{3}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},

\Coh_{2}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},

r<0,

{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{3}=Z_{3}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{2}=Z_{\mu}.

Proposition 4.29
.

For every
\sigma\in\Theta_{31},
there is a
g\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that for
\sigma g=(Z,\mathcal{A})
we can find stability conditions

\sigma_{3}=(Z_{3},\Coh^{r_{3}}(C))\textnormal{ and }\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\Coh^{r_{1}}(C))\in\Stab(C)

with
\Coh^{r_{3}}_{3}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},

\Coh^{r_{1}}_{1}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},
with
r_{3}-r_{1}>0,

{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{3}=Z_{3},

{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{D}}_{1}=Z_{1}
and
M_{3}-M_{1}=I.

4.3.
Non-gluing pre-stability conditions

We will now study pre-stability conditions
\sigma
on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
satisfying that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are stable, where
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is stable with phase one and
j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
has phase 1/2. Note that as proved in Lemma
4.18
, we have
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1]\in\mathcal{A}.
We use Lemma
4.18
to prove that these stability conditions are precisely given by the pair
(Z_{r},\mathcal{A}_{r})
constructed in Lemma
3.48
.

Lemma 4.30
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
be a pre-stability condition on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
such that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are
\sigma
-stable and the object
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
are in
\mathcal{A}
and are also
\sigma
-stable with

Z([j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))])=-1\textnormal{ and }Z([j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})])=i.

Then
\sigma
is given by the pairs constructed in Lemma
3.48
.

Proof 19
.

We prove the statement for
\sigma\in\Theta_{12},
the other two cases follow analogously.
By Proposition
4.21
, there are stability conditions
\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\Coh^{r}(C))\textnormal{ and }\sigma_{2}=(Z_{\mu},\Coh(C))\in\Stab(C),
such that
\Coh^{r}_{1}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and
\Coh_{2}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},
with
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{1}=Z_{1}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{2}=Z_{\mu}.
Note that
-1<f_{1}(0)<0,
where
\sigma_{1}=(T_{1},f_{1})\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}).

First of all we show that
Z
is given by equation
3.19
. Our stability function
Z
is completely determined by
Z_{1}
and
Z_{\mu},
therefore it has the following form

Z(r_{1},d_{1},r_{2},d_{2})=Ad_{1}+Br_{1}-d_{2}+i(Cr_{1}+Dd_{1}+r_{2}).

Let
M\coloneqq T_{1}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-A&B\\
-D&C\\
\end{bmatrix}.
Since
\sigma_{1}
is a stability condition, we have that
\det(M)>0.

As
-1<f_{1}(0)<0,
then by Lemma
4.18
, we obtain
1<\phi_{0}<2
and
D<0.

We will now show that
\det(M+I)>0.

If
\Tr(M)\geq 0,
there is nothing to prove because
\det(M+I)=\det(M)+\Tr(M)+1>0.
Note that if
\Tr(M)<0,
then
l_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
is
\sigma
-stable. Since
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
l_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
are stable, we obtain
\phi_{5}<\phi_{4}<\phi_{5}+1
which is equivalent to the fact that
\det(M+I)>0.

We consider the torsion pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})=\TCoh(C)
given in Lemma
4.18
. We are going to show that it is equal to the torsion pair
(\mathcal{T}^{{}^{\prime}},\mathcal{F}^{{}^{\prime}})
given by Lemma
3.43
. It is enough to show that
\mathcal{T}^{{}^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{F}^{{}^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}.

Let us take a torsion-free
\lambda
-semistable object
E=E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime},
which by definition satisfies
\lambda(E)>3/4.

There is a short exact sequence
0\rightarrow T\rightarrow E\rightarrow F\rightarrow 0,
with
T\in\mathcal{T}
and
F=F_{1}\rightarrow F_{2}\in\mathcal{F}.

By Lemma
4.18
and Lemma
3.43
we have that
i^{!}(E),i^{!}(T)\in\Coh(C).
It follows that
i^{!}(F)\in\Coh(C).

As
F_{1}
is torsion-free and
F_{1}\twoheadrightarrow F_{2},
then
F\neq 0\rightarrow G_{2},
where
G_{2}
is a torsion sheaf. We apply Remark
3.40
and we obtain that
3/4<\lambda(E)\leq\lambda(F)\leq 3/4,
which gives us a contradiction and this implies that
E\in\mathcal{T}.

Let us take a
\lambda
-semistable torsion-free object
E=E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime},
and by definition holds that
\lambda(E)\leq 3/4.
We start with the case
\lambda(E)<3/4.
There is a short exact sequence given by
0\rightarrow T\rightarrow E\rightarrow F\rightarrow 0,
with
T\in\mathcal{T}
and
F\in\mathcal{F}.
We get
\lambda(T)\geq 3/4
and
\lambda(F)\leq 3/4.
If
T\neq 0,
then
by
\lambda
-semistability
3/4\leq\lambda(T)\leq\lambda(E)<3/4,
which gives us a contradiction and this implies that
E\in F.

Let us take a torsion-free
\lambda
-semistable object
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
with
\lambda(E)=3/4.
Consider the short exact sequence
0\rightarrow T\rightarrow E\rightarrow F\rightarrow 0,
with
T\in\mathcal{T}
and
F\in\mathcal{F}.
Note that the inequality
3/4\leq\lambda(T)\leq\lambda(E)=3/4
holds, so that
3/4=\lambda(T).
If
T=T_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi^{\prime}}T_{2},
we get
T[-1]\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(1).
Moreover by Lemma
4.18
, we have that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(1)\subseteq\TCoh(C)
which implies directly that
T=0.

Note that for all torsion-free objects
E\in\mathcal{T}^{{}^{\prime}},
after using its Harder–Narasimhan filtration, we obtain that
E\in\mathcal{T}.
Analogously for
E\in\mathcal{F}^{{}^{\prime}}.
We easily extend the result above to any object in
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{F}.
Consequently
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}^{{}^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}^{{}^{\prime}}.

Remark 4.31
.

Under the assumption of the last proposition, for
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
we can define a third stability condition
\sigma_{3}=((M+I)^{-1},f_{3})\in\Stab(C),
that comes from the data given by
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
l_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}),
where the integer part of
f_{3}(0)=r_{3}
is
-1.
Note that by Lemma
3.50
, we obtain
\Coh^{r_{3}}_{3}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}.

Proposition 4.32
.

Let
\sigma
be a pre-stability condition in
\Theta_{12}.
There is an element
g\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
\sigma g
is given by a CP-glued pre-stability condition or one constructed by tilting in Subsection
3.2.2
.

Proof 20
.

After applying Proposition
4.21
, this follows directly from Lemma
4.26
and Lemma
4.30
.

4.4.
Characterising CP-glued pre-stability conditions in
\Theta_{12}

Let us consider the sets
\Theta_{i}
consisiting of pre-stability conditions, for
i=1,2
or
3,
which are, up to the action of
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
CP-glued with respect to the semiorthognal decomposition
\langle\mathcal{C}_{i},{{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{C}_{i}\rangle.
Note that by Proposition
3.36
, it follows that the set
\Theta_{1}\subseteq\Theta_{12}.

The aim of this section is to characterise the sets
\Theta_{i}
inside
\Theta_{12}.

Condition (*)
: If
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
satisfies that
there are stability conditions
\sigma_{1}=(\Coh^{r}_{1}(C),Z_{1})
and
\sigma_{2}=(\Coh_{2}(C),Z_{\mu})\in\Stab(C)
with
\Coh^{r}_{1}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},

\Coh(C)_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{A},
and
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{1}=Z_{1}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{2}=Z_{\mu}.
Let
f_{1}(0)=n+\theta,
with
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\theta\in[0,1),
we say that it satisfies condition (*). Let
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}.
By Proposition
4.21
, we have that there is
g\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
such that
\sigma g
satisfies condition (*).

Under condition (*), we define
f_{12}(\sigma)(x)=f_{1}(x)-x.

From now on we assume that
\sigma
satisfies condition (*) and
\sigma_{1}=(T,f_{1}).
If
f_{12}(\sigma)(0)\geq 0
by Proposition
4.26
, we already know that
\sigma\in\Theta_{1}.
Now we assume
f_{12}(\sigma)(0)<0.
We would like to understand when
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
is in
\Theta_{i},
for
i=1,2,3.

Recall that by Remark
4.31
there is also
\sigma_{3}=(Z_{3},\Coh^{r_{3}}(C))
with
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{3}=Z_{3}
and
\Coh^{r_{3}}_{3}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},
and we can also define
f_{23}(\sigma)(x)=x-f_{3}(x)\textnormal{ and }f_{31}(\sigma)(x)=f_{3}(x)-f_{1}(x).

Lemma 4.33
.

Let
\sigma
be as in (*), such that
f_{12}(\sigma)(0)<0.
There is a
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f_{12}(\sigma)(t)=0
if and only if
\sigma\in\Theta_{1}.

Proof 21
.

Assume that there is
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f_{12}(\sigma)(t)=0.
Since
f_{2}(t)=t,
this implies that
f_{1}(t)=t.
Let
g=(K_{t\pi},f_{t\pi})\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Since
f_{1}\circ f_{t\pi}(0)=f_{1}(t)=t
and
f_{2}\circ f_{t\pi}(0)=f_{2}(t)=t,
as a consequence
f_{12}(\sigma g)(0)=0
and it follows directly from Lemma
3.28
that
\sigma g=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1}g,\sigma_{2}g).

We now assume that
\sigma\in\Theta_{1},
then there is a
g\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
\sigma g
satisfies the gluing conditions for
\sigma_{1}g
and
\sigma_{2}g
. Without losing generality, we take
g=(K_{l\pi},f_{l\pi})\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
with
l\in\mathbb{R}
Thus,
f_{1}(l)=(f_{1}\circ f_{l\pi})(0)\geq(f_{2}\circ f_{l\pi})(0)=f_{l\pi}(0)=l.

By hypothesis
f_{12}(\sigma)(0)<0,
but also
f_{12}(\sigma)(l)\geq 0.
Since
f_{12}(\sigma)(x)
is a continuous function, there is a
t\in\mathbb{R}
that satisfies
f_{12}(\sigma)(t)=0,
as we desired.

Remark 4.34
.

[Fixed point] Since in our case
f_{12}(\sigma)(x)=f_{1}(x)-x,
we obtain that
f_{12}(\sigma)(x)=0
if and only if there is
x\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f_{1}(x)=x.
After using that the restrictions of
f_{1}
and
T
to
S^{1}
agree, the search of points
x\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f_{1}(x)=x
reduces to the study of the eigenvectors of
T.

Let
M\coloneqq T^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-A&B\\
-D&C\\
\end{bmatrix}.

We will now study the characteristic polynomial
p(x)=x^{2}-\Tr(M)x+\det(M)
of
M.
It plays an important role in determining if
\sigma\in\Theta_{i}
for some
i=1,2,3.
The sign of the discriminant
\Delta(M)=\Tr(M)^{2}-4\det(M)
of
p(x)
tells us about the existence of real eigenvalues.

Proposition 4.35
.

If
f_{12}(\sigma)(0)<1,
the discriminant of
\Delta(M)=\Tr(M)^{2}-4\det(M)
is non-negative and the eigenvalues are positive, then there is
g\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
\sigma g=(Z,\TCoh^{l}(C)),
with
l\in\mathbb{R}
and
-1<l\leq 1.
Moreover
\sigma\in\Theta_{1}.

Proof 22
.

Since
\Delta(M)\geq 0,
it guarantees the existence of real eigenvalues. By hypothesis the eigenvalues are positive. The same follows for
T_{1}.
Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
be an eigenvalue of
T_{1}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
its corresponding eigenvector. We obtain
T_{1}v=\lambda v.
Let us consider the polar coordinates of
v=(m\cos(\phi),m\sin(\phi))
with
\phi\in(-\pi,\pi]
and
