m\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

We claim now that
\sigma g\in\Theta_{1},
where
g=(K_{\phi},f_{\phi})\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}).
First of all we consider
\sigma_{1}g=(T_{1}K_{\phi},f_{1}\circ f_{\phi}).
By the correspondence between
f_{1}\circ f_{\phi}
and
T_{1}K_{\phi}
over
S^{1}
and the fact that
v
is an eigenvector, we obtain that
(f_{1}\circ f_{\phi})(0)=\phi/\pi.
Now we consider
\sigma_{2}g=(K_{\phi},f_{\phi}).
As a consequence,
f_{12}(\sigma g)=0.
By Lemma
3.36
, it is clear that
\sigma g=(Z,\TCoh^{\phi}(C))=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1}g,\sigma_{2}g).

Remark 4.36
.

Note that from Lemma
4.35
and Remark
4.34
, we obtain that if
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
satisfies (*), then
\sigma\in\Theta_{1}
if and only if the eigenvalues of
M
are positive. Moreover, by Lemma
4.33
, if
\Delta(M)<0,
then
\sigma
can never be in
\Theta_{1}.

Under the assumption that the discriminant
\Delta(M)
of
M
is non-negative and that the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
of
M
are both negative we prove the following lemmas.

Remark 4.37
.

Under the assumption that the discriminant
\Delta(M)
of
M
is non-negative and that the eigenvalues are negative, then if
\det(M+I)>0,
there are just two options, either
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}<-1\textnormal{ or }\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}>-1.

The pre-stability conditions in
\Theta_{2}
or
\Theta_{3}
satisfy that
l_{*}(\mathcal{L})
is stable for all line bundles
\mathcal{L}\in\Coh(C).
Hence, in order to determine which
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
are in
\Theta_{2}
or
\Theta_{3}
we need to study the stability of
l_{*}(\mathcal{L}).

Lemma 4.38
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
be
a pre-stability condition as in (*). Let
\mathcal{L}\in\Coh(C)
be a line bundle with
\deg(\mathcal{L})=d\leq\frac{-C}{D}
. The object
l_{*}(\mathcal{L})
is stable in
\sigma
if and only if the inequality
-Dd^{2}-(A+C)d-B>0

holds.

Proof 23
.

Since
\deg(\mathcal{L})=d\leq\frac{-C}{D}
we have
i_{*}(\mathcal{L})\in\mathcal{A}.

By considering the triangle

\textstyle{j_{*}(\mathcal{L})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{l_{*}(\mathcal{L})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{*}(\mathcal{L})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{*}(\mathcal{L})[1]}

and the correspondence between slope and phase the result follows.

Remark 4.39
.

(1)

The discriminant of the quadratic equation
Dd^{2}+(A+C)d+B
is given by
(A+C)^{2}-4BD=\Delta(M),
i.e. it has the same discriminant as
p(x).

(2)

By Remark
4.10
, if
\mathcal{L}
is a line bundle and
i_{*}(\mathcal{L})[-1]\in\mathcal{A},
then
l_{*}(\mathcal{L})
is stable.

Lemma 4.40
.

If
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}<0,
then inequality
-Dd^{2}-(A+C)d-B>0
holds for all
d\leq-\cot(\theta\pi).

Proof 24
.

Let us consider the polynomial
q(x)=Dx^{2}+(A+C)x+B.
As the discriminant of
q(x)
is also
\Delta(M)\geq 0,
we get that
q(x)
has real roots
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathbb{R}.
We assume that
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}.

It is enough to conduct the proof for
\mu_{1}\geq\frac{-C}{D}.
Indeed, if
\frac{-C}{D}\leq\mu_{1}
and
d<\frac{-C}{D}
then
q(x)<0.

As
0>2\lambda_{1}=(-A+C)-\sqrt{\Delta(M)},
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{C}{-D}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\frac{-(A+C)-\sqrt{\Delta(M)}}{2D}=\mu_{1},

as we wanted to prove.

Corollary 4.41
.

If
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}<0,
then
l_{*}(\mathcal{L})
is
\sigma
-stable for all line bundles
\mathcal{L}.
Moreover,
\sigma
is in
\Theta_{23}
and
\Theta_{31}.

Proof 25
.

This follows directly from Lemma
4.38
, Lemma
4.39
and Lemma
4.40
.

Lemma 4.42
.

(1)

There is
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f_{31}(\sigma)(t)=1
if and only if
\sigma\in\Theta_{3}.

(2)

There is a
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f_{23}(\sigma)(t)=1
if and only if
\sigma\in\Theta_{2}.

Proof 26
.

The proof goes along the lines of Lemma
4.33
.

Proposition 4.43
.

(1)

If
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}<-1,
then
\sigma\in\Theta_{3}.

(2)

If
0>\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}>-1,
then
\sigma\in\Theta_{2}.

Proof 27
.

The proof goes exactly as the one of Lemma
4.35
.

Remark 4.44
.

(1)

Note that from Proposition
4.43
and Lemma
4.42
, we obtain that if
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
satisfies (*), then
\sigma\in\Theta_{3}
if and only if the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
of
M
are
<-1.

(2)

Note that from Proposition
4.43
and Lemma
4.42
, we obtain that if
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
satisfies (*), then
\sigma\in\Theta_{2}
if and only if the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
of
M
satisfy
0>\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}>-1.

Remark 4.45
.

Note that if
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
satisfies (*), then either
\sigma\in\Theta_{1}
or
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}\cap\Theta_{23}.
Indeed, if the eigenvalues of
M
are positive, we have that
\sigma\in\Theta_{1}.
If the eigenvalues are negative, this follows from Corollary
4.41
. If
\Delta(M)<0,
it follows from Lemma
4.38
.
This remark is useful when studying
\Theta_{23}
(or
\Theta_{31}
) because either
\sigma\in\Theta_{2}
or
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}\cap\Theta_{23},
thus we can use our classification inside
\Theta_{12}
to understand
\Theta_{23}.

Remark 4.46
.

Note that if
\sigma\in\Theta_{23},
then by Proposition
4.28
, up to the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action we can find stability conditions
\sigma_{3}=(Z_{3},\Coh^{r}(C))=(T_{3},f_{3})\textnormal{ and }\sigma_{2}=(Z_{\mu},\Coh(C))\in\Stab(C)
with
\Coh^{r}_{3}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},

\Coh_{2}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},

r<0,

{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{3}=Z_{3}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{2}=Z_{\mu}.
If
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable for
x\in C
and
\Delta(M_{3})<0,
with
M_{3}\coloneqq T_{3}^{-1},
by following the steps of Lemma
4.38
we can show that
i_{*}(\mathcal{L})
is
\sigma
-stable for all line bundles
\mathcal{L}.
As a consequence, by Lemma
4.30
, we have that
\sigma
is given, up to the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action, by a pre-stability condition constructed in Lemma
3.48
. Moreover,
M_{3}=I+M,
the
M
appearing in Lemma
3.48
and
\Delta(M)=\Delta(M_{3})<0.
Analogously for the case
\sigma\in\Theta_{31}.

Theorem 4.47
.

For all pre-stability conditions
\sigma
on
\mathcal{T}_{C},
we have that

\sigma\in\Theta_{1}\cup\Theta_{2}\cup\Theta_{3}\cup\Gamma,

where

\Gamma
is the set of pre-stability conditions, which up to the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action is given by Lemma
3.48
with
\Delta(M)<0.

Moreover, note that
\Gamma\subseteq\Theta_{ij},
where
ij=12,23,31.

Proof 28
.

By Theorem
4.13
, we have that
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}\cup\Theta_{23}\cup\Theta_{31}.
By Serre duality, it suffices to check for
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}.
By Proposition
4.21
, we have that up to the action of
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
there are
\sigma_{1}=(\Coh^{r}_{1}(C),Z_{1})=(T,f)
and
\sigma_{2}=(\Coh_{2}(C),Z_{\mu})\in\Stab(C)
with
\Coh^{r}_{1}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},

\Coh(C)_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{A},
and
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{1}=Z_{1},

{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}}}_{2}=Z_{\mu}
with
f(0)\geq-1.
We reduce to the study of these pre-stability conditions, because the sets
\Theta_{i}
and
\Gamma,
for
i=1,2,3,
are defined up to the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action.
If
f(0)\geq 0,
then by Lemma
4.26

\sigma\in\Theta_{1}.
If
-1<f(0)<0,
we classify these pre-stability conditions in two sets if
\Delta(M)\geq 0
or
\Delta(M)<0.
If
\Delta(M)<0
then
\sigma\in\Gamma.
If
\Delta(M)\geq 0,
with positive eigenvalues then by Lemma
4.35
we get
\sigma\in\Theta_{1}.
If the eigenvalues are smaller than
-1,
then by Lemma
4.43
we have that
\sigma\in\Theta_{3}
and if the eigenvalues are between
0
and
-1
by Lemma
4.43
, we obtain that
\sigma\in\Theta_{2}
.

Remark 4.48
.

Note that by Lemma
4.33
and Lemma
4.42
we have that

\Theta_{i}\cap\Theta_{j}=\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\Theta_{i}\cap\Gamma=\emptyset\textnormal{ for }i,j\in\{1,2,3\}.

5.
Support property for
\mathcal{T}_{C}
, with
g(C)\geq 1

In this section, we prove the support property for the already constructed pre-stability conditions. We start by studying CP-glued pre-stability conditions
\sigma=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
on
\mathcal{T}_{C}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle,
with
\sigma_{i}=(Z_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i})\in\Stab(D^{b}(C)),
for
i=1,2.
By Theorem
2.49
there is always
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
such that
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}g.
Note that the gluing condition states precisely that
f(0)\geq 0
. Therefore, the proof of the support property falls naturally into the cases
f(0)\geq 1
and
1>f(0)\geq 0.

More precisely we reduce the proof of the support property to three cases. Namely, in Lemma
5.1
we prove it for pre-stability conditions satisfying that
f(0)\geq 1,
in Lemma
5.20
we prove it for CP-glued pre-stability condition with
1>f(0)\geq 0
and
\Delta(T^{-1})>0.
In Lemma
5.22
for CP-glued pre-stability conditions with
1>f(0)\geq 0
and negative discriminant. Finally, in Subsection
5.3
we prove it for
\sigma\in\Gamma.
We give some inequalities to study the
\sigma
-semistable objects in our pre-stability conditions. These inequalities follow closely the steps of
[
8
, Section 3]
. In Subsection
5.3
we prove the support property under the assumption that
g(C)=1,
for non-gluing pre-stability conditions with
\Delta(M)<0.
We conjecture that for
g(C)>1
the same result holds.

5.1.
Support property for CP-glued pre-stability conditions

We start by proving the support property for pre-stability conditions with a stronger orthogonality condition.

Lemma 5.1
.

If the pair
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
is a CP-glued pre-stability condition satisfying that
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{\leq 1}(i_{*}\mathcal{A}_{1},j_{*}\mathcal{A}_{2})=0
or equivalently
f(0)\geq 1,
then it satisfies the support property and in consequence it is a Bridgeland stability condition.

Proof 29
.

We use the following notation
Z_{1}([E])=Z_{1}([i^{*}(E)])
and
Z_{2}([E])=Z_{2}([j^{!}(E)]).

First note that we can linearly extend
Z
and the homomorphism induced by the exact functors
i^{*},j^{!}
to
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{T}_{C})\otimes\mathbb{R}\cong\mathbb{R}^{4}.
We define the quadratic form

Q\colon\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}})\otimes\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\textnormal{ as }Q(v)=\Im(Z_{1}(v))\Im(Z_{2}(v))+\Re(Z_{1}(v))\Re(Z_{2}(v)),

where
\Re(\alpha)
and
\Im(\alpha)
are the real and the imaginary parts of
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
respectively.

By the linearity of
Z
, it is clear that
Q
is a quadratic form.
We first show that it is negative definite on
\Ker(Z)=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\mid\Re(Z_{1}(v))=-\Re(Z_{2}(v))\textnormal{ and }\Im(Z_{1}(v))=-\Im(Z_{2}(v))\}.

Indeed, we have that
Q(v)=-\Im(Z_{1}(v))^{2}-\Re(Z_{1}(v))^{2}\leq 0,

for
v\in\Ker(Z).
Note that
Q(v)=0,
implies that
Z_{i}(v)=0,
for
i=1,2.
Then
v=0
as
\rank(\mathcal{N}(D^{b}(C))=2.

Let
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}
be a
\sigma
-semistable object. By
[
5
, Lemma A.6]
it is enough to show that
Q(E)\geq 0
for
\sigma
-stable objects. Note that
\varphi=0.
Indeed, as
\sigma
is a CP-glued pre-stability condition, by the definition of
\gl_{12}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}),
we have that
E_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
E_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}.
As
\varphi\in\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}(E_{1},E_{2})=\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(i_{*}(E_{1}),j_{*}(E_{2})[1])
and by hypothesis
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(i_{*}(E_{1}),j_{*}(E_{2})[1])=0,
we obtain that
\varphi=0.

Since
E
is
\sigma
-stable and by the definition of a CP-glued heart, we have
i_{*}(E_{1}),j_{*}(E_{2})\in\mathcal{A}.
It follows from the short exact sequence
0\rightarrow j_{*}(E_{2})\rightarrow E\rightarrow i_{*}(E_{1})\rightarrow 0

E_{1}=0
or
E_{2}=0.
As a consequence,
Q([E])=0.

We will now follow the steps of
[
8
, Section 3]
in order to study the
\sigma
-semistable objects in the CP-glued pre-stability conditions
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}),
on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
such that
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}g
where
g=(T,f)
in
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
with
f(0)=0.
Note that by the same argument as in Proposition
4.35
, these are precisely, up to the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action, the pre-stability conditions with
1>f(0)\geq 0
and positive discriminant.

We have that
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
has the following form
T^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-A&B\\
0&C\end{bmatrix},

with
C>0
and
\det(T)>0.
This implies that
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathcal{A}_{2}=\mathcal{H}.

We obtain that
\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\mathcal{H})
and
\sigma_{2}=(Z_{2},\mathcal{H}).

By definition we have

(5.1)

\displaystyle\Re(Z_{1}(w))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-A\Re(Z_{2}(w)))+B\Im(Z_{2}(w)),

\displaystyle\Im(Z_{1}(w))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C\Im(Z_{2}(w))\textnormal{ where }w\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}.

We use the following notation

(5.2)

\displaystyle d_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Re(Z_{2}([j^{!}(E]))\textnormal{, }d_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Re(Z_{2}([i^{*}(E)])),

\displaystyle r_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Im(Z_{2}([j^{!}(E)]))\textnormal{ and }r_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Im(Z_{2}([i^{*}(E)])).

Therefore, we have
Z_{2}([j^{!}(E)])=-d_{2}+ir_{2}\textnormal{ and }Z_{1}([i^{*}(E)])=Ad_{1}+Br_{1}+i(Cr_{1}).

Note that for every
E\in\mathcal{B},
we have that
r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0.
If
r_{1},r_{2}\neq 0,
we define
\mu_{\sigma}(E)=\frac{-Ad_{1}-Br_{1}+d_{2}}{Cr_{1}+r_{2}}.

We obtain the following inequalities.

Lemma 5.2
.

If
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
with
r_{1},r_{2}\neq 0
is a
\sigma
-semistable object and
[\varphi]\neq 0,
then

-B\geq(A+C)\mu_{\sigma}(E)

.

Proof 30
.

As
\mathcal{H}
is an abelian category, we can compute
\Ker(\varphi),\Coker(\varphi)\in\mathcal{H}
and by the definition of
\mathcal{A},
we obtain morphisms
E\twoheadrightarrow i_{*}(\Img(\varphi))
and
j_{*}(\Img(\varphi))\hookrightarrow E
in
\mathcal{A}.

Let
Z_{2}(\Img(\varphi))=-d^{\prime\prime}_{1}+r^{\prime\prime}_{1}i.
Note that
r^{\prime\prime}_{1}\neq 0.
Indeed, if
r^{\prime\prime}_{1}=0,
then
\phi(j_{*}(\Img(\varphi)))=1,
and by the
\sigma
-semistability
\phi(j_{*}(\Img(\varphi)))\leq\phi(E)\leq 1,
therefore
\Img(Z(E))=0,
which implies that
r_{1},r_{2}=0,
which is a contradiction.

From these short exact sequences and the correspondence between slope and phase, we obtain that

\frac{d^{\prime\prime}_{1}}{r^{\prime\prime}_{1}}\leq\mu_{\sigma}(E)\textnormal{ and }\mu_{\sigma}(E)\leq\frac{-Ad^{\prime\prime}_{1}-Br^{\prime\prime}_{1}}{Cr^{\prime\prime}_{1}}.

It follows that
\mu_{\sigma}(E)\leq\frac{-A}{C}\frac{d^{\prime\prime}_{1}}{r^{\prime\prime}_{1}}-\frac{B}{C},
then
\mu_{\sigma}(E)\leq\frac{-A}{C}\mu_{\sigma}(E)-\frac{B}{C}.
As we have that
-A,C>0,
therefore we obtain
\mu_{\sigma}(E)(A+C)\leq-B.

Example 5.3
.

Consider
\sigma_{\alpha}
as in Example
3.37
on
\mathcal{T}_{C}.
As
\sigma_{\alpha}=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{\mu}g,\sigma_{\mu})

with
g=(T,f),
where
T^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\alpha\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}

and
f(0)=0.
If there are
\sigma_{\alpha}
-semistable objects, as
A+C=0
and
-B=\alpha,
then
\alpha\geq 0
(see
[
8
, Proposition 3.13]
).

Lemma 5.4
.

If
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
with
r_{1},r_{2}\neq 0
and
[\varphi]\neq 0
is
\sigma
-semistable, then

-Ad_{1}+d_{2}-\mu_{\sigma}(E)(r_{2}-Ar_{1})\leq 0.

Proof 31
.

We clearly have

\mu_{\sigma}(E)=\frac{-Ad_{1}+d_{2}-Br_{1}}{Cr_{1}+r_{2}}=\frac{-Ad_{1}+d_{2}}{Cr_{1}+r_{2}}+\frac{-Br_{1}}{Cr_{1}+r_{2}}.

By Lemma
5.2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mu_{\sigma}(E)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{-Ad_{1}+d_{2}}{Cr_{1}+r_{2}}+(A+C)\frac{\mu_{\sigma}(E)r_{1}}{Cr_{1}+r_{2}},

\displaystyle\mu_{\sigma}(E)(r_{2}-Ar_{1})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-Ad_{1}+d_{2}.

Lemma 5.5
.

If
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
with
r_{1},r_{2}\neq 0
with
[\varphi]\neq 0
is a
\sigma
-semistable object, then

(r_{2}-r_{1})\mu_{\sigma}(E)\leq d_{2}-d_{1}

Proof 32
.

If
\Ker(\varphi)=0,
we have the exact sequence

0\rightarrow l_{*}(E_{1})\rightarrow E\rightarrow j_{*}(\Coker(\varphi))\rightarrow 0.

By the
\sigma
-semistability of
E,
we directly obtain
\mu_{\sigma}(E)\leq\frac{d_{2}-d_{1}}{r_{2}-r_{1}}.

If
\Coker(\varphi)=0,
we have the exact sequence

0\rightarrow i_{*}(\Ker(\varphi))\rightarrow E\rightarrow l_{*}(E_{2})\rightarrow 0.

We obtain

\mu_{\sigma}(E)\geq\frac{-A(d_{1}-d_{2})-B(r_{1}-r_{2})}{C(r_{1}-r_{2})}=\frac{-A(d_{1}-d_{2})}{C(r_{1}-r_{2})}+\frac{-B}{C}.

By Lemma
5.2
, we have
\mu_{\sigma}(E)\geq\mu_{\sigma}(E)+\mu_{\sigma}(E)\frac{A}{C}-\frac{A(d_{1}-d_{2})}{C(r_{1}-r_{2})}.

Since we assumed
-A,C\geq 0,
we obtain directly that

\mu_{\sigma}(E)\geq\frac{d_{1}-d_{2}}{r_{1}-r_{2}}.

As in this case
r_{1}-r_{2}\geq 0,
because
\rk(\Ker(\varphi))=r_{1}-r_{2}
and
\Ker(\varphi)\in\mathcal{H},

which implies

\mu_{\sigma}(E)(r_{2}-r_{1})\leq(d_{2}-d_{1}).

Now we assume that
\Coker(\varphi)\neq 0
and
\Ker(\varphi)\neq 0.

From the morphism
i_{*}(\Ker(\varphi))\hookrightarrow E

follows that
\frac{-Ad^{\prime}_{1}-Br^{\prime}_{1}}{Cr^{\prime}_{1}}\leq\mu_{\sigma}(E),
where
Z_{2}(\Ker(\varphi))=-d^{\prime}_{1}+r^{\prime}_{1}i.
Note that
r^{\prime}_{1}\neq 0.
If
r^{\prime}_{1}=0,
then
\phi(i_{*}(\Ker(\varphi)))=1
and by
\sigma
-semistability we have that
1=\phi(i_{*}(\Ker(\varphi)))\leq\phi(E)\leq 1,
which implies that
r_{1},r_{2}=0,
and this gives us a contradiction.

Since
\frac{-Ad^{\prime}_{1}}{Cr^{\prime}_{1}}\leq\mu_{\sigma}(E)+\frac{B}{C},

we obtain

(5.3)

-Ad^{\prime}_{1}r_{1}+\mu_{\sigma}(E)r_{2}r^{\prime}_{1}-r^{\prime}_{1}(-Ad_{1}+d_{2})\leq 0.

Note that if
r_{2}-r^{\prime\prime}_{1}\neq 0,
then from the morphism
E\twoheadrightarrow j_{*}(\Coker(\varphi))

follows that

(5.4)

\mu_{\sigma}(E)\leq\frac{d_{2}-d^{\prime\prime}_{1}}{r_{2}-r^{\prime\prime}_{1}},\\

where
Z_{2}(\Img(\phi))=-d^{\prime\prime}_{1}+ir^{\prime\prime}_{1},
as before.

As
d^{\prime\prime}_{1}=d_{1}-d^{\prime}_{1}
and
r^{\prime\prime}_{1}=r_{1}-r^{\prime}_{1},
after multiplying
(
5.4
) by
-Ar_{1}
and adding (
5.3
) we obtain

Ar_{1}(d_{2}-d_{1})-r^{\prime}_{1}(-Ad_{1}+d_{2})+\mu_{\sigma}(E)(-Ar_{1}(r_{2}-r_{1})+r^{\prime}_{1}(-Ar_{1}+r_{2}))\leq 0.

By Lemma
5.4
, we have
d_{1}-d_{2}+\mu_{\sigma}(E)(r_{2}-r_{1})\leq 0.

As a consequence, we get

\mu_{\sigma}(E)(r_{2}-r_{1})\leq d_{2}-d_{1}.

If
r_{2}-r^{\prime\prime}_{1}=0,
then we obtain that
d_{2}-d^{\prime\prime}_{1}\geq 0.

Due to the fact that
d^{\prime\prime}_{1}=d_{1}-d^{\prime}_{1}
and
r^{\prime\prime}_{1}=r_{1}-r^{\prime}_{1},
we get that

-Ar_{1}(d_{1}-d^{\prime}_{1}-d_{2})\leq 0.

Adding the last inequality with (
5.3
), we have

\displaystyle\mu_{\sigma}(E)r_{2}r^{\prime}_{1}-r^{\prime}_{1}(-Ad_{1}+d_{2})-Ar_{1}(d_{1}-d_{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 0.

By Lemma
5.4
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mu_{\sigma}(E)r_{2}r^{\prime}_{1}-r^{\prime}_{1}(\mu_{\sigma}(E)(r_{2}-Ar_{1})-Ar_{1}(d_{1}-d_{2}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 0.

As
-A>0,
we conclude that
\mu_{\sigma}(E)(r_{2}-r_{1})\leq(d_{2}-d_{1}).

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
be as above with
Z=Ad_{1}+Br_{1}-d_{2}+i(Cr_{1}+r_{2}).
If there is a
\sigma
-semistable object with
r_{2}>r_{1}>0
then
Cy+Ax\leq-B,
where
x=\frac{d_{1}}{r_{1}}
and
y=\frac{d_{2}}{r_{2}}.
Moreover, if
[\varphi]\neq 0
then
y-x\geq 0,
and

-B\in[Cy+Ax,(Ax+Cy+y-x-\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}x(A+C))\frac{r_{2}}{r_{2}-r_{1}}].

Proof 33
.

Let
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}
be a
\sigma
-semistable object. Let us consider the following short exact sequence

0\rightarrow j_{*}(E_{2})\rightarrow E\rightarrow i_{*}(E_{1})\rightarrow 0.

By the semistability of
E
and the correspondence between slope and phase it follows that

(5.5)

\frac{d_{2}}{r_{2}}\leq\mu_{\sigma}(E)\leq\frac{-Ad_{1}-Br_{1}}{Cr_{1}}.

Thus it is implied that
Cr_{1}d_{2}+Ad_{1}r_{2}\leq-\frac{B}{C}r_{1}r_{2},

as
r_{1},r_{2},C>0.
We obtain
Cy+Ax\leq-B.

As
\varphi\neq 0,
by Lemma
5.5
, we have the following inequality
\frac{-Ad_{1}-Br_{1}+d_{2}}{Cr_{1}+r_{2}}\leq\frac{d_{2}-d_{1}}{r_{2}-r_{1}}.

From this equation we obtain

-B\leq\frac{Cd_{2}r_{1}+Ad_{1}r_{2}-(C+A)d_{1}r_{1}+d_{2}r_{1}-d_{1}r_{2}}{(r_{2}-r_{1})r_{1},}.

After dividing both sides of the inequality into
r_{1}r_{2}
we get

-B\in[Cy+Ax,(Ax+Cy+y-x-\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}x(A+C))\frac{r_{2}}{r_{2}-r_{1}}].

From the equation

Cy+Ax\leq-B,

we obtain

\displaystyle Cy+Ax

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(Ax+Cy+y-x-\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}x(-A-C))\frac{r_{2}}{r_{2}-r_{1}},

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(y-x)(r_{2}+Cr_{1}).

As
r_{2}+Cr_{1}>0,
then we obtain
y-x\geq 0
.

We will now write Lemma
5.6
explicitly in terms of the
r_{1},r_{2},d_{1},d_{2}
from the Grothendieck group.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\TCoh^{\theta}(C))=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
be a pre-stability condition on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
with
\sigma_{i}=(Z_{i},\Coh^{\theta}(C))
and
\theta\in[0,1).
If
Z_{2}(d_{2},r_{2})=A_{2}d_{2}+Br_{2}+i(C_{2}r_{2}+D_{2}d_{2})
and
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}g,
were
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
and

T^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-A&B\\
0&C\end{bmatrix}

with
C>0
and there is a
\sigma
-semistable object
E\in\TCoh^{\theta}(C)
with
[E]=[r_{1},d_{1},r_{2},d_{2}]
and
C_{2}r_{2}+D_{2}d_{2}>C_{2}r_{1}+D_{2}d_{1}>0,
then
Cy+Ax\leq-B,
where
x=\frac{-A_{2}d_{1}-B_{2}r_{1}}{C_{2}r_{1}+D_{2}d_{1}}
and
y=\frac{-A_{2}d_{2}-Br_{2}}{C_{2}r_{2}+D_{2}d_{2}}.
Moreover, if
[\varphi]\neq 0
then
y-x\geq 0,
and

-B\in[Cy+Ax,(Ax+Cy+y-x-\frac{C_{2}r_{1}+D_{2}d_{1}}{C_{2}r_{2}+D_{2}d_{2}}y(A+C))\frac{C_{2}r_{2}+D_{2}d_{2}}{C_{2}r_{2}+D_{2}d_{2}-C_{2}r_{1}-D_{2}d_{1}}].

5.2.
Duality and semistability on
\mathcal{T}_{C}

The aim of this subsection is to prove an analogous statement to Lemma
5.6
for
\sigma
-stable objects
E
with
r_{1}>r_{2}>0,
where
\sigma
is a pre-stability condition on
\mathcal{T}_{C}.
We follow closely
[
22
]
to define the derived dual in this case and to show that it induces an anti-autoequivalence.

Now we consider the following functor
\mathbb{D}=R\mathcal{H}om(-,\mathcal{O}_{C})\colon D^{b}(C)^{\rm{op}}\rightarrow D^{b}(C).
By
[
31
, Section 2.3]
, we have that
\mathbb{D}
is an equivalence of categories and
\mathbb{D}^{2}=\Id.

Let us consider the functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}om(-,\mathcal{O}_{X})_{\TCoh(C)}\colon\TCoh(C)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\TCoh(C)

\displaystyle E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}om(E_{2},\mathcal{O}_{X})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{H}om(\varphi)}\mathcal{H}om(E_{1},\mathcal{O}_{X}).

Lemma 5.8
.

Every bounded object
E
in
\Kom^{b}(\TCoh(C))
is quasi-isomorphic to a complex
F\in\Kom^{b}(\TCoh(C))
of locally free sheaves in
\mathcal{T}_{C}.

Proof 34
.

It is enough to show that for
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\TCoh(C)
there is
F\in\TCoh(C)
of locally free sheaves with
F\twoheadrightarrow E.
There are locally-free sheaves
F_{i}
and surjective morphisms
F_{i}\xrightarrow{\pi_{i}}E_{i}
for
i=1,2.
Let us consider the triple
G=F_{1}\rightarrow F_{1}\oplus F_{2}
as the inclusion of the first component. Note that
F_{1}\oplus F_{2}
is also locally free. We have a surjective morphism
G\rightarrow E
in
\TCoh(C)
.

For any bounded acyclic object
E\in\Kom^{b}(\TCoh(C))
of locally free sheaves, we have that
\mathcal{H}om(E,\mathcal{O}_{C})_{\TCoh(C)}
is acyclic. As a consequence, the class of objects with locally free sheaves as components in
\TCoh(C)
is adapted for the left exact functor
\mathcal{H}om(-,\mathcal{O}_{C})_{\TCoh(C)}
and by
[
27
, Rem. 2.51]
, we can define the right derived functor
R\mathcal{H}om(-,\mathcal{O}_{C})_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}\colon\mathcal{T}_{C}^{\rm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{C}.

Proposition 5.9
.

The functor
\mathbb{D}_{1}\coloneqq R\mathcal{H}om(-,\mathcal{O}_{C})_{\TCoh(C)}\colon\mathcal{T}_{C}^{\rm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{C}
is an equivalence of categories.

Proof 35
.

Note that
\mathcal{T}_{C}^{\rm{op}}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\rm{op}},\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\rm{op}}\rangle.
Moreover, we have that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathbb{D}_{1}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\rm{op}}}}\colon\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\rm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathbb{D}_{1}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\rm{op}}}}\colon\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\rm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{2}
are equivalences of categories. As
\mathbb{D}_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{3}^{\rm{op}})\subseteq C_{3},
by
[
30
, Lemma 1.3]

we obtain that
\mathbb{D}_{1}
is an equivalence of categories.

We first study the functor
\mathbb{D}
on
D^{b}(C),
where
C
is a curve with
g(C)\geq 1.
By
[
27
, Corollary 3.40]
, we obtain that
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{C}(x))=\mathbb{C}(x)[-1]
and for a locally free sheaf
E\in\Coh(C),
we obtain that
\mathbb{D}(E)=E^{\vee}=\mathcal{H}om(E,\mathcal{O}_{C})
wich satisfies that
\deg(E^{\vee})=-\deg(E)\textnormal{ and }\rank(E)=\rank(E^{\vee}).

Moreover, in general it follows that if
E\in D^{b}(C)
and
[E]=[r,d]
then
[\mathbb{D}(E)]=[r,-d].

Let us consider the following torsion pair

Remark 5.10
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})\in\Stab(D^{b}(C)).
We have the following torsion pair on
\mathcal{A}
.

\mathcal{T}=\{E\in\mathcal{A}\mid\phi_{\sigma}(E)=1\},

\mathcal{F}=\{E\in\mathcal{A}\mid\textnormal{Its }\sigma\textnormal{-semistable factors }F_{i}\textnormal{ satisfy }\phi_{\sigma}(F_{i})<1\}.

If
\Coh^{\theta}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{C}
with
\theta\in[0,1),

we rename the torsion pair given in Remark
5.10
because
\Coh^{\theta}(C)=(T_{\sigma}^{\theta},F_{\sigma}^{\theta}).
Recall that by Remark
2.52
, we also have that
\Coh^{\theta}(C)=(\mathcal{F}_{\theta}[1],\mathcal{T}_{\theta})
where
\mathcal{T}_{\theta}=\mathcal{P}_{\mu}(\theta,1]
and
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}=\mathcal{P}_{\mu}(0,\theta]
in
\Coh(C).

Definition 5.11
.

Let
\sigma=(Z(r,d)=Ad+Br+i(Cr+Dd),\Coh^{\theta}(C))=\Stab(C).
We define
\sigma^{\vee}\coloneqq(Z(r,d)=Ad-Br+i(Cr-Dd),\Coh^{1-\theta}(C)[-1])\in\Stab(C).

Remark 5.12
.

(1)

If we have
\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\Coh^{\theta}(C))
and
\sigma_{2}=(Z_{2},\Coh^{\theta}(C))
with
0\leq\theta<1,
then
F_{\sigma_{1}}^{\theta}=F^{\theta}_{\sigma_{2}}.
Therefore, for any stability condition
\sigma
with heart
\Coh^{\theta}(C),
we denote
F^{\theta}_{\sigma}
by
F^{\theta}.

(2)

Note that
\mathbb{D}(\Coh^{\theta}(C))\neq\Coh^{1-\theta}(C)[-1].

(3)

\sigma^{\vee}_{\mu}=\sigma_{\mu}.

(4)

\mu_{\sigma}(E)=-\mu_{{\sigma}^{\vee}}(\mathbb{D}(E)).

(5)

Let
\sigma=(Z,\Coh^{\theta}(C)),
then
\mathbb{D}(F^{\theta})=F^{1-\theta}[-1],
where
F^{1-\theta}[-1]
is given with respect to
\sigma^{\vee}.

Example 5.13
.

If
\sigma_{\mu}=(Z_{\mu},\Coh(C)),
the torsion pair
T^{0}
and
F^{0}
is given precisely by the subcategory of torsion sheaves and torsion-free sheaves respectively. We have that
\mathbb{D}(\Coh(C))=\langle\mathbb{C}(x)[-1],\mathcal{L}\rangle,
for any line bundle
\mathcal{L}
in
\Coh(C)
and any point
x\in C.
Note that
\langle\mathbb{C}(x)[-1],\mathcal{L}\rangle
is a heart of a bounded t-structure on
D^{b}(C)
which does not admit a stability function. Moreover, we trivially have that
\mathbb{D}(F^{0})=F^{0}.

We now study
\mathbb{D}_{1}
on
\mathcal{T}_{C}.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\TCoh^{\theta}(C))=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
be a pre-stability on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
with
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}g
where
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
f(0)=0
and
T^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-A&B\\
0&C\end{bmatrix}.

Definition 5.14
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\TCoh^{\theta}(C))=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}),
we define the dual stability condition on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
as
\sigma^{*}=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{2}^{\vee},\sigma_{1}^{\vee})=(Z^{*},\TCoh^{1-\theta}(C)[-1]).

Remark 5.15
.

(1)

Let
\sigma=(Z,\TCoh^{\theta}(C))
be a pre-stability condition as above. If
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}
is
\sigma
-stable with
\phi_{\sigma}(E)<1,
i.e.
\Im(Z(E))\neq 0,
then
E_{1},E_{2}\in F^{\theta}.

(2)

Let
E=E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}\in\TCoh^{\theta}(C),
with
E_{1},E_{2}\in F^{\theta}

then
\mu_{\sigma}(E)=-\mu_{\sigma*}(\mathbb{D}_{1}(E)).
Moreover, we also have that

\sigma^{*}=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{2}^{\vee},\sigma^{\vee}_{2}gN_{\frac{2B}{C}}).

(3)

If
\sigma=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{\mu}g,\sigma_{\mu}),
with
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
where

T^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}1&-\alpha\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}

and
f(0)=0,

then
\sigma^{*}=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{\mu},\sigma_{\mu}g^{\prime})
with
g^{\prime}=gN_{-2\alpha}.
Note that
g^{\prime}=g^{-1}.

By the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action, an object
E\in\TCoh(C)
is
\sigma^{*}
-stable if and only if it is
\sigma^{*}g
-stable, where
\sigma^{*}g=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{\mu}g,\sigma_{\mu}).

Lemma 5.16
.

Let us consider
\sigma=(Z,\TCoh^{\theta}(C))=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}).
An object
E\in\TCoh^{\theta}(C)
is
\sigma
-stable with
\phi_{\sigma}(E)<1
if and only if
\mathbb{D}_{1}(E)
is
\sigma^{*}
-stable and
\phi_{\sigma^{*}}(\mathbb{D}_{1}(E))<1.

Proof 36
.

As in Remark
5.15
we have that
E_{1},E_{2}\in F^{\theta}.
By Remark
5.12
, it follows that
\mathbb{D}(E_{1}),\mathbb{D}(E_{2})
are in
F^{1-\theta}[-1].
Then
\mathbb{D}_{1}(E)=\mathbb{D}(E_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(E_{1})\in\TCoh(C)^{1-\theta}[-1]\textnormal{ and }\phi_{\sigma*}(\mathbb{D}_{1}(E))<1.

Let us consider
Q=Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{2}\in\TCoh^{1-\theta}(C)[-1],
satisfying
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in F^{1-\theta}[-1]
and

0\rightarrow G\rightarrow\mathbb{D}_{1}(E)\rightarrow Q\rightarrow 0

a short exact sequence in
\TCoh^{1-\theta}(C)[-1],
where
G=G_{1}\rightarrow G_{2}.
Note that it is enough to prove that
\phi_{\sigma*}(\mathbb{D}_{1}(E))<\phi_{\sigma*}(Q)
to show the stability of
\mathbb{D}_{1}(E).
Indeed, if
P=P_{1}\rightarrow P_{2}
is an arbitrary quotient of
\mathbb{D}_{1}(E),
either there is an object
Q=Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{2}
with
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in F^{1-\theta}[-1]
with
P\twoheadrightarrow Q
and
\phi_{\sigma*}(Q)<\phi_{\sigma^{*}}(P)
or
\phi_{\sigma^{*}}(P)=1
and we trivially obtain that
\phi_{\sigma^{*}}(\mathbb{D}_{1}(E))<\phi_{\sigma^{*}}(P).

We also have that
G_{1},G_{2}\in F^{1-\theta}[-1]
as
F^{1-\theta}[-1]
is closed under subobjects. Moreover, the duality gives us a correspondence between short exact sequences on
F^{1-\theta}[-1]
and
F^{\theta}.

We obtain a short exact sequence in
\TCoh^{\theta}(C)
given by
0\rightarrow\mathbb{D}_{1}(Q)\rightarrow E\rightarrow\mathbb{D}_{1}(G)\rightarrow 0.
By the stability of
E
we obtain that

\mu_{\sigma}(\mathbb{D}_{1}(Q))<\mu_{\sigma}(E)
and it follows if and only if
\mu_{\sigma*}(\mathbb{D}_{1}(E))<\mu_{\sigma*}(Q).
As a consequence,
\mathbb{D}_{1}(E)
is
\sigma^{*}
-stable.

Let
\sigma=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{2}g,\sigma_{2})=(Z,\TCoh^{\theta}(C))
as above and
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}
a
\sigma
-stable object with
\phi(E)<1,

\varphi\neq 0
and
0<C_{2}r_{2}+Dd_{2}<C_{2}r_{1}+D_{2}d_{1},
where
[E]=(r_{1},d_{1},r_{2},d_{2}).
Note that we cannot apply Lemma
5.7
directly.

Note that
\sigma*=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{2}^{\vee},\sigma_{2}^{\vee}gN_{\frac{2B}{C}}).
After applying the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action and by Lemma
5.16
, we have that
E
is
\sigma
-stable if and only if
\mathbb{D}_{1}(E)
is
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma^{*}g^{\prime}=(\sigma_{2}^{\vee}g^{\prime},\sigma^{\vee}_{2})
-stable, where

g^{\prime}=N_{\frac{-2B}{C}}g^{-1}=(T^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})

and
T^{\prime-1}=\frac{1}{\det(T^{-1})}\begin{bmatrix}C&B\\
0&-A\end{bmatrix}.

If
\sigma_{2}=(Z_{2}(r,d)=A_{2}d_{2}+B_{2}r_{2}+i(C_{2}r_{2}+D_{2}d_{2}),\Coh^{\theta}(C)),

then

\sigma^{\vee}_{2}=(Z_{2}^{\vee}(r,d)=A_{2}d_{2}-B_{2}r_{2}+i(C_{2}r_{2}-D_{2}d_{2}),\Coh^{1-\theta}[-1](C)).

If we apply Lemma
5.7
to
\sigma^{\prime},
we have that if
F=F_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{F}}F_{2}
is
\sigma
-stable with

C_{2}\rk(F_{2})-D_{2}\deg(F_{2})>C_{2}\rk(F_{1})-D_{2}\deg(F_{1})

and
[\varphi_{F}]\neq 0,

then

\frac{-A_{2}\deg(F_{1})+B_{2}\rk(F_{1})}{C_{2}\rk(F_{1})-D_{2}\deg(F_{1})}\leq\frac{-A_{2}\deg(F_{2})+B_{2}\rk(F_{2})}{C_{2}\rk(F_{2})-D_{2}\deg(F_{2})}.

Note that
[\mathbb{D}_{1}(E)]=[r_{2},-d_{2},r_{1},-d_{1}].
As a consequence, we obtain that

\mathbb{D}_{1}(E)
satisfies

C_{2}\rk(\mathbb{D}(E_{1}))-D_{2}\deg(\mathbb{D}(E_{1}))>C_{2}\rk(\mathbb{D}(E_{2}))-D_{2}\deg(\mathbb{D}(E_{2})),

because
C_{2}r_{2}+Dd_{2}<C_{2}r_{1}+D_{2}r_{1}.
We can now apply Lemma
5.17
to
\sigma^{\prime}
and we obtain

\frac{A_{2}d_{2}+B_{2}r_{2}}{C_{2}r_{2}+D_{2}d_{2}}\leq\frac{A_{2}d_{1}+B_{2}r_{1}}{C_{2}r_{1}+D_{2}d_{1}},

which is precisely

\frac{-A_{2}d_{1}-B_{2}r_{1}}{C_{2}r_{1}+D_{2}d_{1}}\leq\frac{-A_{2}d_{2}-B_{2}r_{2}}{C_{2}r_{1}+D_{2}d_{2}}.

Moreover, we obtain the following result, which gives us the necessary conditions to have
\sigma
-stable objects.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\TCoh^{\theta}(C))=\gl_{12}(\sigma,\sigma_{2})
be a pre-stability condition on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
with
Z_{2}(d_{2},r_{2})=A_{2}d_{2}+Br_{2}+i(C_{2}r_{2}+D_{2}r_{2})
and
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}g,
were
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
and

T^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-A&B\\
0&C\end{bmatrix}.

If there is a
\sigma
-semistable object with
C_{2}r_{1}+D_{2}d_{1}>C_{2}r_{2}+D_{2}d_{2}>0
then
Cy+Ax\leq-B,
where
x=\frac{-A_{2}d_{1}-B_{2}r_{1}}{C_{2}r_{1}+D_{2}d_{1}}
and
y=\frac{-A_{2}d_{2}-Br_{2}}{C_{2}r_{2}+D_{2}d_{2}}.

Moreover, if
[\varphi]\neq 0
then
y-x\geq 0
and

-B\in[Cy+Ax,Ax+Cy+y-x-\frac{C_{2}r_{2}+D_{2}d_{2}}{C_{2}r_{1}+D_{2}d_{1}}y(A+C)\frac{C_{2}r_{1}+D_{2}d_{1}}{C_{2}r_{1}+D_{2}d_{1}-C_{2}r_{2}-D_{2}d_{2}}].

Corollary 5.18
.

If
\sigma_{\alpha}
as defined in Example
3.37
on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
and
E
is a
\alpha
-stable object with
[E]=(r_{1}.d_{1},r_{2},d_{2})
and
r_{1}\neq r_{2},
then
\alpha\in[\frac{d_{2}}{r_{2}}-\frac{d_{1}}{r_{1}},(\frac{d_{2}}{r_{2}}-\frac{d_{1}}{r_{1}})(1+\frac{r_{1}+r_{2}}{|r_{2}-r_{1}|})].

Remark 5.19
.

Note that Corollary
5.18
agrees with the necessary conditions for the existence of
\sigma_{\alpha}
-stable objects
[
8
, Theorem. 6.1]
.

We will now prove the support property for this type of pre-stability conditions.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
be a pre-stability condition, such that there is
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}g
and
T^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-A&B\\
0&C\\
\end{bmatrix}.
Then
\sigma
satisfies the support property and therefore it is a Bridgeland stability condition.

Proof 37
.

First note that we can linearly extend
Z
to
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{T}_{C})\otimes\mathbb{R}\cong\mathbb{R}^{4}.

We denote
Z_{1}([E])=Z_{1}([i^{*}(E)])
and
Z_{2}([E])=Z_{2}([j^{!}(E)]).
As mentioned before, since
i^{*}
and
j^{!}
are exact, they induce homomorphisms on the Grothendieck groups. We use the notation given above for elements
v\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{T}_{C})\otimes\mathbb{R},
i.e.
Z_{1}(v)
and
Z_{2}(v).

We claim that for
\delta=\frac{-CA}{B^{2}}>0,
the pre-stability condition
\sigma
satisfies the support property with the quadratic form
Q\colon\mathbb{R}^{4}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},
defined as

Q(v)=-\Re(Z_{1}(v))\Im(Z_{2}(v)+\Im(Z_{1}(v))\Re(Z_{2}(v))+\Im(Z_{1}(v))\Im(Z_{2}(v))+\delta(\Re(Z_{1}(v))\Re(Z_{2}(v)),

where
\Re(\alpha)
and
\Im(\alpha)
are the real and the imaginary parts respectively and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{4}.

Note that under the notation of equation (
5.2
),
we obtain that

Q(v)=-Ad_{1}r_{2}-Cd_{2}r_{1}-Br_{1}r_{2}+Cr_{1}r_{2}+\delta(-Ad_{1}d_{2}-Bd_{2}r_{1}).

We first show that it is negative definite on

\Ker(Z)=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\mid\Re(Z_{1}(v))=-\Re(Z_{2}(v))\textnormal{ and }\Im(Z_{1}(v))=-\Im(Z_{2}(v))\}.

In fact, we get that

\displaystyle Q(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Re(Z_{2}(v))\Im(Z_{2}(v))-\Im(Z_{2}(v))\Re(Z_{2}(v))-\Im(Z_{2}(v))\Im(Z_{2}(v))-\delta(\Re(Z_{2}(v))\Re(Z_{2}(v))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Im(Z_{2}(v))^{2}-\delta(\Re(Z_{2}(v))^{2})<0

if
v\neq 0.
Note that if
Q(v)=0,
for
v\in\Ker(Z),
then
Z_{i}(v)=0
for
i=1,2.
It follows that
v=0,
as
\rank(\mathcal{N}(D^{b}(C)))=2.

Let
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}
be a
\sigma
-stable object. We consider the following short exact sequence
0\rightarrow j_{*}(E)\rightarrow E\rightarrow i_{*}(E)\rightarrow 0.
If
[\varphi]=0,
Remark
3.14
implies that either
E_{1}=0
or
E_{2}=0,
as
E
is indecomposable. If
E_{1}=0
or
E_{2}=0,
we clearly have that
Q([E])=0.

If
\Im(Z_{1}(E))=0,
it follows that
-\Re(Z_{1}(E))>0.
We claim that
\Im(Z_{2}(E))=0.
Indeed, after considering the decomposition of
E_{2}
with respect to the standard torsion pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
given in Remark
5.10
on
\mathcal{A},
we have that there is a subtriple
T_{2}=0\rightarrow T(E_{2})
of
E,
such that
\Im(Z_{2}(T_{2}))=0.
If
T_{2}\neq 0,
then

1=\phi(T_{2})\leq\phi(E)\leq 1,
which implies that
\phi(E)=1,
thus
\Im(Z_{2}(E))=0.

If
T_{2}=0,
then
E_{2}\in F
and the morphism
\varphi=0,
as
E_{1}
is in
T.
This implies that
E_{1}\rightarrow 0
is a subtriple and once again we obtain that

1=\phi(E_{1}\rightarrow 0)\leq\phi(E)\leq 1.

So,
\phi(E)=1
and
\Im(Z_{2}(E))=0
and this implies that
E=0.
We obtain that
\Im(Z_{2}(E))=0
and
-\Re(Z_{2}(E))>0.
Hence, we get
Q(E)=\delta(\Re(Z_{1}(E))\Re(Z_{2}(E)))>0.

If
\Im(Z_{2}(E))=0,
then
-\Re(Z_{2}(E))>0.
By the short exact sequence above, we obtain that

1=\phi(0\rightarrow E_{2})\leq\phi(E_{1}\rightarrow 0)\leq 1.

This implies
\Im(Z_{1}(E))=0
and
-\Re(Z_{1}(E))>0.
As a consequence, we get

Q(E)=\delta(\Re(Z_{1}(E))\Re(Z_{2}(E)))>0.

Now we assume
\Im(Z_{1}(E)),\Im(Z_{2}(E))\neq 0
and therefore
[\varphi]\neq 0

Let
y=\frac{-\Re(Z_{2}(E)))}{\Im(Z_{2}(E))}
and
x=\frac{-\Re(Z_{2}(j_{*}i^{*}(E))}{\Im(Z_{2}(j_{*}i^{*}(E)))}.
Note that
\Im(Z_{1}(E))\neq 0,
implies
\Im(Z_{2}(j_{*}i^{*}(E)))\neq 0.

After dividing
Q([E])
into
\Im(Z_{2}(E))\Im(Z_{2}(j_{*}i^{*}(E)))
and by Equation (
5.1
), we obtain that
Q(v)\geq 0
if and only if

-Ax-Cy-B+C+\delta(-Axy-By)\geq 0.

First note that by the semistability of
E
and Lemma
5.6
we have that
Cy+Ax\leq-B
and
0\leq y-x.

Our proof falls naturally into two cases:

Case 1
:
y\geq 0.

As
C>0,
we have
0\leq Cy\leq-Ax-B.
Then
-Axy-By\geq 0
and since
-Ax-Cy-B+C\geq 0,
we get that
Q([E])\geq 0.

Case 2
If
y<0,
then
x<y<0.
Moreover if
-Ax-B\leq 0,
we have that
-Axy-By\geq 0,
and we argue as before. If
0<-Ax-B,
as
-A>0
we get
0<-Ax-B\leq-Ay-B<-B.
Then
Cy\leq-Ax-B\leq-B
and
\frac{B}{-A}<y<0.
Let us consider the following function

\displaystyle f\colon(0,-B)\times(\frac{B}{-A},0)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\frac{-Ax-Cy-B+C}{-Axy-By}.

Note
B\leq-Ax-Cy,
then
0<C\leq-Ax-Cy-B+C.
Also
\frac{B^{2}}{A}<y(-Ax-B),
as
xB>0.
Therefore, we obtain
\frac{-A}{-B^{2}}>\frac{1}{y(x+\alpha)}.
It follows that
f(x,y)<\frac{C}{y(x+\alpha)}<\frac{-CA}{-B^{2}}.

Since
\delta=\frac{-CA}{B^{2}},
we have that
f(x,y)<-\delta.
Hence, we get
-Ax-Cy-B+C+\delta(-Axy-By)>0.

Support property for CP-glued pre-stability conditions with negative discriminant

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
be a pre-stability condition, where
\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\mathcal{A}_{1})
and
\sigma_{2}=(Z_{2},\mathcal{A}_{2}),
such that there is
g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}g
where
(T,f)
satisfies that
M\coloneqq T^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-A&B\\
-D&C\\
\end{bmatrix}

with

\Delta(M)=\Tr(M)^{2}-4\det(M)=(A+C)^{2}-4BD<0\textnormal{ and }0<f(0)<1.

Remark 5.21
.

If
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}
is a
\sigma
-semistable object with

\Im(Z_{2}(E_{1}))<0.
Then
E_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}[1].

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
be as above. Then
\sigma
is a Bridgeland stability condition.

Proof 38
.

We just need to prove that it satisfies the support property. Note that we can extend
Z
to
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}})\otimes\mathbb{R}\cong\mathbb{R}^{4}.
We claim that
\sigma
satisfies the support property with respect to the quadratic form
Q\colon\mathbb{R}^{4}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined as

Q(v)=-\Im(Z_{2}(j^{!}(v))))\Re(Z_{2}(i^{*}(v)))+\Im(Z_{2}(i^{*}v)))\Re(Z_{2}(j^{!}(v)).

We use the following notation

d_{1}=-\Re(Z_{2}(i^{*}(v)))\textnormal{ and }r_{1}=\Im(Z_{2}(i^{*}(v))),

d_{2}=-\Re(Z_{2}(j^{!}(v)))\textnormal{ and }r_{2}=\Im(Z_{2}(j^{!}(v))).

Note that
r_{2}\geq 0.
We first show that
Q
is negative definite on

\Ker(Z)=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\mid\Re(Z_{2}(j^{!}v))=-\Re(Z_{1}(i^{*}v))\textnormal{ and }\Im(Z_{2}(i^{*}v))=-\Im(Z_{1}(j^{!}v))\}.

Note that
Z_{1}(w)=-A\Re(Z_{2}(w))+B\Im(Z_{2}(w))+i(-D\Re(Z_{2}(w))+C\Im(Z_{2}(w))),
with
w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.
We get that
Q(v)=-Dd_{1}^{2}+(A+C)d_{1}r_{1}+Br_{1}^{2}<0.
Indeed, as
0\leq f(0)=t<1
and
t<1<t+1,
we have
D>0.
Moreover since
\Delta(M)=(A+C)^{2}-4BD<0,
then
B>0
and
-Dd^{2}-(A+C)dr-Br^{2}<0
for all
(r,d)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.
If
(r,d)=0,
we obtain that
v=0.

Now let
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
be a
\sigma
-stable object. We show that
Q([E])\geq 0.
First of all, if
[E]=v\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}})
with
\Im(Z_{2}(v))=0,
then
\Re(Z_{2}(v))<0
and
after considering the following short exact sequence
j_{*}(E_{2})\rightarrow E\rightarrow i_{*}(E_{1})\rightarrow j_{*}(E_{2})[1]
in
\mathcal{A},
we obtain

1=\phi(0\rightarrow E_{2})\leq\phi(E_{1}\rightarrow 0)\leq 1.

Then
\phi(E_{1}\rightarrow 0)=1,
and this implies that
Cr_{1}+Dd_{1}=0
and
r_{1}\leq 0.
As
-d_{2}<0,
we get

Q([E])=-d_{2}r_{1}\geq 0.

If
r_{1}=0,
as
Cr_{1}+Dd_{1}\geq 0,
then
Dd_{1}\geq 0
and
d_{1}\geq 0.

Therefore, we have
Q([E])=d_{1}r_{2}\geq 0.

Let us now assume that
r_{2},r_{1}\neq 0.
From the short exact sequence mentioned above and the correspondence between slope and phase, we obtain

\frac{d_{2}}{r_{2}}\leq\frac{-Ad_{1}+Br_{1}+d_{2}}{Cr_{1}+Dd_{1}+r_{2}}\textnormal{ i.e.\ }-Dd_{1}d_{2}-Cr_{1}d_{2}-Ad_{1}r_{2}-Br_{1}r_{2}\geq 0.

We define
x=\frac{d_{1}}{r_{1}}
and
y=\frac{d_{2}}{r_{2}}.
Let us consider two cases:

Case 1:

r_{1}>0.
Since by definition
Cr_{1}+Dd_{1}\geq 0,
then
C+Dx\geq 0.

Moreover we have
C+Dx>0.
Indeed, if
C+Dx=0,
we get
E_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}[1]
and
r_{1}\leq 0,
which contradicts our assumption. Then, we obtain

y\leq\frac{-Ax-B}{Dx+C}.

As
\Delta(M)<0
and
D>0,
then for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have that
Dt^{2}+(A+C)t+B>0.

Thus, we obtain
x>\frac{-Ax+B}{Dx+C}.
Finally, we get
x>y
or equivalently
Q([E])=d_{1}r_{2}-d_{2}r_{1}>0.

Case 2:

r_{1}<0.
We claim that
[\varphi]=0.
Indeed, by Remark
5.21
, we have that
E_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}[1].
Then
\varphi\in\Hom_{D^{b}(\mathcal{A})}(E_{1},E_{2})=0,
as
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is the heart of a bounded t-structure in
D^{b}(\mathcal{A}).

This implies that
E_{1}=0
or
E_{2}=0,
and as a consequence,
Q([E])=0.

We will now prove that all CP-glued pre-stability conditions on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
satisfy the support property.

Proposition 5.23
.

If the pair
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
is a pre-stability condition with
{\sigma=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}),}
then it satisfies the support property and therefore, it is a Bridgeland stability condition.

Proof 39
.

There is
{g=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})}
with
{(Z_{1},\mathcal{A}_{1})=\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}g}
and
\sigma_{2}=(Z_{2},\mathcal{A}_{2}).
The proof falls naturally into the following cases:

Case 1
: If
f(0)\geq 1,
then this follows directly from Lemma
5.1
.

Case 2
: If
0\leq f(0)<1
and
\Delta(M)\geq 0,
then we have the existence of real eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R}.
If
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}>0,
then by Lemma
5.20
and the fact that the support property is stable under the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action, we have that
\sigma
has the support property. If
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}<0,
then by arguments along the lines of Proposition
4.43
there is
h\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
such that
\sigma h=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1}h,\sigma_{2}h)
with
\sigma_{i}h=(Z^{\prime}_{i},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{i})
and

\Hom^{\leq 1}(i_{*}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{1},j_{*}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{2})=0,
therefore it follows from
Lemma
5.1
.

Case 3:
If
0<f(0)<1
and
\Delta(M)<0,
then this case follows directly from Lemma
5.22
.

Remark 5.24
.

By Remark
2.46
, after applying Serre duality, we have that any pre-stability condition
\sigma\in\Theta_{i},
with
i\in\{1,2,3\}
satisfies the support property. By Theorem
4.47
, we just need to prove the
support property for
\sigma\in\Gamma.

We will now prove the support property for
\sigma\in\Gamma
just when
g(C)=1.
For
g(C)>1,
we conjecture that it is also satisfied. We start by studying the
\sigma
-semistable objects in the pre-stability conditions in Lemma
3.48
under the assumption that
g(C)\geq 1.

Semistability on non-gluing pre-stability conditions

We will now study
\sigma
-semistable objects, where
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
satisfies that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are
\sigma
-stable and
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable of phase one. After applying the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action on
\sigma
the objects
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),

j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are always
\sigma
-stable. We use the description of the hearts given in Lemma
4.18
, Lemma
4.19
and Lemma
4.20
to prove that all
\sigma
-semistable objects have a particular form.

Lemma 5.25
.

If
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
is
\sigma
-semistable then

E\in\TCoh(C)
or
E\in\mathcal{H}_{23}[-1]
or
E\in\mathcal{H}_{31}[-1].

Proof 40
.

First note that there are elements
g_{1},g_{2}\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
\delta_{i}=\sigma g_{i}=(W_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i})
for
i=1,2,
such that
\delta_{1}
satisfies that
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\delta_{1}
-stable of phase one,
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are
\delta_{1}
-stable and
\delta_{2}
satisfies that
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\delta_{2}
-stable of phase one and
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are
\delta_{2}
-stable.
We have
E\in\mathcal{A}.
Note that Lemma
4.18
describes the cohomology of
E.
For an object
G=G_{1}\xrightarrow{\psi}G_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{1},
Lemma
4.19
describes its cohomology and if
G=G_{1}\xrightarrow{\psi}G_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2},
in Lemma
4.20
we obtain the description of its cohomology.

As
E
is
\delta_{i}
-semistable we have that
E\in\mathcal{B}_{i}[n],
for
i=1,2
and
n\in\mathbb{Z},
where the only possible cases are
n=1,0,-1,-2.
We study the non-trivial cases.

(1)

If
E\in\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}[-2]\cap\mathcal{B}_{2}[-1],
as the intersection is contained in
\Coh_{3}(C)[-1],
then
E\in\mathcal{H}_{23}[-1].

(2)

If
E\in\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}[-1]\cap\mathcal{B}_{2}[-1],
then
E\in\mathcal{H}_{23}[-1].

(3)

If
E\in\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}[-1]\cap\mathcal{B}_{2},
then
E\in\mathcal{H}_{31}[-1].

(4)

If
E\in\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}\cap\mathcal{B}_{2}[-1],
as the intersection is contained in
\Coh_{2}(C),
then
E\in\TCoh(C).

(5)

If
E\in\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}\cap\mathcal{B}_{2},
then
E\in\TCoh(C).

(6)

If
E\in\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}\cap\mathcal{B}_{2}[1],
as the intersection is contained in
\Coh_{1}(C),
then
E\in\TCoh(C).

5.3.
Support property for non-gluing pre-stability conditions with negative discriminant and
g=1

We consider pre-stability conditions
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
as constructed in Lemma
3.48
with
\Delta<0,
i.e. there is
\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\Coh_{1}^{r_{1}}(C))=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
\Coh_{1}^{r_{1}}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\Coh_{1}^{r_{1}}(C)}}=Z_{1},
with
-1<f(0)<0.
We assume
\Delta(M)<0,
where
M=T^{-1}.
Under the assumption that
g(C)=1,
these pre-stability conditions satisfy the support property and as a consequence, they are Bridgeland stability conditions.

Since
\Delta(M)<0,
after applying the
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action we never obtain a CP-glued pre-stability condition, because
f(\theta)<\theta
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}.
However, under the assumption
g=1,
the quadratic form induced by the Euler bilinear form
-\chi(E,E)=d_{2}r_{1}-d_{1}r_{2}
is negative definite on
\Ker(Z)
as in Lemma
5.22
.
Therefore, it is a good candidate for being a quadratic form appearing in the support property.

We will now prove some useful statements about
\mathcal{A}.

Lemma 5.26
.

If
F\in\Coh(C)
is
\mu
-stable, then
i_{*}(F),

j_{*}(F)
and
l_{*}(F)
are
\sigma
-stable.

Proof 41
.

The proof goes along the lines of Proposition
4.5
.
If
F
is
\mu
-stable, it is either a skyscraper sheaf of a torsion-free sheaf. In Proposition
4.5
we proved the statement for skyscraper sheaves, therefore we assume that
F
is torsion-free and
r>0.

Let us assume that
i_{*}(F)
is not
\sigma
-semistable. Then, we consider the last triangle of its Harder–Narasimhan filtration
E\rightarrow i_{*}(F)\rightarrow A\rightarrow E[1]

which satisfies
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}^{\leq n}(E,A)=0,
with
n\leq 0.
By Lemma
4.1
we have that
\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}(E_{1},A_{1})=0
and
E_{1},A_{1}\in\Coh(C).
By Serre duality on
C
, we get
\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}(A_{1},E[1])=0,
which implies that the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow E_{1}\rightarrow F\rightarrow A_{1}\rightarrow 0

splits. As
F
is
\mu
-stable, it is indecomposable. Therefore, either
E_{1}=0
or
A_{1}=0.
Following exactly the same steps as in Lemma
4.5
with
X=F,
we show that
A_{1}=0
and
j_{*}(F),l_{*}(F)
are
\sigma
-stable.
As in Lemma
4.7
, the last triangle of the HN-filtration of
i_{*}(F)
is given by

l_{*}(F)\rightarrow i_{*}(F)\rightarrow j_{*}(F)[1]\rightarrow l_{*}(F)[1]

and this implies that

\phi(l_{*}(F))>\phi(j_{*}(F))+1.
As
j_{*}(F)\in\Coh_{2}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},

then
1<\phi(j_{*}(F))+1\leq 2,
which implies that
1<\phi(l_{*}(F)).

Moreover, note that
\phi(l_{*}(F))<2.
By the stability of
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
we have that
1<\phi_{4}<2
and we also have a non-zero morphism
l_{*}(F)\rightarrow l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)).

By Lemma
3.50
we have that
\Coh^{r_{3}}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A},
so that
l_{*}(F)[-1]\in\mathcal{A}.

Moreover, by the correspondence between slope and phase, we have that

\phi(j_{*}(F))=\frac{d}{r}<\frac{Ad+Br-d}{-Cr-Dd-r}=\phi(l_{*}(F)[-1]),

which implies
-Dd^{2}-(A+C)dr-Br^{2}<0
and induces a contradiction, because
\Delta(M)=(A+C)^{2}-4BD<0
and
D,B<0.
Therefore, we obtain that
i_{*}(F)
is
\sigma
-semistable.
Now we assume that
i_{*}(F)
is strictly-semistable, by Remark
4.10
we obtain exactly the same contradiction. Therefore, we get that
i_{*}(F)
is
\sigma
-stable. Analogously, we prove that
j_{*}(F)
and
l_{*}(F)
are
\sigma
-stable.

Lemma 5.27
.

If
F\in\Coh(C)
is
\mu
-semistable, then
i_{*}(F),

j_{*}(F)
and
l_{*}(F)
are
\sigma
-semistable.

Proof 42
.

If
F=\mathbb{C}(x),
the statement is already proved by Lemma
4.16
. Therefore, we assume that
F
is torsion-free. Let us consider a JH-filtration of
F
with respect to
\mu.
Note that all the
\mu
-stable factors
A_{i},
for
i=0,\cdots,n,
have the same slope
\mu(F).
By Lemma
5.26
, we obtain that
j_{*}(A_{i})
is
\sigma
-stable in
\mathcal{A}.
As
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\Coh_{2}(C)}}=Z_{\mu},
we have that
\phi(j_{*}(A_{i}))=\phi(j_{*}(F))=\lambda,
with
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}.
Since the category
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)
is closed under extensions, we obtain that
j_{*}(F)
is
\sigma
-semistable.
Note that since
F
is
\mu
-semistable, then
i_{*}(F)
is in
\mathcal{A}
or in
\mathcal{A}[1]
and
l_{*}(F)
is in
\mathcal{A}
or in
\mathcal{A}[1].
Analogously, the same conclusion can be drawn for
i_{*}(F)
and
l_{*}(F).

Remark 5.28
.

Note that
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{C}_{2}=\Coh_{2}(C).

Lemma 5.29
.

Let
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}\in\TCoh(C)
be a
\sigma
-semistable in
\mathcal{A}
. Then
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,E[2])=0.

Proof 43
.

First note that
E\in\TCoh(C)\cap\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{F},
where
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{T}[-1])
as in Lemma
4.18
. By Serre duality
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,E[2])=\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E[2],\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E))^{*}=\Hom(E[1],E_{2}\rightarrow C(\varphi))^{*}.
It suffices to prove that
\Hom(E[1],E_{2}\rightarrow C(\varphi))=0.

Let us consider the triangle

i_{*}(C(\varphi))\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E)[-1]\rightarrow j_{*}(E_{2})\rightarrow i_{*}(C(\varphi))[1].
Applying
\Hom
induces a long exact sequence and as a consequence, it is enough to prove that
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E[1],i_{*}(E_{2}))=0\textnormal{ and }\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E[1],j_{*}(C(\varphi)))=0.

We have
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E[1],i_{*}(E_{2}))=\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}(E_{1}[1],E_{2})=0
as
E_{1}\textnormal{, }E_{2}\in\Coh(C).
We will now prove that
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E[1],j_{*}(C(\varphi)))=0.

Case 1:
If
\Ker(\varphi)=0,
we obtain that
C(\varphi)=\Coker(\varphi)
and by adjointness we get

\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E[1],j_{*}(C(\varphi)))=\Hom_{D^{b}(C)}(\Coker(\varphi)[1],\Coker(\varphi))=0.

Case 2:

\Ker(\varphi)\neq 0.
It is enough to show that
\phi(E)+1>\phi^{+}(j_{*}(C(\varphi))).

Let us compute
\phi^{+}(j_{*}(C(\varphi))).
By Remark
5.28
we have that
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{C}_{2}=\Coh_{2}(C),
so
j_{*}(C(\varphi))\notin\mathcal{A}.
As a consequence, we first need to consider its filtration in the t-structure induced by
\mathcal{A},
given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}(\Ker(\varphi))[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}(C(\varphi))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}(\Ker(\varphi))[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}(\Coker(\varphi)).}

By definition,
\phi^{+}(j_{*}(C(\varphi)))=\phi^{+}(j_{*}(\Ker(\varphi)))+1.

Let us consider the HN-filtration
0\subseteq H_{1}\cdots\subseteq H_{n-1}\subseteq H_{n}=j_{*}(\Ker(\varphi))

of
j_{*}(\Ker(\varphi))
in
\mathcal{A}
with respect to
\sigma.
Note that by Lemma
5.27
, if
F\in\Coh(C)
is
\mu
-semistable then
j_{*}(F)
is also
\sigma
-semistable. Therefore if we consider the HN-filtration of
\Ker(\varphi)
with respect to
\mu,
as
\Coh_{2}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\Coh_{2}(C)}}=Z_{\mu},
it will give us the HN-filtration of
j_{*}(\Ker(\varphi))
in
\mathcal{A}
with respect to
\sigma.
By the uniqueness of the HN-filtration, we deduce that
H_{i}\in\Coh_{2}(C),
for all
i=0,\dots,n.
Moreover, we have that
H_{1}\neq 0
is
\sigma
-semistable and
\phi(H_{1})=\phi^{+}(j_{*}(\Ker(\varphi)))=\phi^{+}(j_{*}(C(\varphi)))-1.

Let
H_{1}=0\rightarrow F_{1},
with
F_{1}\in\Coh(C).
By definition
F_{1}\subseteq\Ker(\varphi).

As
\Ker(\varphi)\rightarrow 0
is a subobject of
E
in
\TCoh(C)
and
\mathcal{F}
is closed under subobjects, we obtain that
F_{1}\rightarrow 0\in\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{A}.
By Lemma
5.27
, as
F_{1}
is
\mu
-semistable,
i_{*}(F_{1})
is also
\sigma
-semistable. Moreover, we have a non-zero morphism
i_{*}(F_{1})\rightarrow E.
As they are both
\sigma
-semistable this implies that
\phi_{\sigma}(i_{*}(F_{1}))\leq\phi_{\sigma}(E).

Let
d=\deg(F_{1})
and
r=\rank(F_{1}).
By the definition of
\mathcal{F},
we get that

\Ker(\varphi)
and therefore
F_{1}
is torsion-free and
r>0.
As
i_{*}(F_{1})\in\mathcal{F},
we also have that
Cr+Dd\geq 0.
Moreover, we have
\phi(H_{1})<\phi(i_{*}(F_{1})).

As a consequence, we obtain
\phi^{+}(j_{*}(C(\varphi)))-1=\phi(j_{*}(F_{1}))<\phi(E)
as we wanted to prove.

We now use Serre duality to obtain the same results for the
\sigma
-stable objects
E\in\mathcal{H}_{23}[-1],

\mathcal{H}_{31}[-1].

Corollary 5.30
.

If
E
is
\sigma
-stable, then
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,E[2])=0.

Now we compute the Euler form for all
\sigma
-stable objects.

Lemma 5.31
.

If
E
is a
\sigma
-stable object, then
-\chi(E,E)=d_{2}r_{1}-d_{1}r_{2}\geq 0.

Proof 44
.

The proof falls naturally into two cases:

Case 1:

[\varphi]=0.

It implies that either
E_{1}=0
or
E_{2}=0.
If not it would contradict that
E
is
\sigma
-stable. It clearly follows that
-\chi(E,E)=0.

Case 2:

[\varphi]\neq 0.
As
E\in\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}
is the heart of a bounded t-structure, we have that
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,E[n])=0
for all
n<0.
By Corollary
5.30
we have that
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,E[2])=0
and as
\TCoh(C)
has homological dimension
2,
we get that
\mathcal{H}_{23}[-1]
and
\mathcal{H}_{31}[-1]
also have homological dimension 2.
Therefore, it follows that
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,E[n])=0
for
n\geq 2.
As a consequence, we obtain

-\chi(E,E)=-\hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,E)+\hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,E[1]).

As
E
is
\sigma
-stable, it follows that
-\hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,E)=-1.
To prove our claim, it suffices to show that
\hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,E[1])>0.
By Serre duality
\Hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,E[1])=\Hom(E[1],\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E))^{*}
where
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E)=E_{2}[1]\xrightarrow{i_{E}[1]}C(\varphi)[1].
We have
\hom_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E))>0
because there is a non-zero morphism
E\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}(E)[-1].
Therefore,
-\chi(E,E)=d_{2}r_{1}-d_{1}r_{2}>0.

Proposition 5.32
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
be a pre-stability condition as in Lemma
3.48
with
\Delta(M)<0.
Then it satisfies the support property and therefore it is a Bridgeland stability condition.

Proof 45
.

We claim that
\sigma
satisfies the support property with respect to the following quadratic form

Q(r_{1},d_{1},r_{2},d_{2})=d_{2}r_{1}-d_{1}r_{2}.

We first show that
Q
is negative definite on
\Ker(Z)=\{(r_{1},d_{1},r_{2},d_{2})\mid d_{2}=Ad_{1}+Br_{1}\textnormal{ and }r_{2}=-Cr_{1}-Dd_{1}\}.
Let
(r_{1},d_{1},r_{2},d_{2})\in\Ker(Z),
then
Q(r_{1},d_{1},r_{2},d_{2})=Dd_{1}^{2}+(A+C)d_{1}r+Br_{1}^{2}<0
as
-1<f(0)=r<0,
we have that
D<0.
Moreover since
\Delta(M)=(A+C)^{2}-4BD<0,
then
B<0
and
Dd_{1}^{2}+(A+C)d_{1}r_{1}+Br_{1}^{2}<0
for all
(r_{1},d_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Let
E=E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi}E_{2}
be a
\sigma
-semistable object. By
[
5
, Lemma A.6]
it is enough to show that
Q(E)\geq 0
for
\sigma
-stable objects. By Claim
5.31
we have that
d_{2}r_{1}-d_{1}r_{2}\geq 0.

Remark 5.33
.

To prove the support property of the pre-stability conditions with negative discriminant in the case
g>1,
we would need to prove
Lemma
5.29
. But this would not be enough as we cannot use the Euler form
-\chi(E,E)=d_{2}r_{1}-d_{1}r_{2}-(1-g)(r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2}-r_{1}r_{2})
directly, because it is not negative definite on
\Ker(Z).

Theorem 5.34
.

Let
g=1
and
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}
be a pre-stability condition. Then it satisfies the support property and therefore is a Bridgeland stability condition.

Proof 46
.

If
\sigma\in\Theta_{i},
this follows directly from Proposition
5.23
and if
\sigma\in\Gamma,
then it follows from Proposition
5.32
.

6.
Topological description of
S_{12}

It is now our purpose to study the topology of
\Stab(\mathcal{T}_{C}),
we proceed by defining the following sets

\displaystyle S_{12}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\sigma\in\Stab(\mathcal{T}_{C})

\displaystyle\mid

\displaystyle i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}),j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})\>\>\sigma\textnormal{-stable}\textnormal{ for all closed points $x\in C$}

\displaystyle\}

,

\displaystyle S_{23}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\sigma\in\Stab(\mathcal{T}_{C})

\displaystyle\mid

\displaystyle j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}),l_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})\>\>\sigma\textnormal{-stable}\textnormal{ for all closed points $x\in C$}

\displaystyle\}

,

\displaystyle S_{31}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\sigma\in\Stab(\mathcal{T}_{C})

\displaystyle\mid

\displaystyle i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}),l_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})\>\>\sigma\textnormal{-stable}\textnormal{ for all closed points $x\in C$}

\displaystyle\}

,

Throughout the whole section, we assume that every pre-stability condition
\sigma\in\Gamma
is also a Bridgeland stability condition, i.e. it satisfies the support property.
The aim of this section is to prove that
S_{12}
is an open, connected four dimensional complex manifold. It is based on the well-behaved wall and chamber decompositions of the space of stability conditions. See
[
3
, Proposition 3.3]
or
[
12
, Proposition 9.3]
. Let us consider a connected component
\Stab^{\dagger}(\mathcal{T}_{C})
of
\Stab(\mathcal{T}_{C}).

Lemma 6.1
.

The set
S_{12}\cap\Stab^{\dagger}(\mathcal{T}_{C})
is open in
\Stab^{\dagger}(\mathcal{T}_{C}).

Proof 47
.

Let
S=\{i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}),j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})\mid x\in C\}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{C}.
First note that the classes of
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),

j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),
and
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
and
j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
in
K(\mathcal{T}_{C})
are primitive. By
[
3
, Proposition 3.3]
, we have a well-behaved wall and chamber decomposition.
We consider the set
\Theta^{\dagger}
of points
\sigma\in\Stab^{\dagger}(\mathcal{T}_{C})
at which all objects of
S
are
\sigma
-stable. We will now prove that
\Theta^{\dagger}
is open.
Let
B\subseteq\Stab^{\dagger}(\mathcal{T}_{C})
be a compact set, we show that
F=\{\sigma\in B\mid\textnormal{ not every }E\in S\textnormal{ is stable in }\sigma\}
is a closed set. As in
[
12
, Proposition 9.4]
this follows from the fact that
F=\cup^{n}_{j=0}\bar{C_{j}},
where each
C_{j}
is a chamber in which some
E\in S
is not stable.

Let
g_{1},g_{2}\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}),
with
g_{1}=(T_{1},f_{1})
and
g_{2}=(T_{2},f_{2}).
We denote by

M_{i}=T_{i}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-A_{i}&B_{i}\\
-D_{i}&C_{i}\end{bmatrix}\textnormal{ for }i=1,2.

Let us consider the subset

\mathcal{P}_{12}=\{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})^{2}\mid\phi_{0}<\phi_{2}+1,\phi_{1}<\phi_{3}+1\textnormal{ and if }\phi_{0}>\phi_{2}\textnormal{, then }\det(M_{1}+M_{2})>0\},

where
f_{1}(0)=n+\theta_{1},

f_{2}(0)=m+\theta_{2}
,
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in[0,1),

\rho(\sigma_{1})=(m_{0},m_{1},\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
and
\rho(\sigma_{2})=(m_{2},m_{3},\phi_{2},\phi_{3}),
as we explained in Remark
2.53
.

Remark 6.2
.

For
\sigma\in S_{12},
we consider

\displaystyle\phi_{0}=\phi(i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)))

and

\displaystyle\phi_{1}=\phi(i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}))

(6.1)

\displaystyle\phi_{2}=\phi(j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)))

and

\displaystyle\phi_{3}=\phi(j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})).

Lemma 6.3
.

For every
\sigma\in S_{12},
we have that
\phi_{1}<\phi_{0}<\phi_{1}+1\textnormal{ and }\phi_{3}<\phi_{2}<\phi_{3}+1.

Proof 48
.

It follows directly from the stability of
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
and
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}).

Since every
\sigma\in S_{12}
satisfies
\phi_{1}<\phi_{0}<\phi_{1}+1\textnormal{ and }\phi_{3}<\phi_{2}<\phi_{3}+1,
then by Remark
2.53
for
(m_{0},m_{1},\phi_{0},\phi_{1}),
where

m_{0}=\lvert Z(i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)))\rvert\textnormal{ and }m_{1}=\lvert Z(i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}))\rvert

and for
(m_{2},m_{3},\phi_{2},\phi_{3}),
where

m_{2}=\lvert Z(j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)))\rvert\textnormal{ and }m_{3}=\lvert Z(j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C}))\rvert.
Therefore, we obtain two stability conditions
\sigma_{1}=(Z_{1},\mathcal{A}_{1})=(T_{1},f_{1})
and
\sigma_{2}=(Z_{2},\mathcal{A}_{2})=(T_{2},f_{2})
in
\Stab(C).

We define the map

(6.2)

\displaystyle\pi\colon S_{12}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{12}

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}).

Note that
{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}=Z_{1}\textnormal{ and }{\left.\kern-1.2ptZ\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}_{2}}}=Z_{2}.

Remark 6.4
.

The group
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
acts freely on
\mathcal{P}_{12}
by the definition of the action.

Lemma 6.5
.

The map
\pi
is well-defined, continuous, open and
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-equivariant.

Proof 49
.

First note that
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
also acts freely on
S_{12}.
As
\pi
is defined in terms of the slicing, we clearly have a
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
-equivariant continuous map from
S_{12}
to
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}).

We will now show that
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{12}.
First, we prove that
m-n\geq-1.
Since
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are stable and we have a non-zero morphism
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))\rightarrow j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[1],
it follows that
\phi_{0}-\phi_{2}<1.

If
\phi_{0}>\phi_{2},
then by Remark
4.10
we get that
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is stable. We show now that in this case
\det(M_{1}+M_{2})>0.
By Proposition
4.21
and the analogous propositions for
\Theta_{23}
and
\Theta_{31},
we obtain that there is
g\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that by acting by
g
we obtain a stability condition
\sigma^{{}^{\prime}}=\sigma g
such that
\pi(\sigma^{{}^{\prime}})=(\sigma_{1}g,\sigma_{\mu}).
Let
\sigma^{{}^{\prime}}=\sigma_{1}g=(T^{{}^{\prime}},f^{{}^{\prime}})
and
M^{{}^{\prime}}={T^{{}^{\prime}}}^{-1}.
By Lemma
4.30
, we have
\det(M^{{}^{\prime}}+I)>0.
Note that
M^{{}^{\prime}}=M_{2}^{-1}M_{1},
therefore

0<\det(M_{2}^{-1}M_{1}+I)=\det(M_{2})\det(M_{1}+M_{2}).

As
\phi_{3}<\phi_{2}<\phi_{3}+1,
we obtain
\det(M_{2})>0.
This implies
\det(M_{1}+M_{2})>0.
Moreover, as
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
and
j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
are
\sigma
-stable and there is a non-zero morphism
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})\rightarrow j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})[1],
then we immediately obtain that
\phi_{1}<\phi_{3}+1.

Consequently, we obtain that
\pi
is well defined.

Since
\pi^{\prime}\colon S_{12}\rightarrow\rm{GL}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})^{2}
is a local homeomorphism, where
\pi^{\prime}
maps a stability condition to its stability function, the fact that
\pi
is a local homeomorphism follows almost directly from the fact that
p\circ\pi=\pi^{\prime},
where

p\colon\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})^{2}\rightarrow\rm{GL}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
is the universal covering.

In order to prove that the map
\pi
is in fact a homeomorphism, we study the action of
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
on
S_{12}.

As
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
acts freely on
S_{12},
we define a section of the action

\mathcal{V}_{12}=\{\sigma\in S_{12}\mid\sigma=\pi(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\textnormal{ such that }\sigma_{2}=\sigma_{\mu}\}.

Lemma 6.6
.

If
\sigma\in\mathcal{V}_{12}
and
\pi(\sigma)=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{\mu})
with
\sigma_{1}=(T,f)\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
then
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable if and only if
-1<f(0)<0.

Proof 50
.

If
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\sigma
-stable, since
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
and
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
are also
\sigma
-stable, we obtain that
\phi_{2}<\phi_{4}<\phi_{0}<\phi_{2}+1
and therefore that
n=-1
and
-1<f(0)<0.

If
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma
-stable, then by Remark
4.7
we have
\phi_{0}-\phi_{2}\leq 0,
which implies that
f(0)\geq 0
and we obtain a contradiction.

Remark 6.7
.

If
\sigma\in\mathcal{V}_{12}
and
\pi(\sigma)=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{\mu})
and
0\leq f(0),
then as
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is not
\sigma
-stable, by Theorem
4.13
we have
\sigma\in\Theta_{12}.

The image of
\mathcal{V}_{12}
under
\pi
is contained in

\mathcal{L}_{12}=\{(\sigma,\sigma_{\mu})\in\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})^{2}\mid f(0)>-1\textnormal{, }3/2>f^{-1}(1/2)\textnormal{ and if}f(0)<0\textnormal{ then }\det(M+I)>0\}.

Abusing the notation we see
\mathcal{L}_{12}
as a subset of
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}).

Lemma 6.8
.

The subset
\mathcal{L}_{12}\subseteq\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R})
is open and connected.

Proof 51
.

By definition
\mathcal{L}_{12}
is clearly an open subset of
\widetilde{\textnormal{GL}}^{+}(2,\mathbb{R}).

We define
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{4}
as follows: We say that
(m_{0},m_{1},\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\in Y
if
m_{i}>0,\textnormal{ }\phi_{0}<2\textnormal{, }
\phi_{1}<\frac{3}{2}\textnormal{, }\phi_{1}<\phi_{0}<\phi_{1}+1
and if
1\leq\phi_{0}<2\textnormal{ and }0<\phi_{1}<\frac{3}{2}\textnormal{, then }\delta(m_{0},m_{1},\phi_{0},\phi_{1})>-1,
where

\displaystyle\delta\colon\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times(1,2)\times(0,\frac{3}{2})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle(m_{0},m_{1},\phi_{0},\phi_{1})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle m_{0}m_{1}\sin((\phi_{0}-\phi_{1})\pi)-m_{0}\cos(\phi_{0}\pi)+m_{1}\sin(\phi_{1}\pi).

Note that
Y
is connected, because although
f
is defined in terms of trigonometric functions, it is restricted to intervals where it behaves well.
It is easy to see that the map

(6.3)

\displaystyle\rho\colon\mathcal{L}_{12}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle(T,f)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(m_{0},m_{1},f^{-1}(1),f^{-1}(\frac{1}{2}))

where
m_{0}=|A+Di|,m_{1}=|B+Ci|,
is a homeomorphism. It follows that
\mathcal{L}_{12}
is connected.

Proposition 6.9
.

The map

(6.4)

\displaystyle\pi\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{12}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{12}

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\mapsto\sigma_{1},

is a homeomorphism.

Proof 52
.

First, we prove that
\pi
is injective. Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
,
\tau=(W,\mathcal{B})\in\mathcal{V}_{12},
such that
\pi(\sigma)=\pi(\tau)=\sigma_{1}.

If
0\leq f(0),
by Remark
6.7
, we obtain
\sigma,\tau\in\Theta_{12}.
By Lemma
4.26
, it follows that
\sigma=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=\tau.

If
0>f(0)>-1,
by Lemma
6.6
, we have that
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is stable. In Lemma
4.30
we described this type of stability conditions and its hearts. As by definition
Z=W
and
d(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})<1,
where
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
are the slicing of
\sigma
and
\tau
respectively, then
\sigma=\tau
.

Thus, by
Lemma
6.5
we already have a homeomorphism onto the image of
\mathcal{V}_{12}.
We now prove that it is in fact onto. By
Lemma
6.1
and
Lemma
6.5
the image of
\mathcal{V}_{12}
is open.
Since
\mathcal{L}_{12}
is also connected, it is enough to prove that
\pi(\mathcal{V}_{12})
is closed. Moreover, it contains a dense subset as the image of the discrete stability conditions constructed in
Section
and
Lemma
3.48
. We prove it by contradiction. Assume that
\pi(\mathcal{V}_{12})
is not close and let us take a
\tau_{1}=(Z,\mathcal{A})
in the boundary of
\pi(\mathcal{V}_{12})
which does not belong to the image.
Note that there is
\tau^{{}^{\prime}}=(Z^{{}^{\prime}},\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime}})=\pi(\sigma^{{}^{\prime}})\in\pi(\mathcal{V}_{12}),
where
\sigma^{\prime}=(W^{\prime},\mathcal{B^{\prime}}),
sufficiently close to
\tau_{1}
such for
W=Z(r_{1},d_{1})+Z_{\mu}(r_{2},d_{2}),
we have that
Q_{W^{\prime}}
restricted to
\Ker{W}
is negative definite, where
Q_{W^{\prime}}
is the quadratic form given by the support property satisfied by
\sigma^{\prime}.

Consider the open subset of
\Hom(\mathbb{Z}^{4},\mathbb{C}),
consisting in homomorphisms whose kernel is negative definite with respect to
Q_{W^{\prime}}
and let
U
be the connected component containing
W^{\prime}.
Then as in
[
5
, Proposition A.5]
, there is a continuos function
C\colon U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
C(Y)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfies that
||v||C(Y)<|Y(v)|
for
v\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
with
Q_{W^{\prime}}(v)\geq 0,
an appropiate norm in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
and
|-|
the Euclidean norm in
\mathbb{C}.

Then there is
0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{8},
such that
|W-W^{{}^{\prime}}|_{\infty}\leq\sin(\pi\epsilon)C(W^{\prime}).

As a consequence we have that
|W-W^{{}^{\prime}}|_{\infty}<\sin(\epsilon\pi)\frac{|W^{\prime}(E)|}{||[E]||},
which implies
||W-W^{\prime}||_{\sigma^{\prime}}<\sin(\pi\epsilon).

By Bridgeland’s deformation Theorem
2.43
, there is a stability condition
\sigma=(W,\mathcal{B})
in the neighbourhood of
\sigma^{{}^{\prime}}.
It is possible to choose
\tau^{{}^{\prime}}
appropriately, such that
\sigma
is in a desired wall. Now we assume the object
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
to be
\sigma
-semistable but not stable and
j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))\in\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}(1).
By Remark
4.10
the Jordan-Hölder filtration is given by
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1]\rightarrow j_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))\rightarrow l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x)),
all with the same phase.
Therefore, we obtain
i_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))[-1]\in\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}(1),
which implies that
{\left.\kern-1.2ptW\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}=Z=Ad+Br+iCr.
It follows that
\tau_{1}
is a stability condition given by
(Z,\Coh(C)[n]),
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}.
By the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{12}
we have that
f(0)=n\geq 0.
As a consequence, the stability conditions
\tau_{1}
and
\sigma_{\mu}
satisfy the CP-gluing conditions. Moreover
\sigma_{1}=\gl_{12}(\tau_{1},\sigma_{\mu})
is a discrete stability condition, by Corollary
3.36
and Lemma
5.20
a Bridgeland stability condition in
\mathcal{V}_{12}
, satisfying

\pi(\sigma_{1})=\tau_{1}.
We obtain a contradiction. The argument goes along the same lines for the other walls. We finally obtain
\pi(\mathcal{V}_{12})=\mathcal{L}_{12}.

Corollary 6.10
.

The map

(6.5)

\displaystyle\pi\colon

\displaystyle S_{12}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{12}

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\mapsto(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})

is a homeomorphism.

We can finally prove the Harder–Narasimhan property for non-discrete stability conditions.

Proposition 6.11
.

The pairs
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
constructed in Corollary
3.36
with the semiorthogonal decomposition
\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle
and the pairs
\sigma=(Z_{r},\mathcal{A}_{r})
given in Lemma
3.47
with
f^{-1}(\frac{1}{2})<\frac{3}{2}
are Bridgeland stability conditions.

Proof 53
.

Whenever
\sigma_{1}=(T_{1},f_{1})=(Z_{1},\mathcal{A}_{1})\in\Stab(C)
and
\sigma_{2}
are discrete we have already shown that the data gives us a Bridgeland stability condition. It is enough to show this whenever
\sigma_{2}=\sigma_{\mu}.

Let
\sigma=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{\mu})
be a CP-glued pair.
As
f_{1}(0)\geq 0,
we get
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{\mu})\in\mathcal{L}_{12}.
Therefore, there is a stability condition
\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{12}
such that
\pi(\tau)=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}).
By Remark
6.7
, we obtain
\tau\in\Theta_{12}
and by Lemma
4.26
, we have that
\tau=\gl_{12}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=(Z,\mathcal{A}).
As a consequence, the pair
(Z,\mathcal{A})
gives a Bridgeland stability condition.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{r},\mathcal{A}_{r}).
We consider
\sigma_{1}
as in Lemma
3.47
with
f^{-1}(\frac{1}{2})<\frac{3}{2}.

We get that
-1<f_{1}(0)<0
and by hypothesis
\det(M_{1}+I)>0,
we have that
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{\mu})\in\mathcal{L}_{12}.
As a consequence, there is a stability condition
\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{12}
such that
\pi(\tau)=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}).
By Lemma
6.6
, we obtain that
l_{*}(\mathbb{C}(x))
is
\tau
-stable. Therefore by Lemma
4.30
, we have that
\tau
is given precisely by the construction in Proposition
3.47
. Then
\tau=(Z_{r},\mathcal{A}_{r})
and as a consequence, it is a Bridgeland stability condition.

Remark 6.12
.

If
\sigma=(Z_{r},\mathcal{A}_{r})
is a pre-stability condition given in Lemma
3.47
with
f^{-1}(\frac{1}{2})\geq\frac{3}{2},
then either
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
is not stable or
j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
is not stable. Then
\sigma
is in
S_{23}
or in
S_{31}.
Precisely, by Lemma
4.16
if
\phi_{5}>\frac{3}{2}
then
j_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
and
l_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
are
\sigma
-stable, and if
\phi_{5}<1/2
then
l_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
and
i_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{C})
are
\sigma
-stable.
As a consequence, all the already constructed pairs in Lemma
3.47
are Bridgeland stability conditions.

Theorem 6.13
.

The space of stability conditions
\Stab(\mathcal{T}_{C})=S_{12}\cup S_{23}\cup S_{31}
is a connected, four dimensional complex manifold.

Proof 54
.

Since
\mathcal{V}_{12}
is connected, this implies that
S_{12}
is also connected.

Moreover,
S_{12}\cap S_{23}=S_{23}\cap S_{31}=S_{12}\cap S_{31}
is not empty. Therefore
\Stab(\mathcal{T}_{C})
is connected.

Appendix A
Recollement

In this appendix, we would like to stress the importance of the gluing condition (
3.5
) of the hearts in the definition of the CP-glued stability conditions. In
[
35
, Proposition 2.8.12]
, we showed that CP-gluing of hearts correspond to hearts constructed by classical recollement in the sense of
[
6
]
where the gluing condition (
3.5
) is satisfied. We recall the definitions in
[
6
]
below.

Definition A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{D}
be triangulated categories.
\mathcal{D}
is said to be a
recollement
of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
if there are six triangulated functors as in the diagram

\mathcal{X}
\mathcal{D}
\mathcal{Y}
j_{*}=j_{!}
j^{*}
j^{!}
i^{*}=i^{!}
i_{!}
i_{*}

such that

(1)

(i^{*},i_{*})
,
(i_{!},i^{!})
,
(j^{*},j_{*})
,
(j_{!},j^{!})
are adjoint pairs;

(2)

j_{*}
,
i_{*}
,
i_{!}
are full embeddings;

(3)

j^{!}\circ i_{*}=0
(and thus also
i^{*}\circ j_{*}=0
and
j^{*}\circ i_{!}=0
);

(4)

for every
T\in\mathcal{D}
there are triangles

\textstyle{i_{!}i^{!}T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{*}j^{*}T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{!}i^{!}T[1]}
\textstyle{j_{!}j^{!}T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{*}i^{*}T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{!}j^{!}T[1].}

Note that the functors of the definition of recollement satisfy the following properties as a consequence of the vanishing condition (3.).

•

\prescript{\perp}{}{\mathcal{X}}=\ker(j^{*})
and
j^{*}\circ i_{!}=0
implies that
i_{!}
embeds the category
\mathcal{Y}
as
\prescript{\perp}{}{\mathcal{X}}
.

•

\mathcal{X}^{\perp}=\ker(j^{!})
and
j^{!}\circ i_{*}=0
implies that
i_{*}
embeds the category
\mathcal{Y}
as
\mathcal{X}^{\perp}
.

Hence, if
i_{*}
denotes the natural embedding of
\mathcal{X}
into
\mathcal{D}
, we have
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}^{\perp}
. In fact, the definition of recollement gives the two semiorthogonal decompositions
\mathcal{D}=\langle\mathcal{X},\prescript{\perp}{}{\mathcal{X}}\rangle
and
\mathcal{D}=\langle\mathcal{X}^{\perp},\mathcal{X}\rangle
associated to an admissible full subcategory
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{D}
.

Proposition A.2

(
[
35
, Proposition 2.8.2]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a triangulated category and let
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{D}
be a full triangulated subcategory. Then,
\mathcal{D}
is a recollement of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\perp}
if and only if
\mathcal{X}
is (left and right) admissible.

The following theorem shows how to construct t-structures from t-structures in the smaller subcategories.

Theorem A.3

(
[
6
, Theorem 1.4.10]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{D}
be triangulated categories such that
\mathcal{D}
is a recollement of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
and assume the notation of Definition
A.1
. Let
(\mathcal{X}^{\leq 0},\mathcal{X}^{\geq 0})
and
(\mathcal{Y}^{\leq 0},\mathcal{Y}^{\geq 0})
be t-structures on
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
respectively. Then there is a t-structure
(\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0},\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0})
in
\mathcal{D}
defined by:

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}\coloneqq\{T\in\mathcal{D}\mid i^{*}T\in\mathcal{Y}^{\leq 0},j^{*}T\in\mathcal{X}^{\leq 0}\}\\
\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}\coloneqq\{T\in\mathcal{D}\mid i^{*}T\in\mathcal{Y}^{\geq 0},j^{!}T\in\mathcal{X}^{\geq 0}\}.\end{array}

If we write
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{Y}}
for the corresponding hearts in
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
respectively, we denote by
\rec(\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{Y}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{X}})\coloneqq\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}\cap\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}
.

Remark A.4
.

From Remark
3.3
, we know that all three subcategories
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
are admissible in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
. Therefore, by Proposition
A.2
they define 3 recollements of
\mathcal{T}_{C}
. Given hearts
\mathcal{A}_{i}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{j}
) in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}_{j}
), we will denote by
\rec_{ij}(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{A}_{j})
the heart in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
as recollement of
\mathcal{C}_{j}
and
\mathcal{C}_{i}
constructed as in Theorem
A.2
for
ij\in\{12,23,31\}
.

In general, when a heart is constructed by CP-gluing we know it agrees with a heart constructed from recollement. See
[
35
, Proposition 2.8.12]
. On the other hand, at least in the context of holomorphic triples, a heart constructed by recollement without satisfying CP-gluing condition does not accept a stability function as shown in the example below.

Lemma A.5

(Jealousy Lemma)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{T}_{C}
be a heart constructed by recollement of hearts
\mathcal{A}_{i}\subset\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
\mathcal{A}_{j}\subset\mathcal{C}_{j}
which do not satisfy CP-gluing conditions. Then,
\mathcal{A}
does not accept a stability function defined on
K(\mathcal{A})
, i.e.
Z(\mathcal{A})\not\subset\overline{\mathbb{H}}
for every
Z\colon K(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.

Proof.

We give the proof for the case of
\mathcal{T}_{C}
as recollement of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and the other cases will follow by acting with the Serre functor
S_{\mathcal{T}_{C}}
(or its inverse) on
\sigma
.

Let
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A}_{12})
be a stability condition on
\mathcal{T}_{C}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{12}\coloneqq\rec_{12}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
is a heart in
\mathcal{T}_{C}
defined by recollement from given hearts
\mathcal{A}_{i}\coloneqq\coh^{r_{i}}(C)
in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, for
i=1,2
that do not satisfy the CP-gluing condition (
3.5
), i.e. such that

(A.1)

r_{2}-1<r_{1}<r_{2}.

First of all, we claim that the hearts
i_{*}\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
j_{*}\mathcal{A}_{2}
and
l_{*}\mathcal{A}_{1}
are in
\mathcal{A}_{12}
. Indeed, it follows from the definitions. Recall from the isomorphism of Theorem
2.49
that we can identify
\mathcal{A}_{i}=\mathcal{P}(r_{i},r_{i}+1]
for
i=1,2
, where
\mathcal{P}(0,1]=\Coh(C)
. Therefore,
j_{*}\mathcal{P}(r_{2},r_{2}+1]\subset\mathcal{A}_{12}
since
i^{*}j_{*}=0
and by adjunction
j^{*}j_{*}=\id
and
j^{!}j_{*}=\id
. Also
i_{*}\mathcal{P}(r_{1},r_{1}+1]\subset\mathcal{A}_{12}
, since
j^{!}i_{*}=0
,
i^{*}i_{*}=\id
and using
j^{*}i_{*}=j^{*}j_{!}[1]
and by (
A.1
),
\mathcal{P}(r_{1}+1,r_{1}+2]\subset\mathcal{P}(r_{2},\infty)
. Similarly,
l_{*}\mathcal{P}(r_{1},r_{1}+1]\subset\mathcal{A}_{12}
, since
l_{*}=i_{!}
,
j^{*}i_{!}=0
,
i^{*}i_{!}=\id
and using
j^{!}i_{!}=l^{*}l_{*}
and by (
A.1
),
\mathcal{P}(r_{1},r_{1}+1]\subset\mathcal{P}(-\infty,r_{2}+1]
.

As in Remark
2.52
, we write each
r_{i}=n_{i}+\theta_{i}
for unique
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\theta_{i}\in[0,1)
. We assume
n_{2}
to be (up to shift) equal to
0
. Note that equation (
A.1
) implies that either
n_{1}=0
and
\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}<\theta_{1}+1
or
n_{1}=-1
and
\theta_{2}<\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}+1
.

If we look closely at the imaginary part of
Z
, it has the form
\Im Z(r_{1},d_{1},r_{2},d_{2})=D_{1}d_{1}+D_{2}d_{2}+C_{1}r_{1}+C_{2}r_{2}
with
C_{i},D_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, for
i=1,2
. The restrictions to the previous hearts are

\begin{array}[]{l}\Im Z\mid_{i_{*}\mathcal{A}_{1}}=D_{1}d+C_{1}r\\
\Im Z\mid_{j_{*}\mathcal{A}_{2}}=D_{2}d+C_{2}r\\
\Im Z\mid_{l_{*}\mathcal{A}_{1}}=(D_{1}+D_{2})d+(C_{1}+C_{2})r\end{array}

for
d,r\in\mathbb{Z}
with
r\geq 0
. We recall that if
Cr+Dd
is the imaginary part of a stability function on
\Coh^{\theta}
, then the value
\theta
is determined by the quotient
D/C
which implies that
\theta_{1}
is determined by the quotients
D_{1}/C_{1}
and
(D_{2}+D_{1})/(C_{1}+C_{2})
. But these two quotients cannot determine the same
\theta_{1}
unless
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}
, which contradicts the assumption (
A.1
).
∎

Remark A.6
.

The contradiction was a consequence of including 3 smaller hearts in the big one. The only case when this situation does not create a contradiction is for the extreme cases of CP-gluing conditions in Proposition
3.21
.
